# Exegetical Analysis of Wealth, Poverty, and Generosity in Proverbs

> **Topical study** — How Proverbs speaks of riches and poverty (its vocabulary of the rich *ʿāšîr*, wealth *hôn* and *ʿōšer*, the poor *rāš* and *dal*, the needy *ʾebyôn* and the afflicted *ʿānî*), of generosity and withholding, of justice to the poor and honest weights, of surety, debt and interest, and of the "better than" sayings that relativize wealth, weighing the book's retribution principle against its own qualifications.
>
> Primary passages: Proverbs 3:9-10, 27-28; Proverbs 6:1-5; Proverbs 8:18-21; Proverbs 10:2, 15, 22; Proverbs 11:1, 4, 24-26, 28; Proverbs 13:7-8, 11, 23; Proverbs 14:20-21, 31; Proverbs 15:16-17; Proverbs 16:8, 11, 19; Proverbs 17:1, 5; Proverbs 18:11, 23; Proverbs 19:1, 4, 7, 17; Proverbs 21:13, 17; Proverbs 22:1-2, 7, 9, 16, 22-23, 26-27; Proverbs 23:4-5, 10-11; Proverbs 28:6, 8, 11, 20, 22, 27; Proverbs 30:7-9; Deuteronomy 15:7-11; Psalm 49; Luke 12:13-21; 1 Timothy 6:6-10, 17-19

---

> **Proverbs 3:9-10** "Honor the LORD with your wealth and with the firstfruits of all your crops; then your barns will be filled with plenty, and your vats will overflow with new wine."
>
> **Proverbs 3:27-28** "Do not withhold good from the deserving when it is within your power to act. Do not tell your neighbor, “Come back tomorrow and I will provide”—when you already have the means."
>
> **Proverbs 10:15** "The wealth of the rich man is his fortified city, but poverty is the ruin of the poor."
>
> **Proverbs 10:22** "The blessing of the LORD enriches, and He adds no sorrow to it."
>
> **Proverbs 11:1** "Dishonest scales are an abomination to the LORD, but an accurate weight is His delight."
>
> **Proverbs 11:24-25** "One gives freely, yet gains even more; another withholds what is right, only to become poor. A generous soul will prosper, and he who refreshes others will himself be refreshed."
>
> **Proverbs 13:23** "Abundant food is in the fallow ground of the poor, but without justice it is swept away."
>
> **Proverbs 14:31** "Whoever oppresses the poor taunts their Maker, but whoever is kind to the needy honors Him."
>
> **Proverbs 16:8** "Better a little with righteousness than great gain with injustice."
>
> **Proverbs 18:11** "A rich man’s wealth is his fortified city; it is like a high wall in his imagination."
>
> **Proverbs 19:17** "Kindness to the poor is a loan to the LORD, and He will repay the lender."
>
> **Proverbs 22:2** "The rich and the poor have this in common: The LORD is Maker of them all."
>
> **Proverbs 22:7** "The rich rule over the poor, and the borrower is slave to the lender."
>
> **Proverbs 22:26-27** "Do not be one who gives pledges, who puts up security for debts. If you have nothing with which to pay, why should your bed be taken from under you?"
>
> **Proverbs 23:4-5** "Do not wear yourself out to get rich; be wise enough to restrain yourself. When you glance at wealth, it disappears, for it makes wings for itself and flies like an eagle to the sky."
>
> **Proverbs 30:8-9** "Keep falsehood and deceitful words far from me. Give me neither poverty nor riches; feed me with the bread that is my portion. Otherwise, I may have too much and deny You, saying, ‘Who is the LORD?’ Or I may become poor and steal, profaning the name of my God."
>
> — *Berean Standard Bible (BSB)*

---

## Overview

### 1. Big Idea

**Proverbs treats wealth as a real but lesser good that the LORD gives, tests and judges, so that how a person gains it, trusts it and shares it with the poor reveals whether that person fears the LORD** (see Theological §3). The book states a confident pattern: generosity and righteousness tend toward plenty, stinginess and haste toward want. In the same collections it qualifies that pattern, naming poverty caused by injustice and ranking integrity above income (see Literary §1-2). Its retribution sayings are true generalizations, not guarantees, which rules out both a prosperity reading and a cynical one (see Application §4).

### 2. In Context

The topic runs through every collection (see Historical §1). The prologue makes the son's first temptation economic (Proverbs 1:13-14), then brings wealth in as worship, justice and Wisdom's lesser gift (Proverbs 3:9-10, 27-28; 8:18-21) (see Hermeneutic §5). Nearly three-quarters of the material sits in the two Solomonic collections, and most "better than" sayings fall in Proverbs 15-21, not in Proverbs 25-27 as Distinctions.md suggests (see Literary §1-2). The sayings of the wise make the LORD the poor man's advocate (Proverbs 22:22-23; 23:10-11), and the book closes with Agur's prayer and the valiant woman's open palm (Proverbs 30:7-9; 31:20) (see Literary §2). Against the Distinctions.md character chart, wealth and poverty are conditions that test character, not stages on its path (see Hermeneutic §4). Laziness belongs to "The Sluggard", royal justice to "The King", and the rich man's friends to "The Friend and Neighbor" (see Literary §8; Historical §7). Canonically, the theme runs from Torah's open hand to the Rich One made poor (2 Corinthians 8:9), traced in Hermeneutic §1.

### 3. Key Verse and Movements

> **Proverbs 16:8** "Better a little with righteousness than great gain with injustice."

**Why this verse:** Literary §1 makes the "better than" form the grammar by which the book qualifies its retribution principle, and Theological §3 builds on it.

1. **The LORD gives, and conduct tends toward consequence** (Proverbs 3:9-10; 10:22; 11:24-25) — Wealth is the LORD's gift, honored with the first portion, and generosity tends toward plenty (see Theological §1, §3).
2. **The pattern qualified from within** (Proverbs 10:15; 13:23; 15:16-17; 18:10-11) — Paired and "better than" sayings expose wealth's imagined security and admit poverty caused by injustice (see Literary §2; Linguistic §6).
3. **The poor share a Maker and an Advocate** (Proverbs 11:1; 14:31; 19:17; 22:2) — Oppressing the poor taunts their Maker, and kindness to them is a loan to the LORD (see Theological §3-4).
4. **Wealth as a test of trust** (Proverbs 11:28; 22:26-27; 30:7-9) — Trusting riches and rash pledges are dangers, and Agur prays for "neither poverty nor riches" (see Theological §2-3).

---

## Historical & Cultural Analysis

### 1. Author

**Several voices, one editorial frame.** Proverbs has no single author for its teaching on money. The book's own headings name at least five sources. There are the "proverbs of Solomon" (Proverbs 1:1; 10:1), the "words of the wise" (Proverbs 22:17) and "sayings of the wise" (Proverbs 24:23), the "additional proverbs of Solomon, which were copied by the men of Hezekiah king of Judah" (Proverbs 25:1), the words of Agur son of Jakeh (Proverbs 30:1), and the words of King Lemuel, "the burden that his mother taught him" (Proverbs 31:1). The poem of the valiant woman (Proverbs 31:10-31) has no heading of its own. Sayings about wealth, poverty and generosity turn up in every one of these collections, but they are not spread evenly:

| Collection | Attributed voice | Share of the wealth material | Characteristic emphasis |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| Proverbs 1:1-9:18 | Solomon (book title); a father and mother teaching a son | Moderate | Wealth as Wisdom's gift (Proverbs 3:16; 8:18-21); firstfruits (Proverbs 3:9-10); prompt generosity (Proverbs 3:27-28); the surety warning (Proverbs 6:1-5); the gang's plunder (Proverbs 1:13-19) |
| Proverbs 10:1-22:16 | Solomon | By far the largest (well over half the relevant verses) | Paired sayings on rich and poor; honest weights; the Maker of the poor (Proverbs 14:31; 17:5; 22:2); most of the "better than" sayings |
| Proverbs 22:17-24:22 | "The wise" (anonymous sages) | Concentrated | Robbing the poor at the gate (Proverbs 22:22-23); surety (Proverbs 22:26-27); boundaries (Proverbs 22:28; 23:10-11); riches that fly away (Proverbs 23:4-5); the closest parallels to Egyptian *Amenemope* |
| Proverbs 25:1-29:27 | Solomon, copied by Hezekiah's officials | Significant | Integrity in poverty (Proverbs 28:6); interest (Proverbs 28:8); the rich man's self-image (Proverbs 28:11); haste to be rich (Proverbs 28:20, 22); giving to the poor (Proverbs 28:27) |
| Proverbs 30 | Agur son of Jakeh | Small but climactic | The prayer for "neither poverty nor riches" (Proverbs 30:7-9); the generation that devours the poor (Proverbs 30:14) |
| Proverbs 31 | Lemuel's mother; the anonymous acrostic | Small | The king's duty to the poor (Proverbs 31:8-9, which belongs to "The King"); the valiant woman's trade and her open hand (Proverbs 31:16-24) |

**Solomon.** The tradition that ties the core of Proverbs to Solomon (reigned c. 970-931 BC) comes from the book's headings and from 1 Kings 4:29-34, which credits him with 3,000 proverbs. For this topic Solomon is more than a name on a title page. The narrative of 1 Kings presents him as the man who asked for wisdom rather than riches and was given riches as well (1 Kings 3:11-13). That pattern is the narrative form of Proverbs 3:16 and 8:18. The same narrative records his fabulous revenues (1 Kings 10:14-29), and it ends with the people's complaint, "Your father put a heavy yoke on us" (1 Kings 12:4) under Deuteronomy 17:17's warning against a king who multiplies silver and gold. *Observation:* Scripture holds both pictures of Solomon at once, wisdom rewarded with wealth and wealth gone wrong. *Inference:* a book carrying his name could hardly treat riches naively, and it doesn't. (The royal side of this belongs to the sibling study "The King.")

**The sages and the scribes of Hezekiah.** Proverbs 25:1 shows that the material was collected and edited at court. The verb there, **copied** (Hebrew: הֶעְתִּיקוּ, *heʿtîqû*, H6275), means to move or transcribe. The "wise" of Proverbs 22:17 and 24:23 were a recognized group in Judah. Jeremiah 18:18 lists them beside priest and prophet, and they were plausibly connected with scribal training and royal administration. *Inference:* those who shaped the wealth sayings were literate, and most likely belonged to the landowning or administrative classes. That helps explain why the book usually speaks *about* the poor more than *to* them (see "Original Audience").

**Agur and Lemuel.** Both are otherwise unknown. Each heading uses the word **oracle / burden** (Hebrew: מַשָּׂא, *maśśāʾ*, H4853). Many interpreters read it here as a place name, Massa, an Ishmaelite tribe of northern Arabia (Genesis 25:14; 1 Chronicles 1:30). The BSB translates it "the burden." If the place-name reading is right, the book's most personal words on wealth (Proverbs 30:7-9) and its sharpest charge to defend the poor (Proverbs 31:8-9) come from outside Israel's own borders. That is a debated inference, not an established fact. Lemuel's teaching is also the one place where a woman, the queen mother, is the named voice of instruction.

**Distribution worth noticing.** Two observations about the vocabulary rest on the interlinear tagging gathered for this study; the word counts are set out in the Literary Analysis (§7). First, the ordinary words for "rich man," **ʿāšîr** (Hebrew: עָשִׁיר, *ʿāšîr*, H6223), and "poor man," **rāš** (Hebrew: רָשׁ, *rāš*, H7326), occur only in the sentence collections of Proverbs 10:1-29:27. Second, most of the "better than" sayings sit in Proverbs 15-21. They do not sit in Proverbs 25-27, as an aside in the working notes (Distinctions.md) suggests; what clusters in Proverbs 25-26 is the simile. The wealth-related "better than" sayings are Proverbs 12:9; 15:16, 17; 16:8, 19; 17:1; 19:1, 22; 22:1; and 28:6. *Inference:* the short two-line sentence form was the main medium for observing rich and poor side by side, while the longer instructions (Proverbs 1-9; 22:17-24:22) prefer direct commands: give, don't rob, don't pledge.

### 2. Original Audience

**An addressee with something to lose.** The implied audience of the wealth teaching is not the destitute. The son in Proverbs is assumed to have the means to act. He has "the means" to help a neighbor today (Proverbs 3:27-28). He has firstfruits to offer (Proverbs 3:9). He might be asked to stand surety, and he has a bed that could be seized (Proverbs 6:1-5; 22:26-27). He is in a position to lend at interest (Proverbs 28:8), to rob the poor "because he is poor" (Proverbs 22:22), to move a boundary stone (Proverbs 23:10), or to hide his eyes from the needy (Proverbs 28:27). The household around him has flocks, herds and maidservants (Proverbs 27:23-27; 31:15), and its head sits among the elders at the gate (Proverbs 31:23). *Observation:* nearly all the generosity and justice commands assume the hearer holds power over someone poorer. *Inference:* the book's first audience was the sons of propertied households, whether village landowners or the urban and court elite, who were being trained to use their advantages rightly.

**Scholarly debate about social location.** R. N. Whybray (*Wealth and Poverty in the Book of Proverbs*, 1990) argued that the collections reflect different settings. On his reading, Proverbs 10:1-22:16 and 25-29 mostly reflect small farmers of modest means, for whom poverty was a real risk, while Proverbs 1-9 and 22:17-24:22 reflect wealthier, more urban circles. J. David Pleins ("Poverty in the Social World of the Wise," *JSOT* 37, 1987) emphasized the wise as an elite group whose sayings tend to explain poverty as a result of personal failing. Harold C. Washington (1994) compared the Hebrew sayings with Egyptian instruction literature. Timothy J. Sandoval (2006) read the wealth language partly as rhetoric that uses riches as a figure for the value of wisdom itself (compare Proverbs 3:14-15; 8:10-11, 19; 16:16). These are interpretive proposals, not settled results. Still, they warn against assuming that all of Proverbs speaks from one social position.

**The poor as subject, not only object.** The book does give the poor a voice. The poor man's speech is quoted in posture if not in words: "The poor man pleads for mercy, but the rich man answers harshly" (Proverbs 18:23). The poor man pursues his friends with pleading in vain (Proverbs 19:7). The discerning poor man sees through the rich (Proverbs 28:11). Agur's prayer looks at poverty from the inside, as a temptation to theft (Proverbs 30:9). *Inference:* the audience is taught to see the poor as persons who share the same Maker (Proverbs 22:2; 29:13), not merely as a category for charity.

**Later audiences.** Once gathered into a book, the sayings addressed the whole covenant community. If the final editing happened in the Persian period (see section 3), the sayings on debt, surety and interest would have spoken directly into the debt crisis Nehemiah describes. People were mortgaging fields, vineyards and homes for grain, borrowing to pay "the king's tax," and seeing their children sold into slavery (Nehemiah 5:1-5). That setting is a reasonable inference about reception, not a claim about original composition.

### 3. Date of Composition and Events

**Composition.** The book itself points to a process stretching over centuries. It runs from Solomonic material (10th century BC), through Hezekiah's editorial activity (late 8th or early 7th century BC), to anonymous and non-Israelite collections whose dates cannot be fixed. Many scholars place the final arrangement, including the prologue of Proverbs 1-9 in its present form, in the Persian period (5th-4th century BC). Others argue for an earlier, monarchic completion. Neither view can be proven from the wealth sayings themselves, because they rarely contain datable details.

**Events.** There are no narrated events. The "events" behind the sayings are recurring transactions and situations of ancient economic life: weighing silver at a sale (Proverbs 11:1; 20:10, 23), haggling (Proverbs 20:14), striking hands to guarantee a loan (Proverbs 6:1; 17:18), seizing a pledged garment (Proverbs 20:16; 27:13), hoarding grain in a shortage (Proverbs 11:26), a lawsuit at the gate (Proverbs 22:22), a boundary stone shifted at night (Proverbs 22:28; 23:10), a harvest washed out by a storm (Proverbs 28:3), and an estate passed down to grandchildren (Proverbs 13:22). Those situations are attested across the whole monarchic and post-exilic periods, which is why the sayings travel so well.

| Event | Date | Significance |
|:--|:--|:--|
| Laws of Eshnunna and of Hammurabi | c. 1800-1750 BC | Mesopotamian law sets interest ceilings and limits on debt-servitude; Hammurabi's epilogue presents the king as protector of the weak, the orphan and the widow (background to Proverbs 22:7; 28:8; 31:8-9) |
| Old Babylonian royal debt-release edicts (*mīšarum*), e.g. Ammisaduqa | c. 17th century BC | Kings periodically cancelled certain debts: an ANE analogue to the sabbatical release of Deuteronomy 15:1-11 |
| Egyptian *Book of the Dead*, spell 125 (the "negative confession") | New Kingdom onward (c. 1550 BC and later) | The deceased denies tampering with grain measures and scales: a close conceptual parallel to Proverbs 11:1; 16:11; 20:10, 23 |
| *Instruction of Amenemope* composed | Usually dated to the Ramesside period, c. 1300-1075 BC (estimates vary; the British Museum papyrus itself is later) | The Egyptian text most closely paralleled in Proverbs 22:17-24:22 on robbing the poor, boundaries, and riches that fly away |
| Babylonian *kudurru* boundary stones | c. 14th-7th centuries BC | Land-grant monuments carved with divine symbols and curses on anyone who moves them (Proverbs 22:28; 23:10) |
| Solomon's reign | c. 970-931 BC | Traditional source of the core collections; a reign of great wealth and heavy taxation (1 Kings 10:14-29; 12:4) |
| Samaria ostraca | early 8th century BC | Records of wine and oil delivered to individuals in the northern kingdom: the luxury goods of Proverbs 21:17 |
| Amos, Hosea, Isaiah, Micah | mid-to-late 8th century BC | Prophetic protest against land-grabbing, rigged scales and crushing the poor (Isaiah 5:8; Hosea 12:7; Amos 8:4-6; Micah 2:1-2; 6:10-11) |
| Hezekiah's reign; *lmlk* ("belonging to the king") storage jars | c. 715-686 BC | Hezekiah's officials edit Proverbs 25-29 (Proverbs 25:1); royal storage and provisioning |
| Inscribed Judean limestone weights in wide use | late 8th to early 6th century BC | Standardized shekel-based weights, with measurable variation between specimens (Proverbs 11:1; 16:11) |
| Mesad Hashavyahu (Yavneh-Yam) ostracon | c. 630-609 BC | A harvest worker petitions an official to return his confiscated garment (compare Exodus 22:26-27; Proverbs 20:16) |
| Fall of Jerusalem | 586 BC | Loss of land, patrimony and royal institutions; questions about retribution intensify (Lamentations; Job) |
| Elephantine loan contracts | 5th century BC | Jewish loan documents from Egypt with interest of about 5 percent a month and pledge clauses (Proverbs 22:7; 28:8) |
| Nehemiah's reform of debt and usury | mid-5th century BC | Mortgaged fields, the king's tax, children enslaved for debt (Nehemiah 5:1-13) |
| Likely final arrangement of Proverbs | c. 5th-4th century BC (debated) | The collections are brought together under the prologue's frame of the fear of the LORD (Proverbs 1:7) |
| Ben Sira | c. 190-180 BC | Sirach 29 develops lending, surety and almsgiving at length: early Jewish reception of the Proverbs theme |

### 4. Notable Practices of the Day

**Weighing silver.** Before coinage reached Judah in the Persian period, silver was weighed, not counted. Buyers and sellers carried stone weights in a **bag** (Hebrew: כִּיס, *kîs*, H3599). The same word appears for the gang's shared purse in Proverbs 1:14. A transaction meant setting silver against weight-stones on a hand-held **balance** (Hebrew: מֹאזְנַיִם, *mōʾzənayim*, H3976; also פֶּלֶס, *peles*, H6425). Jeremiah 32:9-10 shows the procedure at a land purchase. Fraud was simple: keep one set of stones for buying and another for selling. Deuteronomy 25:13 forbids exactly this: "You shall not have two differing weights in your bag, one heavy and one light." Proverbs 20:10 gives the same idea in the Hebrew idiom "a stone and a stone, an ephah and an ephah" (BSB footnote). This is why Proverbs calls dishonest scales an "abomination to the LORD" (Proverbs 11:1; 20:10, 23) and says that "all the weights in the bag are His concern" (Proverbs 16:11). The marketplace was not morally neutral ground. The buyer's ritual complaint, "Worthless, worthless!", followed by his boasting afterward (Proverbs 20:14), shows that the sages watched the market closely.

**Standing surety by striking hands.** A guarantee was sealed by a public gesture, **striking the palm** (Hebrew: תָּקַע כַּף, *tāqaʿ kap*, H8628, H3709), on behalf of a neighbor or an outsider (Proverbs 6:1; 11:15; 17:18; 22:26). Job uses the same legal language when he pleads for God to stand as his guarantor (Job 17:3). The guarantor took on the debtor's liability. If the debtor defaulted, the guarantor's goods were seized, down to the bed under him (Proverbs 22:27). Proverbs 20:16 (repeated at Proverbs 27:13) tells a creditor to "Take the garment of the one who posts security for a stranger." This is a hard-headed note: a stranger's guarantor is a poor risk, so get collateral. Torah limited the seizure of a *poor* man's cloak (Exodus 22:26-27; Deuteronomy 24:12-13), and the Mesad Hashavyahu ostracon shows a worker appealing exactly such a seizure. *Inference:* the surety warnings protect the household estate from rash commitments, especially commitments to an **outsider** (Hebrew: זָר, *zār*, H2114) or a **foreigner** (Hebrew: נָכְרִי, *nokrî*, H5237), whose obligations were hard to enforce. Whether they forbid every guarantee is taken up in the Theological Analysis and the Application. Genesis 43:9 shows Judah standing surety for Benjamin at great cost to himself.

**Lending and interest.** Torah forbade charging **interest** (Hebrew: נֶשֶׁךְ, *nešek*, H5392) or **increase** (Hebrew: תַּרְבִּית, *tarbît*, H8636) to a poor fellow Israelite (Exodus 22:25; Leviticus 25:35-37; Deuteronomy 23:19-20). The pair *nešek* and *tarbît* in Proverbs 28:8 is the same pairing as in Leviticus 25:36. In the surrounding cultures, interest was normal and steep. Mesopotamian law codes typically capped it around 20 percent a year on silver and 33⅓ percent on grain, and fifth-century Elephantine contracts charge about 5 percent a month. Debt could end in servitude for the debtor's children (2 Kings 4:1; Nehemiah 5:5). This is the reality summed up in "the borrower is slave to the lender" (Proverbs 22:7), a description of how things worked, not an endorsement.

**Firstfruits and tithes.** Honoring the LORD "with the firstfruits of all your crops" (Proverbs 3:9) draws on the practice of bringing the first portion of the harvest to the sanctuary (Exodus 23:19; Deuteronomy 26:1-11; Nehemiah 10:35-37). The promised result is **barns** (Hebrew: אֲסָמִים, *ʾăsāmîm*, H618) and wine **vats** (Hebrew: יְקָבִים, *yəqābîm*, H3342) overflowing with **new wine** (Hebrew: תִּירוֹשׁ, *tîrôš*, H8492). That is the language of an agrarian household's storerooms and rock-cut winepresses. The Torah tied worship to provision for the poor: the third-year tithe went to feed the Levite, the resident foreigner, the fatherless and the widow (Deuteronomy 14:28-29). *Inference:* in its ancient setting, Proverbs 3:9-10 describes a farmer putting God first in the order of the harvest. It does not describe a financial investment strategy.

**Grain sales in shortage.** "The people will curse the hoarder of grain, but blessing will crown the one who sells it" (Proverbs 11:26). In a rain-fed economy, bad years were common. Those with stored **grain** (Hebrew: בַּר, *bar*, H1250) could hold it back to drive prices up. The participle for "the one who sells," **mašbîr** (H7666), is the verb used for Joseph selling grain in Egypt's famine (Genesis 41:56-57). Amos 8:4-6 attacks merchants who shrink the ephah while selling grain, and Nehemiah 5:2-3 shows families mortgaging land to buy it.

**The gate as court.** "Do not rob a poor man because he is poor, and do not crush the afflicted at the gate" (Proverbs 22:22). The town **gate** (Hebrew: שַׁעַר, *šaʿar*, H8179) was where elders heard cases (Deuteronomy 21:19; Ruth 4:1-11; Amos 5:12, 15). A poor man's land claim could be "swept away" there "without justice" (Proverbs 13:23). The gate was also where bribes changed hands. A **bribe** (Hebrew: שֹׁחַד, *šōḥad*, H7810) slipped from the fold of the garment (Proverbs 17:23; 21:14, BSB footnotes "a bribe from the bosom" and "a bribe in the bosom") is the ancient image of a payoff passed under the table. Torah says bribes blind the judge (Exodus 23:8; Deuteronomy 16:19).

**Gifts and patronage.** A **gift** (Hebrew: מַתָּן, *mattān*, H4976) "opens doors" and brings a man "before great men" (Proverbs 18:16), and "everyone is a friend of the gift giver" (Proverbs 19:6). Ancient society ran on patron-client relationships, and gifts to superiors were expected (1 Samuel 10:27; 1 Kings 10:25). The sages describe this without approving every use of it. Proverbs 22:16 warns that "giving gifts to the rich" ends in poverty.

**Meals, meat and feasting.** The "better than" sayings use the realities of the ancient table. The ordinary diet was bread, oil, wine, legumes and greens. Meat was for festivals, sacrificial meals and the wealthy. So "a dish of vegetables where there is love" stands against "a fattened ox with hatred" (Proverbs 15:17). The Hebrew for that animal is **šôr ʾābûs** (Hebrew: שׁוֹר אָבוּס, *šôr ʾābûs*, H7794, H75), a stall-fed animal. "A dry morsel in quietness" stands against "a house full of feasting with strife" (Proverbs 17:1). There the word for feasting is **zibḥê** (Hebrew: זִבְחֵי, *zibḥê*, H2077), "sacrifices" (BSB footnote), meaning the meat of a sacrificial meal eaten at home, as in Proverbs 7:14. *Observation:* the rich man's table in these sayings is sometimes a religious feast. *Inference:* abundance and even religious festivity are worth less than peace in the household.

**Rations and "my portion."** Agur asks, "feed me with the bread that is my portion" (Proverbs 30:8). The phrase is **leḥem ḥuqqî** (Hebrew: לֶחֶם חֻקִּי, *leḥem ḥuqqî*, H2706), "bread of my allotment." The word is used for the "portions" the valiant woman gives her maidservants (Proverbs 31:15). Ancient households, temples and garrisons ran on fixed rations, which ostraca from Arad record for soldiers. Agur asks for the dependable daily share rather than surplus. This is the background to the petition for daily bread in Matthew 6:11.

### 5. Archaeology and External History

**Ancient Near Eastern wisdom and law.** Israel's sages shared their concerns with neighboring cultures. The parallels are real but uneven. Proverbs adopts much and changes a good deal.

| Source | Date (approximate) | Parallel | Proverbs | What Proverbs does differently |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| *Instruction of Amenemope* (Egypt; ch. 2 in Lichtheim's numbering) | Ramesside period, c. 1300-1075 BC | Warns against robbing the wretched and attacking the weak | Proverbs 22:22-23 | The LORD Himself becomes the plaintiff ("the LORD will take up their case") |
| *Amenemope* ch. 6 | same | Do not move the markers on field borders or encroach on a widow's boundary; better poverty in the hand of the god than riches in the storehouse | Proverbs 22:28; 23:10-11; 15:25; 15:16; 16:8 | The orphan's "Redeemer" (Hebrew: גֹּאֵל, *gōʾēl*, H1350) is the LORD; "better" is defined by the fear of the LORD (Proverbs 15:16) |
| *Amenemope* ch. 7 | same | Riches make themselves wings like geese and fly to the sky (paraphrase) | Proverbs 23:4-5 | The bird becomes an **eagle** or griffon vulture (Hebrew: נֶשֶׁר, *nešer*, H5404) |
| *Amenemope* chs. 16-17 | same | Do not tamper with the scales, weights or grain measure | Proverbs 11:1; 16:11; 20:10, 23 | Scales "are from the LORD"; fraud is "abomination to the LORD" |
| *Amenemope* ch. 13 (as usually rendered) | same | Counsels forgiving most of a poor man's debt | Proverbs 19:17; 28:8 | Proverbs frames kindness to the poor as a loan to the LORD, who repays |
| *Book of the Dead* spell 125 | New Kingdom onward | The deceased denies diminishing the grain measure or falsifying the balance | Proverbs 11:1; 20:23 | No afterlife tribunal; the LORD's present "delight" and "abomination" |
| Babylonian *Great Hymn to Shamash* | Composition date uncertain (known from 1st-millennium BC copies) | The sun-god punishes the merchant with two sets of weights and blesses the honest one | Proverbs 20:10, 23 | The LORD, not the sun-god, is the judge of trade |
| *Eloquent Peasant* (Egypt) | Middle Kingdom | A peasant robbed of his goods pleads with an official, likened to a balance that must not tilt | Proverbs 22:22-23; 13:23 | The defender of the poor is God before any official |
| Ugaritic *Aqhat* and *Kirta* epics | c. 13th century BC (tablets) | The ideal ruler judges the widow's case and the orphan's cause at the gate | Proverbs 31:8-9; 29:14 (see "The King") | Proverbs extends the duty to every householder (Proverbs 14:31; 22:9) |
| Laws of Hammurabi, epilogue; §117 | c. 1750 BC | Justice so the strong do not oppress the weak; debt-servitude of family members limited to three years | Proverbs 22:7, 16, 22 | Grounding in creation: the LORD made rich and poor (Proverbs 22:2) |

The connection between *Amenemope* and Proverbs 22:17-24:22 was argued in detail by Adolf Erman in 1924, shortly after E. A. Wallis Budge published the papyrus (British Museum EA 10474; extracts 1922, facsimile 1923). The direction and extent of the dependence are still debated. Most scholars see Proverbs adapting selected Egyptian material, not translating it. The Hebrew of Proverbs 22:20 likely refers to "thirty sayings" (the Ketiv/Qere is set out in the Linguistic Analysis), and *Amenemope* likewise has thirty chapters. The chapter numbers above follow Miriam Lichtheim's *Ancient Egyptian Literature*, vol. 2 (also *Context of Scripture* 1.47). Chapters 2, 6 and 7 were checked against a published translation; the numbering given for chapters 13 and 16-17 follows Lichtheim's sequence but was not checked against the printed volume.

**Inscribed weights.** Excavations at Jerusalem, Lachish, Arad and many other Judean sites have produced hundreds of small dome-shaped limestone weights from the late 8th to early 6th centuries BC. Many are inscribed with a sign usually read as "shekel," with numerals, or with the names of fractional units: **beqaʿ** (half-shekel; Genesis 24:22; Exodus 38:26), **pîm** (about two-thirds of a shekel; 1 Samuel 13:21) and **neṣep**. Raz Kletter's study (*Economic Keystones: The Weight System of the Kingdom of Judah*, 1998) documents a fairly standardized system, with a shekel of roughly 11.3-11.5 grams. It also shows measurable variation between specimens of the same denomination. *Inference:* every transaction depended on trusting a merchant's stones. A just weight was a moral achievement, not a technical given.

**Silver hoards and the move to coinage.** Hoards of cut and broken silver ("hacksilber") from Iron Age sites, such as the large hoard from Eshtemoa, confirm that silver was weighed, not counted. Small silver coins stamped "Yehud" appear only in the Persian period (4th century BC). The weighing sayings of Proverbs thus fit a pre-coinage economy and remained meaningful after coinage arrived.

**The confiscated garment.** The Mesad Hashavyahu ostracon, a late-7th-century BC Hebrew letter from a coastal fort near Yavneh-Yam, contains a reaper's plea. He says an overseer seized his garment after he had finished his quota, and he asks the official to have it returned. It is the clearest extra-biblical illustration of the garment-pledge practice behind Exodus 22:26-27 and Proverbs 20:16 and 27:13. It is also an illustration of the poor man who "pleads for mercy" (Proverbs 18:23).

**Royal storage and estates.** Hundreds of jar handles stamped *lmlk* ("belonging to the king"), many tied to Hezekiah's reign, along with storehouses at sites such as Lachish and Beersheba, show a royal economy of stored grain, wine and oil (compare 2 Chronicles 32:27-29). The Samaria ostraca record wine and oil shipped to individuals in the 8th century BC. These are the goods of "the one who loves wine and oil," who "will never be rich" (Proverbs 21:17), and of the prophets' complacent elite who drink from bowls and anoint themselves with the finest oils (Amos 6:6, by reference).

**Gates, walls and the fortified city.** Excavated multi-chambered gates with benches (Dan, Gezer, Megiddo, Beersheba, Lachish) give the setting of Proverbs 22:22 and 31:23. The image of wealth as a "fortified city" and "high wall" (Proverbs 10:15; 18:11) drew on the most visible kind of security in the Iron Age landscape. Sennacherib's reliefs of the siege of Lachish (701 BC, from Nineveh) show such walls and towers being breached. *Application, not proof:* even the strongest real walls fell, which sharpens the irony of a wall that exists "in his imagination" (Proverbs 18:11).

**External histories of debt.** Nehemiah 5 is the Bible's own historical record of a debt crisis: "We are mortgaging our fields, our vineyards, and our homes to get grain during the famine" (Nehemiah 5:3). Nehemiah himself had been lending money and grain and calls for an end to "this usury" (Nehemiah 5:10). Elephantine loan contracts of the 5th century BC show interest compounding into principal and creditors entitled to seize the debtor's goods on default. These documents are later than much of Proverbs, but they show the same pressures the sayings address.

### 6. Political-Socio-Economic Context

**An agrarian kinship economy.** Throughout the biblical period, most Israelites lived in villages and worked family land: grain in the valleys, vines and olives on terraced hillsides, sheep and goats on the margins. The core unit was the **father's house** (Hebrew: בֵּית אָב, *bêt ʾāb*), an extended household that owned its **inheritance** (Hebrew: נַחֲלָה, *naḥălâ*, H5159). Ideally the land was inalienable, held from the LORD as tenant (Leviticus 25:23), as Naboth's refusal to sell his vineyard shows (1 Kings 21:3). Boundary stones marked these inheritances (Deuteronomy 19:14; 27:17). Moving one was theft from a family's future, not just from its present (Proverbs 22:28; 23:10). When the **kinsman-redeemer** (Hebrew: גֹּאֵל, *gōʾēl*, H1350) could buy back lost land (Leviticus 25:25; Ruth 4; Jeremiah 32:6-15), the family survived. When there was no male protector, as with the widow and the fatherless, land was most at risk. That is why the LORD Himself is named the protector of "the boundaries of the widow" (Proverbs 15:25) and the strong Redeemer of the fatherless (Proverbs 23:11).

**Monarchy and stratification.** The monarchy brought taxation, forced labor and royal estates (1 Samuel 8:11-17; 1 Kings 5:13-14; 12:4; 1 Chronicles 27:25-31). It created a class of officials and creditors who could accumulate land. The 8th-century prophets describe the result: houses and fields joined together until no room is left (Isaiah 5:8), the needy sold for the price of sandals (Amos 2:6), fields coveted and seized (Micah 2:1-2). Proverbs approaches the same reality through sayings rather than oracles. The rich rule the poor (Proverbs 22:7). The poor man's harvest is swept away "without justice" (Proverbs 13:23). A "generation" devours "the oppressed from the earth and the needy from among men" (Proverbs 30:14). *Observation:* Proverbs knows structural injustice as well as personal vice. Royal responsibility for this, through the king's courts and the king's defense of the poor (Proverbs 29:14; 31:8-9), is developed in "The King."

**Many roads to poverty.** Read against its social setting, Proverbs names several causes of poverty. They include laziness (Proverbs 6:6-11; 24:30-34, developed in "The Sluggard"); pleasure-seeking and drink (Proverbs 21:17; 23:20-21); haste and greed (Proverbs 21:5; 28:20, 22); rejecting correction (Proverbs 13:18); and oppression and lost justice (Proverbs 13:23; 22:22; 28:3). Behind all of these stands the plain fact that the LORD made rich and poor alike (Proverbs 22:2; 29:13). Proverbs 28:3 pictures one who oppresses the poor as "a driving rain that leaves no food." In a land where a single storm or a failed season could wipe out a family's year, the image is vivid. The Hebrew of the subject is disputed. The Linguistic Analysis prefers the Masoretic reading, in which the oppressor is himself a poor man; the BSB's "destitute leader" blends that reading with an emended "ruler."

**The Persian-period economy (if the book was finished then).** Under Persian rule Yehud was a small temple province paying imperial tribute in silver. Nehemiah 5:4 mentions "the king's tax on our fields and vineyards," and coinage was beginning. Debt, mortgage and the enslavement of children were acute problems (Nehemiah 5:1-13). If the collections were edited in that period, the sayings on surety, interest and generosity were a word on target. This is inference about reception, offered with caution.

### 7. Social Norms and Issues

**Honor, shame and the social cost of poverty.** In a face-to-face society, wealth bought standing and poverty cost friends. Proverbs records this without sentiment: "The poor man is hated even by his neighbor, but many are those who love the rich" (Proverbs 14:20); "Wealth attracts many friends, but a poor man is deserted by his friend" (Proverbs 19:4); "All the brothers of a poor man hate him" (Proverbs 19:7). These are observations of social reality, not moral approval. The next line of Proverbs 14:21 immediately calls despising one's neighbor a sin. A "good name" outranks great riches (Proverbs 22:1) because honor was itself a form of capital. (The friendship dimension of these sayings belongs to "The Friend and Neighbor.")

**Family structure and inheritance.** The household was the unit of production, consumption and inheritance. A good man's estate reaches "his children's children" (Proverbs 13:22). "Houses and wealth are inherited from fathers" (Proverbs 19:14). An inheritance "gained quickly" at the start will not be blessed at the end (Proverbs 20:21); some interpreters see here an heir demanding his share early (compare Luke 15:12). A wise servant could share the inheritance "as one of the brothers" (Proverbs 17:2). In this society, then, character could outrank birth in practice, though that was exceptional. Robbing one's own parents and calling it no offense (Proverbs 28:24) was a crime inside the family economy. The valiant woman runs a productive household enterprise: she buys a field, plants a vineyard, makes and sells linen, and supplies sashes "to the merchants" (Proverbs 31:16-24). Her hands also open to the poor (Proverbs 31:20). (Her role in the book's design belongs to "Wisdom, Folly, and the Valiant Woman.") The widow and the fatherless, without an adult male to defend the household's land, were the most exposed members of this system (Proverbs 15:25; 23:10).

**Worldview and cosmology: an ordered, personally governed world.** The sages assumed that the world has moral grain, so that conduct and consequence are linked. Klaus Koch (1955) famously described this as a deed that carries its own results, like seed producing a crop (his phrase was a "fate-producing deed sphere"; the label "act-consequence connection," *Tun-Ergehen-Zusammenhang*, became standard in the debate his essay started). Egypt expressed a comparable idea through *maʿat*, the cosmic order of truth and justice, pictured as a goddess with a feather and associated with the balance. Proverbs shares the sense of order (Proverbs 11:24-25; 22:8) but places it in the hands of a personal God. It is "the blessing of the LORD" that enriches (Proverbs 10:22). The LORD repays the one who lends to Him by helping the poor (Proverbs 19:17), takes up the case of the robbed (Proverbs 22:23), and weighs hearts (Proverbs 16:2; 21:2; 24:12). The sages also stretch the time-frame of consequence. Riches fail "in the day of wrath" (Proverbs 11:4; compare Zephaniah 1:18, "Neither their silver nor their gold will be able to deliver them on the Day of the LORD's wrath"). The sinner's wealth eventually passes to the righteous (Proverbs 13:22; 28:8). There "is a future" for the one who does not envy sinners (Proverbs 23:17-18). *Observation:* the book qualifies its own pattern with "better than" sayings (Proverbs 15:16-17; 16:8; 19:1; 28:6) and with sayings in which the poor suffer injustice (Proverbs 13:23). *Inference:* in its ancient setting, the retribution principle was a confident generalization about God's governance. It was not a mechanical guarantee, and the sages themselves did not treat it as one. The theological weighing belongs to the Theological Analysis, but the historical point matters: ancient wisdom writers knew perfectly well that the wicked sometimes prosper (compare Job 21; Psalm 73).

**Creation, not only covenant, as the ground of justice to the poor.** The repeated formula that the poor share a Maker grounds justice in creation: "Whoever oppresses the poor taunts their Maker" (Proverbs 14:31); "He who mocks the poor insults their Maker" (Proverbs 17:5); "The LORD is Maker of them all" (Proverbs 22:2; compare Proverbs 29:13). Egyptian and Mesopotamian texts present the gods and kings as protectors of the weak. Proverbs goes further in its appeal: rich and poor stand on the same level before the One who made them. *Observation:* Proverbs rarely cites Israel's exodus story, which is the Torah's usual ground for generosity (Deuteronomy 15:15; 24:18), but its commands match Torah closely.

**The "good eye" and the "evil eye."** In Proverbs, the eye is a figure for disposition toward others' need. The generous man is literally "he whose eye is good" (Proverbs 22:9, BSB footnote). The stingy man is "him whose eye is evil" (Proverbs 23:6, BSB footnote) or "a man whose eye is evil" (Proverbs 28:22, BSB footnote). This is not the folk-magical "evil eye" known across the Mediterranean world. It is the grudging look Deuteronomy 15:9 warns against, looking "upon your poor brother begrudgingly." The idiom continues into the New Testament (Matthew 6:22-23; 20:15).

**Intercultural dynamics.** Proverbs openly belongs to an international conversation about wisdom (1 Kings 4:30-31 says Solomon's wisdom was "greater than that of all the men of the East, greater than all the wisdom of Egypt"). On wealth, the sages took over Egyptian themes and gave them a new theological center. *Amenemope*'s "god" and moon-god become the LORD, who pleads the poor man's case. The Egyptian geese become an eagle. The appeal to *maʿat* becomes the LORD's own "delight" in a just weight (Proverbs 11:1). The trading world was multicultural. The Hebrew word for the valiant woman's buyer in Proverbs 31:24 is literally "the Canaanite" (Hebrew: כְּנַעֲנִי, *kənaʿănî*, H3669), because Phoenician and Canaanite traders were so identified with commerce that the ethnic name came to mean "merchant." Hosea 12:7 turns the same word into a rebuke of Israel: "A merchant loves to defraud with dishonest scales in his hands." The warnings about guaranteeing debts for a *zār* or *nokrî* (Proverbs 6:1; 11:15; 20:16) reflect a world where Israelites dealt with outsiders whom kinship obligations did not bind, and where Torah allowed interest to be charged to foreigners (Deuteronomy 23:20). If Agur and Lemuel were from Massa, the book's final word on wealth itself comes from the edge of Israel's world.

**Iconography.** The sayings draw on a shared stock of images that ancient viewers would have recognized:

| Image in Proverbs | Proverbs | ANE iconography and artifacts |
|:--|:--|:--|
| The balance and its stones | Proverbs 11:1; 16:2, 11; 20:23 | Egyptian vignettes of the heart weighed against the feather of *maʿat* (e.g. the Papyrus of Ani); tomb scenes of officials weighing metal rings; Judean dome-shaped weights |
| Riches with wings | Proverbs 23:5 | *Amenemope*'s geese flying to the sky; the eagle or vulture as a common symbol of swift flight |
| The ancient boundary stone | Proverbs 22:28; 23:10 | Babylonian *kudurru* stelae with divine emblems and curses on whoever moves them |
| The fortified city and high wall | Proverbs 10:15; 18:11 | Iron Age walled towns; Assyrian siege reliefs (Lachish) |
| The open palm toward the poor | Proverbs 31:20 | The gesture of the outstretched hand as giving, set against the clenched or withdrawn hand of Deuteronomy 15:7-8 |
| The strong tower | Proverbs 18:10 | Watchtowers and city towers, contrasted in Proverbs 18:10-11 with the rich man's imagined wall |

**The working notes' character chart.** Distinctions.md charts a movement from the Simple to the Sluggard or the Prudent, and then to the Mocker or the Wise. It has no place for rich or poor, and the historical evidence suggests why. In Proverbs' social world, economic status did not mark moral stage. A poor man may walk in integrity (Proverbs 19:1; 28:6) or see through a self-satisfied rich man (Proverbs 28:11). A rich man may be "wise in his own eyes" (Proverbs 28:11). Poverty comes to the sluggard, but also to the pleasure-lover, the drunkard, the hasty, the stingy and the victim of injustice. *Inference:* wealth and poverty work in Proverbs as conditions that test and reveal character (Proverbs 30:8-9), not as positions on the path of character. Whether the sluggard "hardens into" the mocker, as the chart proposes, is for "The Sluggard" and "The Two Paths" to test.

### 8. Geography

**The household and the field.** The smallest stage is the family farm. It holds the storehouse and the vat (Proverbs 3:10), the flocks and herds whose condition the owner must know because "riches are not forever" (Proverbs 27:23-27), and the poor man's **newly broken ground** (Hebrew: נִיר, *nîr*, H5215), marginal land brought under the plow that yields food only to be swept away (Proverbs 13:23; compare Jeremiah 4:3; Hosea 10:12). The Judean hill country, with its terraces, thin soils and dependence on seasonal rain (Deuteronomy 11:10-17), made every harvest precarious. That is the background to the destructive "driving rain" of Proverbs 28:3 and the "clouds and wind without rain" of Proverbs 25:14.

**The village boundary and the town gate.** One step outward are the boundary stones between family plots (Proverbs 22:28; 23:10) and the gate where land disputes were judged (Proverbs 22:22; 31:23). Here economic life became public and legal.

**The market and the city.** In the town market the balance and the weight-bag did their work (Proverbs 11:1; 16:11; 20:10, 14, 23). There grain was hoarded or sold (Proverbs 11:26), and loans were guaranteed by a handclasp before witnesses (Proverbs 17:18). The walled city supplied the image of wealth as a stronghold (Proverbs 10:15; 18:11). Jerusalem, with its court and royal storehouses, is the probable setting in which the collections were preserved (Proverbs 25:1).

**International trade.** Beyond Judah lay the wider world of commerce. Merchant ships brought food "from afar" (Proverbs 31:14). Canaanite traders bought finished goods (Proverbs 31:24). Corals, gold and silver were imported prestige goods (Proverbs 3:14-15; 8:10-11, 19; 20:15). Solomon's sea trade through Ezion-geber and Ophir (1 Kings 9:26-28; 10:22) is the narrative backdrop to the book's lavish comparisons between wisdom and precious metals. Egypt, the source of *Amenemope*, and northern Arabia, the possible home of Agur and Lemuel, mark the outer edges of the book's geography.

**The widening horizon.** The geography of the topic expands in steps: household and field, gate and market, city and court, international trade, and finally creation itself. Wisdom's wealth is set against the whole created order (Proverbs 3:19-20; 8:18-31), and the LORD is the Maker of every rich and poor person on earth (Proverbs 22:2). The same God stands behind every scale in the marketplace (Proverbs 16:11) and every boundary stone in the field (Proverbs 23:10-11). The sages refuse to treat any economic space as outside His rule.

---

## Linguistic Analysis

A note on method. The word counts and verb parsings below come from a lemma search of the Westminster Leningrad Codex as tagged by the Open Scriptures Hebrew Bible (OSHB), which uses Strong's numbering. Where a Ketiv/Qere pair exists (the written consonants versus the reading the Masoretes marked to be read aloud), the counts include both forms unless stated otherwise. The Greek evidence comes from the CATSS morphologically tagged Septuagint (LXX) of Proverbs. English renderings in the comparison tables were taken from the parallel-version pages on Bible Hub. Scripture quotations are from the Berean Standard Bible (BSB). These counts are *observations*. What they mean for the teaching is *inference*, and it is labelled as such.

### 1. Original Language

The wealth sayings are written in **Classical Biblical Hebrew poetry**, almost all of it in the compact two-line **proverb** (Hebrew: מָשָׁל, *māšāl*, H4912; Proverbs 1:1, 6). The topic runs through every collection in the book. It appears in the father's instructions (Proverbs 3:9-10, 27-28; 6:1-5), Solomon's sentence sayings (Proverbs 10:1-22:16), the "sayings of the wise" (Proverbs 22:17-24:22), the Hezekiah collection (Proverbs 25:1-29:27), Agur's prayer (Proverbs 30:7-9) and the acrostic poem on the valiant woman (Proverbs 31:20). The vocabulary stays stable from one collection to the next, so usage can be traced across the whole book without changing dictionaries partway through.

Three features of the language shape this topic.

**Proverbs has its own economic vocabulary.** Several of the book's key wealth and poverty words are rare elsewhere, or found nowhere else:

| Lexeme | Strong's | Whole OT | Proverbs | Observation |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| **rêš / rîš** "poverty" | H7389 | 7 | 7 | Found only in Proverbs |
| **hôn** "wealth, substance; enough" | H1952 | 26 | 18 | Proverbs' favorite wealth word (Ezekiel 4, Psalms 3) |
| **maḥsôr** "want, lack" | H4270 | 13 | 8 | The usual word for the consequence of vice |
| **rāš** "poor (man)" | H7326 | 24 | 16 | Two-thirds of all uses; the rest mostly 2 Samuel 12 and Ecclesiastes |
| **ʿāšîr** "rich (man)" | H6223 | 23 | 9 | Nearly always paired with a "poor" word |
| **ʿōšer** "riches" | H6239 | 37 | 9 | Shared with Chronicles and Ecclesiastes |
| **dal** "poor, weak, low" | H1800 | 47 | 15 | The justice and generosity word |
| **ʾebyôn** "needy" | H34 | 61 | 4 | At home in the Psalms (23) and Deuteronomy (7) |
| **ʿānî** "afflicted, poor" | H6041 | 79 | 8 (3 of them Ketiv) | At home in the Psalms (31) and Isaiah (13) |

**Observation:** the two poor words most typical of the Psalms and the prophets, **ʾebyôn** (Hebrew: אֶבְיוֹן, *ʾebyôn*) and **ʿānî** (Hebrew: עָנִי, *ʿānî*), are uncommon in Proverbs. They appear mostly where the book turns to justice and advocacy (Proverbs 14:31; 22:22; 30:14; 31:9, 20). For ordinary social and economic description the sages prefer **rāš** (Hebrew: רָשׁ, *rāš*) and **dal** (Hebrew: דַּל, *dal*). **Inference:** Proverbs speaks of poverty first as a social and economic condition seen in the market, the family and the gate. It is not first a term for the pious, oppressed "poor of the LORD" of the Psalms, although the two vocabularies meet in the justice sayings.

**Several registers mix.** The topic draws on at least four specialist vocabularies:

- **Agrarian**: barns and wine vats (Proverbs 3:10), firstfruits (Proverbs 3:9), fallow ground (Proverbs 13:23), grain hoarding and selling (Proverbs 11:26), flocks (Proverbs 27:23-27).
- **Commercial**: scales, weight-stones and the merchant's bag (Proverbs 11:1; 16:11; 20:10, 23), haggling (Proverbs 20:14), trade profit (Proverbs 3:14; 31:18).
- **Credit**: standing surety, striking hands, debts, lending and interest (Proverbs 6:1-5; 11:15; 17:18; 22:7, 26-27; 28:8).
- **Legal**: the gate as law court, "plead a case," the kinsman-redeemer and boundary stones (Proverbs 22:22-23, 28; 23:10-11).

Proverbs uses all four registers with moral and theological weight. A false scale is an "abomination to the LORD" (Proverbs 11:1). A loan to the poor is a loan "to the LORD" (Proverbs 19:17).

**Greek and English flatten the field.** The LXX translates the four Hebrew poor words mostly with two Greek ones, **πτωχός** (Greek: πτωχός, *ptōchos*, "beggar, poor") and **πένης** (Greek: πένης, *penēs*, "poor, laboring poor"), and moves between them freely (it occasionally uses others, such as *asthenēs*, "weak," for **ʿānî** in Proverbs 22:22 and 31:9, and *endeēs*, "needy," in Proverbs 3:27). In Proverbs 14:31 it uses *penēs* for **dal** and *ptōchos* for **ʾebyôn**. In Proverbs 22:22 it uses *penēs* and *ptōchos* for the repeated **dal**. English versions do the same: BSB "poor" covers **rāš**, **dal** and sometimes **ʿānî**, and "needy" usually renders **ʾebyôn**. The distinctions traced in this section can be seen only in the Hebrew, which is why the Hebrew terms are given throughout.

### 2. Lexical Semantics

**The vocabulary of wealth.**

| Term | Strong's | Root | Meaning | Occurrences (Prov / OT) | Key Usage |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| **hôn** (Hebrew: הוֹן, *hôn*) | H1952 | הון | substance, wealth; "enough" | 18 / 26 | Offered to the LORD (Proverbs 3:9); a "fortified city" (Proverbs 10:15; 18:11); useless on the day of wrath (Proverbs 11:4); "Enough!" (Proverbs 30:15-16) |
| **ʿōšer** (Hebrew: עֹשֶׁר, *ʿōšer*) | H6239 | עשׁר | riches | 9 / 37 | Wisdom's gift (Proverbs 3:16; 8:18; 22:4); misplaced trust (Proverbs 11:28); one half of Agur's "neither ... nor" (Proverbs 30:8) |
| **ʿāšîr** (Hebrew: עָשִׁיר, *ʿāšîr*) | H6223 | עשׁר | rich (man) | 9 / 23 | Paired with a poor word in 8 of its 9 uses (Proverbs 10:15; 14:20; 18:23; 22:2, 7, 16; 28:6, 11); the exception, Proverbs 18:11, repeats the first line of Proverbs 10:15 |
| **ʿāšar** (Hebrew: עָשַׁר, *ʿāšar*) | H6238 | עשׁר | be or become rich; hiph. make rich | 6 / 17 | The LORD's blessing "enriches" (Proverbs 10:22); human haste "to get rich" (Proverbs 23:4; 28:20) |
| **ʾôṣār** (Hebrew: אוֹצָר, *ʾôṣār*) | H214 | אצר | treasure, storehouse | 5 / 79 | literally "treasures of wickedness" (Proverbs 10:2); "great treasure with turmoil" (Proverbs 15:16); treasure by a lying tongue (Proverbs 21:6) |
| **ḥōsen** (Hebrew: חֹסֶן, *ḥōsen*) | H2633 | חסן | stored wealth | 2 / 5 | In the house of the righteous (Proverbs 15:6); "not forever" (Proverbs 27:24) |
| **təbûʾâ** (Hebrew: תְּבוּאָה, *təbûʾâ*) | H8393 | בוא | yield, income | — | "Great gain with injustice" (Proverbs 16:8); the income of the wicked leads to sin (Proverbs 10:16) |

The terms are not interchangeable. **hôn** is the concrete word for property: what one has, can offer, can lose. Its semantic range reaches as far as "sufficiency," as the cry "Enough!" in Proverbs 30:15-16 shows. **ʿōšer** is the more abstract word for "riches" as a status, and it is the word paired with **kābôd** ("honor," H3519) when Wisdom distributes her gifts (Proverbs 3:16; 8:18; 22:4). **Observation:** when the book speaks of wealth as Wisdom's reward it prefers **ʿōšer**. When it speaks of wealth as something to rely on, use or lose it prefers **hôn** (Proverbs 10:15; 11:4; 13:11; 18:11; 19:4; 28:8, 22). **Inference:** the vocabulary itself keeps two ideas apart: riches as a by-product of a wise life, and property as an object of trust.

**The vocabulary of poverty.**

| Term | Strong's | Root | Meaning | Occurrences (Prov / OT) | Key Usage |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| **rāš** (Hebrew: רָשׁ, *rāš*) | H7326 | רושׁ | poor (man), destitute | 16 / 24 | Hated by neighbor and kin (Proverbs 14:20; 19:7); walking in integrity (Proverbs 19:1; 28:6); shares a Maker with the rich (Proverbs 22:2; 29:13) |
| **rêš / rîš** (Hebrew: רֵישׁ, *rêš*) | H7389 | רושׁ | poverty | 7 / 7 | Comes on the sluggard "like a robber" (Proverbs 6:11; 24:34); the "ruin" of the poor (Proverbs 10:15); Agur's first danger (Proverbs 30:8) |
| **dal** (Hebrew: דַּל, *dal*) | H1800 | דלל | low, weak, poor | 15 / 47 | Oppressed (Proverbs 14:31; 22:16, 22); "a loan to the LORD" (Proverbs 19:17); his cry ignored (Proverbs 21:13); discerning (Proverbs 28:11) |
| **ʾebyôn** (Hebrew: אֶבְיוֹן, *ʾebyôn*) | H34 | אבה (?) | needy | 4 / 61 | Honored by kindness (Proverbs 14:31); devoured (Proverbs 30:14); defended (Proverbs 31:9, 20) |
| **ʿānî / ʿānāw** (Hebrew: עָנִי / עָנָו, *ʿānî / ʿānāw*) | H6041 / H6035 | ענה III | afflicted; humble | 8 / 79 and 3 / 26 | Crushed at the gate (Proverbs 22:22); grim days (Proverbs 15:15); Ketiv/Qere alternation in Proverbs 3:34; 14:21; 16:19 |
| **maḥsôr** (Hebrew: מַחְסוֹר, *maḥsôr*) | H4270 | חסר | want, lack | 8 / 13 | Result of withholding (Proverbs 11:24), pleasure (Proverbs 21:17), haste (Proverbs 21:5), oppression (Proverbs 22:16); absent for the giver (Proverbs 28:27) |
| **ḥeser** (Hebrew: חֶסֶר, *ḥeser*) | H2639 | חסר | want | 1 / 2 | Overtakes the stingy man who chases wealth (Proverbs 28:22) |
| **yāraš** niphal (Hebrew: יָרַשׁ, *yāraš*) | H3423 | ירשׁ | be dispossessed, become poor | 3 of 4 uses in Prov | Sleep (Proverbs 20:13), drink (Proverbs 23:21), Agur's fear (Proverbs 30:9) |

The table supports one of the most important lexical observations in this study. **Observation:** the two words that name a *person* as poor, **rāš** and **dal**, are almost never used in sayings that blame the poor person. Across the 31 occurrences of these two words, the poor man is hated (Proverbs 14:20; 19:7), mocked (Proverbs 17:5), oppressed (Proverbs 14:31; 22:16, 22; 28:3, where the victims are **dallîm**), ruled (Proverbs 22:7), pleading (Proverbs 18:23), ignored (Proverbs 21:13), honored for integrity (Proverbs 19:1; 28:6), discerning (Proverbs 28:11), given to (Proverbs 22:9; 28:27) and made by God (Proverbs 22:2; 29:13). There are only two exceptions. In Proverbs 10:4 "a slack hand" produces **rāʾš**, which here is better taken as an abstract "poverty" (see §5). In Proverbs 28:3, on the Masoretic reading, the oppressor is himself a **rāš** (see §6-7). The sayings that blame vice for poverty use the *outcome* words instead: **rêš**, **maḥsôr**, **ḥeser** and the niphal of **yāraš**. These words name a condition someone falls into. They do not name a class of people (Proverbs 6:11; 11:24; 13:18; 14:23; 21:5, 17; 22:16; 23:21; 24:34; 28:19, 22).

**Inference:** the vocabulary itself keeps two claims apart. One is that vice tends to produce poverty, which is a claim about outcomes. The other would be that the poor are vicious, a claim about persons, and Proverbs does not make it. The retribution sayings warn the reader about where *his own* folly will lead. They do not supply a vocabulary for judging the poor neighbor. Laziness as one cause of poverty is developed in "The Sluggard"; this study notes only that the poverty words used of the sluggard are outcome words.

**The vocabulary of generosity and withholding.**

| Term | Strong's | Root | Meaning | Occurrences (Prov / OT) | Key Usage |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| **ḥānan** (Hebrew: חָנַן, *ḥānan*) | H2603 | חנן | be gracious, show favor | 6 / 79 | To the poor in Proverbs 14:21, 31; 19:17; 28:8 (the other two uses, Proverbs 21:10; 26:25, are not economic) |
| **pāzar** piel (Hebrew: פָּזַר, *pizzēr*) | H6340 | פזר | scatter, distribute | 1 / 10 | "One gives freely [lit. scatters], yet gains even more" (Proverbs 11:24); compare Psalm 112:9 |
| **ḥāśak** (Hebrew: חָשַׂךְ, *ḥāśak*) | H2820 | חשׂך | hold back, spare | 6 / 27 | Withholding "what is right" (Proverbs 11:24); the righteous "does not hold back" (Proverbs 21:26); also "spares the rod" (Proverbs 13:24) |
| **mānaʿ** (Hebrew: מָנַע, *mānaʿ*) | H4513 | מנע | withhold, refuse | 5 / 29 | "Do not withhold good" (Proverbs 3:27); the grain-hoarder (Proverbs 11:26); Agur's "do not refuse me" (Proverbs 30:7) |
| **ṭôb-ʿayin** (Hebrew: טוֹב־עַיִן, *ṭôb-ʿayin*) | H2896 + H5869 | — | "good of eye" = generous | 1 | Shares bread with the poor (Proverbs 22:9) |
| **raʿ ʿāyin** (Hebrew: רַע עָיִן, *raʿ ʿāyin*) | H7451 + H5869 | — | "evil of eye" = stingy, grudging | 2 | The grudging host (Proverbs 23:6); the stingy man in haste (Proverbs 28:22) |
| **lāwâ** hiphil (Hebrew: הִלְוָה, *hilwâ*) | H3867 | לוה | lend | 3 tokens in Prov (2 hiphil "lender," 1 qal "borrower") | "A loan to the LORD" (Proverbs 19:17); "the borrower is slave to the lender" (Proverbs 22:7) |
| **šālam** piel (Hebrew: שִׁלֵּם, *šillēm*) | H7999 | שׁלם | repay in full | 10 / 116 (all stems) | The LORD repays the lender to the poor (Proverbs 19:17) and the one who feeds his enemy (Proverbs 25:22) |
| **bərākâ** (Hebrew: בְּרָכָה, *bərākâ*) | H1293 | ברך | blessing; gift | — | "The blessing of the LORD enriches" (Proverbs 10:22); "a generous soul" is literally a "soul of blessing" (Proverbs 11:25); blessing crowns the seller of grain (Proverbs 11:26) |

Two features of this vocabulary carry the theology.

First, the main generosity verb, **ḥānan**, is the verb of *grace*. It is the root of **ḥēn** ("favor," H2580) and of the LORD's own self-description as **ḥannûn** ("gracious," Exodus 34:6). When Proverbs 14:31 says that "whoever is kind [*ḥônēn*] to the needy honors Him," the human act is described with the vocabulary of divine character. **Inference:** generosity in Proverbs is less a transaction than an imitation of the God who "gives grace" to the humble (Proverbs 3:34, where **ḥēn** is the object).

Second, the "eye" idioms describe generosity and stinginess as a way of *looking*. **ṭôb-ʿayin** (Proverbs 22:9) and **raʿ ʿāyin** (Proverbs 23:6; 28:22) have nothing to do with the folk-magic "evil eye." The phrases describe a disposition: a gaze that is open-handed or grudging toward what one has and what others need. Deuteronomy 15:9 uses the same idiom with a verb. Israel is warned lest "your eye be evil" (Hebrew: וְרָעָה עֵינְךָ, *wərāʿâ ʿênəkā*) toward the needy brother (**ʾebyôn**) as the year of release draws near. Proverbs 28:27 reverses the image: the one who "hides his eyes" (Hebrew: מַעְלִים עֵינָיו, *maʿlîm ʿênāyw*) from the poor gathers curses.

**The vocabulary of commerce, credit and justice.**

| Term | Strong's | Root | Meaning | Occurrences (Prov / OT) | Key Usage |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| **mōʾzənayim** (Hebrew: מֹאזְנַיִם, *mōʾzənayim*) | H3976 | אזן II | (pair of) scales | 3 / 15 | "Scales of deceit" (Proverbs 11:1; 20:23); "scales of justice" belong to the LORD (Proverbs 16:11) |
| **ʾeben** (Hebrew: אֶבֶן, *ʾeben*) | H68 | — | stone; weight-stone | — | "A whole stone" (*ʾeben šəlēmâ*, an honest weight) is His delight (Proverbs 11:1); "stone and stone," two sets of weights (Proverbs 20:10, 23) |
| **tôʿēbâ** (Hebrew: תּוֹעֵבָה, *tôʿēbâ*) | H8441 | תעב | abomination | 22 / 118 | False scales (Proverbs 11:1; 20:10, 23); second only to Ezekiel in frequency |
| **ʿārab** (Hebrew: עָרַב, *ʿārab*) | H6148 | ערב I | pledge oneself, stand surety | 6 surety uses in Prov (9 tokens) | Proverbs 6:1; 11:15; 17:18; 20:16; 22:26; 27:13 (the other three tokens, Proverbs 14:10; 20:19; 24:21, are hithpael forms meaning "share, associate") |
| **tāqaʿ kap** (Hebrew: תָּקַע כַּף, *tāqaʿ kap*) | H8628 + H3709 | — | strike the palm | 4 | The handshake that seals a guarantee (Proverbs 6:1; 11:15; 17:18; 22:26) |
| **nešek** (Hebrew: נֶשֶׁךְ, *nešek*) | H5392 | נשׁך | interest ("bite") | 1 / 12 | Proverbs 28:8; paired with **tarbît** as in Leviticus 25:36 |
| **tarbît** (Hebrew: תַּרְבִּית, *tarbît*) | H8636 | רבה | increase, usury | 2 tokens (Ketiv and Qere) / 7 | Proverbs 28:8 |
| **šōḥad** (Hebrew: שֹׁחַד, *šōḥad*) | H7810 | שׁחד | bribe | 4 / 23 | literally "a stone of favor [**ʾeben-ḥēn**] in the eyes of its owner" (Proverbs 17:8); bends justice (Proverbs 17:23); placates wrath (Proverbs 21:14) |
| **mattān** (Hebrew: מַתָּן, *mattān*) | H4976 | נתן | gift | 3 / 5 | Opens doors (Proverbs 18:16); everyone befriends the giver (Proverbs 19:6); in parallel with **šōḥad** (Proverbs 21:14) |
| **gəbûl** (Hebrew: גְּבוּל, *gəbûl*) | H1366 | גבל | boundary | 3 / 241 | The widow's boundary kept by the LORD (Proverbs 15:25); the ancient boundary (Proverbs 22:28; 23:10) |
| **gōʾēl** (Hebrew: גֹּאֵל, *gōʾēl*) | H1350 | גאל | kinsman-redeemer | 1 / 105 | "Their Redeemer is strong" (Proverbs 23:11), the book's only use |

**Observation:** the legal register describes the LORD's role toward the poor. He is the one who "will take up their case" (**rîb**, H7378; Proverbs 22:23; 23:11), their **gōʾēl** (Proverbs 23:11), the one who "plunders" (**qābaʿ**, H6906) their plunderers (Proverbs 22:23), and the owner of the scales (Proverbs 16:11). The commercial and legal vocabularies are not left as purely civil matters. Each one is tied back to the LORD by a phrase such as "abomination to the LORD," "the LORD's," "the LORD will plead," or "their Maker."

**The vocabulary of comparative value.** The "better than" saying is built with **ṭôb ... min** (Hebrew: טוֹב ... מִן, *ṭôb ... min*, "good ... from" = "better than"). The things weighed against wealth include **məʿaṭ** ("a little," H4592; Proverbs 15:16; 16:8), **tōm** ("integrity," H8537; Proverbs 19:1; 28:6), **ṣədāqâ** ("righteousness," H6666; Proverbs 16:8), **yirʾat YHWH** ("the fear of the LORD," Proverbs 15:16), love (Proverbs 15:17), quiet (Proverbs 17:1), **šēm** ("a name," Proverbs 22:1) and wisdom itself (Proverbs 3:14; 8:11, 19; 16:16). Wealth is named in the luxury terms of the ancient world: **kesep** ("silver," H3701), **ḥārûṣ** ("fine gold," H2742), **paz** ("pure gold," H6337) and **pənînîm** ("corals" or "rubies," H6443). **ḥārûṣ** is a homonym: it also means "diligent" (Proverbs 10:4; 12:24, 27; 13:4; 21:5). A reader of Hebrew may hear the overlap (whether the sages exploit it is uncertain; see the Literary Analysis) between the gold that Wisdom outranks (Proverbs 3:14; 8:10, 19; 16:16) and the diligence that tends to gain wealth.

**Canonical horizon (intertextual connections).** The verbal links below were checked in the Hebrew. They show that Proverbs' economic vocabulary is shared with Torah and the Psalms and is not peculiar to the sages:

| Proverbs | Shared wording | Elsewhere |
|:--|:--|:--|
| Proverbs 28:8 | **nešek** + **tarbît** | Leviticus 25:36 (lending to a poor brother); Psalm 15:5 (**nešek** beside **šōḥad**, compare Proverbs 17:23) |
| Proverbs 11:1; 20:10, 23 | **ʾeben wāʾāben** ("stone and stone"), **ʾeben šəlēmâ** ("a whole stone") | Deuteronomy 25:13-15 |
| Proverbs 22:9; 23:6; 28:22 | the "evil eye" toward the **ʾebyôn** | Deuteronomy 15:9; compare the "closed hand" of Deuteronomy 15:7 |
| Proverbs 19:17; 22:7 | **ḥônēn** + **malweh** ("gracious" + "lends") | Psalm 37:21, 26; 112:5 |
| Proverbs 11:24 | **pizzēr** ("scatters") | Psalm 112:9 (literally "he scattered, he gave to the needy [**ʾebyônîm**]"), quoted in 2 Corinthians 9:9 |
| Proverbs 23:11 | **gōʾălām ḥāzāq** ("their Redeemer is strong") | Jeremiah 50:34, word for word in the first two words, followed by the same *rîb*-formula |
| Proverbs 10:22 | hiphil of **ʿāšar** with the LORD as agent | 1 Samuel 2:7 (the LORD *môrîš ûmaʿăšîr*, "makes poor and makes rich") |
| Proverbs 13:8; 11:4 | **kōper** ("ransom"), trust in **ʿōšer** | Psalm 49:6-8 |
| Proverbs 22:7 | the lender rules, the borrower serves | Deuteronomy 28:12, 44 (lending as covenant blessing, borrowing as curse) |
| Proverbs 6:1; 17:18; 22:26 | **ʿārab** (stand surety) | Genesis 43:9; 44:32 (Judah's self-giving surety for Benjamin) |
| Proverbs 22:23 | **qābaʿ** ("rob, plunder"), a rare verb | Malachi 3:8-9 (robbing God in tithes) |

The Greek of the LXX extends this horizon into the New Testament. LXX Proverbs renders the surety verb with **ἐγγυάω** (Greek: ἐγγυάω, *engyaō*; Proverbs 6:1, 3; 17:18) and the noun **ἐγγύη** (*engyē*, "a pledge"; Proverbs 17:18; 22:26). This is the word family of **ἔγγυος** (Greek: ἔγγυος, *engyos*, "guarantor"), which Hebrews 7:22 uses of Jesus. LXX Proverbs 22:8a contains a line with no Hebrew equivalent, "God blesses a cheerful and generous man" (Greek: ἄνδρα ἱλαρὸν καὶ δότην εὐλογεῖ ὁ θεός, *andra hilaron kai dotēn eulogei ho theos*). 2 Corinthians 9:7 (BSB: "For God loves a cheerful giver") appears to echo it, using the same pair **ἱλαρός** (*hilaros*, "cheerful") and **δότης** (*dotēs*, "giver") (see §7). **Inference:** the New Testament's language of generosity and of a divine guarantor grows partly out of the Greek Proverbs. That does not mean the Hebrew surety warnings were "about" Christ. It means the vocabulary was available for that use.

### 3. Morphological and Grammatical Features

**Participles turn practices into identities.** The wealth sayings name people by what they habitually do. They use active participles, often in construct with their object:

| Form | Parsing | Gloss | Reference |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| **ʿōšēq dāl** (עֹשֵׁק דָּל) | qal ptc. ms + object | "oppressor of the poor" | Proverbs 14:31; 22:16 |
| **ḥônēn dāl / ʾebyôn** (חֹנֵן) | qal ptc. ms | "one gracious to the poor" | Proverbs 14:31; 19:17; 28:8 |
| **lōʿēg lārāš** (לֹעֵג לָרָשׁ) | qal ptc. ms | "mocker of the poor" | Proverbs 17:5 |
| **ʾōṭēm ʾoznô** (אֹטֵם אָזְנוֹ) | qal ptc. ms | "one who stops his ear" | Proverbs 21:13 |
| **mōnēaʿ bār** (מֹנֵעַ בָּר) | qal ptc. ms | "withholder of grain" | Proverbs 11:26 |
| **mašbîr** (מַשְׁבִּיר) | hiphil ptc. ms | "seller of grain" | Proverbs 11:26 |
| **nōtēn lārāš** (נוֹתֵן לָרָשׁ) | qal ptc. ms | "giver to the poor" | Proverbs 28:27 |
| **malwēh / lōweh** (מַלְוֶה / לֹוֶה) | hiphil / qal ptc. ms | "lender / borrower" | Proverbs 19:17; 22:7 |
| **tōqəʿê kap**, **ʿōrəbîm** (תֹּקְעֵי כָף, עֹרְבִים) | qal ptc. mp construct / absolute | "strikers of the palm," "guarantors" | Proverbs 22:26 |

**Inference:** the grammar portrays generosity and oppression as settled character, not occasional acts. That fits the book's interest in *who one is becoming*, and it is why the same verse can speak of the one who oppresses and of what that person does to God (Proverbs 14:31).

**A unique pair of reflexive stems (Proverbs 13:7).** In Proverbs 13:7 (BSB: "One pretends to be rich, but has nothing; another pretends to be poor, yet has great wealth") the verbs are **mitʿaššēr** (Hebrew: מִתְעַשֵּׁר, hithpael participle of **ʿāšar**) and **mitrôšēš** (Hebrew: מִתְרוֹשֵׁשׁ, hithpolel participle of **rûš**). OSHB shows that this verse contains the *only* hithpael of **ʿāšar** and the *only* hithpolel of **rûš** in the Hebrew Bible. Both stems here carry an "estimative" or "self-presenting" sense: "making oneself out to be rich / poor." **Observation:** the grammar makes appearance versus reality a theme in its own right. **Inference:** the verse warns the reader that economic status is often a performance, which in turn cautions against reading the retribution sayings as a public scorecard of virtue.

**Divine agency in the causative stem.** The hiphil of **ʿāšar** ("make rich") has two kinds of subject in Proverbs:

- In Proverbs 10:22 the subject is "the blessing of the LORD," resumed by an emphatic feminine pronoun: **birkat YHWH hîʾ taʿăšîr** (Hebrew: בִּרְכַּת יְהוָה הִיא תַעֲשִׁיר, "the blessing of the LORD, *it* makes rich"). The pronoun **hîʾ** stresses the point: it is the blessing, and nothing else, that enriches. 1 Samuel 2:7 uses the same causative of the LORD who "makes rich."
- In Proverbs 23:4 and Proverbs 28:20 the hiphil infinitive **ləhaʿăšîr** (Hebrew: לְהַעֲשִׁיר, "to get rich") has a human subject. It stands with verbs of exertion and haste: "do not wear yourself out [*tîgaʿ*]" (Proverbs 23:4) and "one who hastens [*ʾāṣ*]" (Proverbs 28:20).

**Inference:** the morphology sets the *giver* of wealth (the LORD's blessing) against the *grasper* of wealth (the person who strains or hurries "to get rich"). That is the lexical shape of the book's qualification of the retribution principle.

**Gnomic perfect and participle in the "Maker" sayings.** Proverbs 14:31 and 17:5 share the clause **ḥērēp ʿōśēhû** (Hebrew: חֵרֵף עֹשֵׂהוּ, "has taunted his Maker"). The verb is a piel perfect used gnomically (a timeless truth), and the object is a qal participle of **ʿāśâ** ("make," H6213) with a suffix. The suffix "his" refers to the poor man: the oppressor insults *the poor man's* Maker. Proverbs 22:2 uses the same participle without a suffix, **ʿōśēh kullām** ("Maker of them all"). It is joined to the niphal **nipgāšû** (Hebrew: נִפְגָּשׁוּ, "they meet one another"), a reciprocal niphal. Proverbs 29:13 repeats the frame with the poor man and the "man of oppressions" (**təkākîm**, H8501, found only here). **Observation:** in Proverbs the theology of the poor is carried by participial "Maker" language, that is, by creation and not by covenant terminology.

**Predicate-first word order (Proverbs 19:17).** The Hebrew reads **malwēh YHWH ḥônēn dāl** (Hebrew: מַלְוֵה יְהוָה חוֹנֵן דָּל). It is a verbless clause made of two participial phrases, literally "a lender-of-the-LORD [is] one-gracious-to-the-poor." The construct **malwēh YHWH** ("lender of the LORD") is striking: the LORD becomes the debtor. Most versions take the second phrase as the subject and the first as the predicate ("Whoever is generous to the poor lends to the LORD," ESV). Young's Literal reverses this ("Whoso is lending to Jehovah is favouring the poor"). The second line uses the piel **yəšallem** (Hebrew: יְשַׁלֶּם, "will repay in full") with **gəmûlô** ("his deed, his benefit," H1576) as object. The suffix probably refers to the giver's deed. **Observation:** the word order puts "lender of the LORD" first, so the surprise comes before the explanation.

**Infinitive with an ambiguous pronoun (Proverbs 22:16).** The first line, **ʿōšēq dāl ləharbôt lô** (Hebrew: עֹשֵׁק דָּל לְהַרְבּוֹת לוֹ), consists of a participle, an object, a hiphil infinitive of purpose or result, and the preposition **lô** ("to/for him"). **lô** can refer to the oppressor ("to increase [wealth] for himself") or to the poor man ("one oppresses the poor, [and yet it results] in increase for him"). The second line, **nōtēn ləʿāšîr ʾak-ləmaḥsôr** ("giving to the rich, only to want"), uses the restrictive particle **ʾak** ("only, surely"), which also appears in Proverbs 11:24. See §6 for how the versions divide.

**The "there is ... and there is" frame.** **yēš** (Hebrew: יֵשׁ, "there is," H3426) sets up a paradox in Proverbs 11:24 (**yēš məpazzēr wənôsāp ʿôd**, "there is one who scatters, and is added to still more") and in Proverbs 13:7. The same particle introduces the injustice clause of Proverbs 13:23 (**wəyēš nispeh**, "and there is [what is] swept away"). The construction presents observed exceptions to what one would expect, not general rules.

**Stem play in the generosity cluster (Proverbs 11:24-25).** The verbs change stem to sharpen the paradox. The giver is active: piel participle **məpazzēr** ("scattering"), hiphil participle **marweh** ("one who waters"). What happens to him is passive: niphal participle **nôsāp** ("is added to"), pual imperfect **təduššān** ("will be made fat"), and the final **yôreʾ** ("will be watered"). OSHB parses **yôreʾ** as a hophal, but its spelling is irregular and its root is debated. Most explain it as a by-form of **rāwâ** ("be saturated," H7301), which the parallel requires. **Inference:** the active/passive alternation carries the theology. The generous person acts, and is then acted upon, presumably by God, although the agent is not named.

**Emphatic verb and a Ketiv/Qere (Proverbs 23:5).** "It makes wings for itself" is **ʿāśōh yaʿăśeh-lô** (Hebrew: עָשֹׂה יַעֲשֶׂה־לּוֹ), an infinitive absolute before a finite verb: "it *surely* makes." The opening question differs between the written text and the reading tradition. The Ketiv **hătāʿûp** (qal) means "will your eyes fly to it?" The Qere **hătāʿîp** (hiphil) means "will you make your eyes fly to it?" A second Ketiv/Qere follows: the Ketiv ועיף is a perfect ("and it flies"), the Qere **yāʿûp** an imperfect ("it flies"). Either way, the verb **ʿûp** ("fly," H5774) describes both the eye darting toward wealth and the wealth flying away. **Observation:** the same verb is used for the greedy glance and for the escaping riches, which makes the futility vivid.

**Prohibition with motive in the admonitions.** The sayings of the wise use **ʾal** + jussive followed by a motive clause:

- Proverbs 22:22-23: **ʾal-tigzol dāl kî dal-hûʾ** ("do not rob the poor because he is poor"), with the motive **kî-YHWH yārîb rîbām** ("for the LORD will plead their case").
- Proverbs 22:26-27: **ʾal-təhî bətōqəʿê-kāp** ("do not be among those who strike hands"), with the conditional **ʾim** and the rhetorical **lāmmâ** ("why should he take your bed?").
- Proverbs 23:4: **ʾal-tîgaʿ** ("do not wear yourself out") followed by the imperative **ḥădāl** ("stop!").
- Proverbs 23:10-11: **ʾal-tassēg** (hiphil jussive of **nāsag**, "do not move back") with the motive **kî-gōʾălām ḥāzāq** ("for their Redeemer is strong").

The prologue uses the same pattern (Proverbs 3:27-28: **ʾal-timnaʿ**, **ʾal-tōʾmar**). The sentence sayings of Proverbs 10:1-22:16, by contrast, almost never command. They describe.

**Agur's prayer (Proverbs 30:8-9).** The petition uses a hiphil imperative, **haṭrîpēnî leḥem ḥuqqî** (Hebrew: הַטְרִיפֵנִי לֶחֶם חֻקִּי, "feed me the bread of my portion"). The verb **ṭārap** ("tear [prey]," H2963) is the word for a predator's food. In the hiphil it means "provide food," and Proverbs 31:15 uses the related noun **ṭerep**. **ḥōq** ("portion, allotment," H2706) is what is appointed. The two dangers follow in **pen** ("lest") clauses. The first is **pen-ʾeśbaʿ wəkiḥaštî** ("lest I be full and deny," with a piel perfect consecutive of **kāḥaš**, H3584). The second is **ûpen-ʾiwwārēš wəgānabtî wətāpaśtî** ("lest I be dispossessed and steal and lay hold of [the name]"). **ʾiwwārēš** is the niphal of **yāraš** (H3423), a verb otherwise associated with taking or losing possession of the land. **tāpaś** (H8610) means "seize, handle," so the poor thief "lays hands on" God's name.

**Two small but telling forms.**

- Proverbs 19:1 and Proverbs 28:6 open identically: **ṭôb-rāš hôlēk bətummô mēʿiqqēš ...** ("better a poor man walking in his integrity than one perverse of ..."). Proverbs 19:1 ends "... of lips, and he is a fool." Proverbs 28:6 ends "... of two ways [*dərākayim*, dual], and he is rich." The dual "two ways" describes double-dealing. The variation tells us what the comparison is aimed at. In one version the foil is the fool, in the other the rich man.
- Proverbs 28:20 uses abstract plurals: **ʾîš ʾĕmûnôt rab-bərākôt** ("a man of faithfulnesses, abundant in blessings"). The trustworthy man is matched with the hasty one, **ʾāṣ ləhaʿăšîr** ("hastening to get rich"), who "will not go unpunished" (niphal of **nāqâ**, H5352, the same verdict passed on the mocker of the poor in Proverbs 17:5).

### 4. Discourse Structure

**How the topic is distributed across the book.** Each collection handles wealth in its own discourse mode:

| Collection | Dominant mode | How wealth is handled |
|:--|:--|:--|
| Prologue (Proverbs 1-9) | Second-person instruction with imperatives and motive clauses | Wealth as a duty to the LORD and to the neighbor (Proverbs 3:9-10, 27-28); surety as a trap to escape (Proverbs 6:1-5); riches as Wisdom's gift and as worth less than Wisdom (Proverbs 3:14-16; 8:10-21). The person-words for "poor" are almost absent (only the Ketiv at Proverbs 3:34) |
