| Solomon I (Proverbs 10:1-22:16) | Two-line indicative sentences | Observation (Proverbs 10:15; 14:20; 18:23; 19:4, 7; 22:7), consequence (Proverbs 11:24; 21:17; 22:16), relativizing comparison (Proverbs 15:16-17; 16:8, 19; 17:1; 19:1, 22; 22:1) and creation grounding (Proverbs 14:31; 17:5; 22:2) |
| Sayings of the wise (Proverbs 22:17-24:22) | Prohibitions with motive clauses | Do not rob the poor, move boundaries, stand surety or wear yourself out for riches (Proverbs 22:22-28; 23:4-5, 10-11) |
| Hezekiah (Proverbs 25-29) | Sentences, with a dense chapter 28 cluster | Proverbs 28:3, 6, 8, 11, 20, 22, 25, 27 put integrity, discernment and generosity against the rich and the hasty |
| Agur (Proverbs 30) | Prayer and numerical sayings | "Neither poverty nor riches" (Proverbs 30:7-9); the predatory generation (Proverbs 30:14); the insatiable "Enough!" (Proverbs 30:15-16) |
| Lemuel and the acrostic (Proverbs 31) | Royal instruction and alphabetic poem | Defend the poor and needy (Proverbs 31:9, a royal duty treated in "The King"); the valiant woman's trade and her open palm (Proverbs 31:16-20) |

**Testing the parallelism categories.** Distinctions.md sorts the sayings by the traditional "synonymous / antithetical / synthetic" scheme of Robert Lowth (1753). The wealth sayings show why James Kugel and Robert Alter found the scheme too coarse. In their account the second line typically sharpens, specifies or intensifies the first, rather than simply repeating or opposing it. Four examples:

- **Proverbs 10:15.** "The wealth of the rich man is his fortified city, but poverty is the ruin of the poor." This is formally antithetical (rich against poor, fortress against ruin), and the Literary Analysis lists it so, but both lines make the *same* kind of observation (possessions protect, lack destroys). The second line does not contradict the first. It completes a picture of social reality.
- **Proverbs 11:25.** "A generous soul will prosper, and he who refreshes others will himself be refreshed." This looks synonymous, but the second line moves from a general statement (the "soul of blessing" will be "made fat") to a specific and reflexive image (the one who waters *is himself* watered, **gam-hûʾ**). The line sharpens the first.
- **Proverbs 22:7.** "The rich rule over the poor, and the borrower is slave to the lender." The movement is from a class ("the rich," "the poor") to a contract ("borrower," "lender"), and from "rule" to "slave." Calling this "synonymous" misses the escalation.
- **Proverbs 14:31.** "Whoever oppresses the poor taunts their Maker, but whoever is kind to the needy honors Him." The second line reverses the order of the first. Line one runs subject, verb, object ("oppressor of the poor / has taunted / his Maker"). Line two puts the reference to God first: **ûməkabbədô ḥônēn ʾebyôn** ("and the one who honors Him [is] the one gracious to the needy"). The chiastic reversal (an A-B / B-A crossing) puts "the LORD" at the center:

```
A   oppressor of the poor   (ʿōšēq dāl)
 B    taunts his Maker      (ḥērēp ʿōśēhû)
 B'   honors Him            (ûməkabbədô)
A'  one gracious to the needy (ḥônēn ʾebyôn)
```

"Synthetic," the third category, turns out to be a catch-all for sayings that fit neither of the other two, such as Proverbs 22:2, whose second line *explains* the first. **Conclusion:** the Lowth labels can be used as a first sorting tool, but the wealth sayings work by intensification and juxtaposition, and that is where their teaching lies.

**Juxtaposition: sayings that comment on their neighbors.** The editors of Solomon I often placed a bare observation next to a saying that evaluates it. In these pairs the meaning lies in the sequence, not in either line alone:

- **Proverbs 14:20 → 14:21.** The observation that the poor man is hated even by his neighbor while the rich have many friends is followed at once by the verdict "He who despises his neighbor sins, but blessed is he who shows kindness to the poor" (Proverbs 14:21). The catchword **rēaʿ** ("neighbor") links the two. What 14:20 describes, 14:21 condemns.
- **Proverbs 18:10 → 18:11.** The two sayings share two key words. "The name of the LORD is a strong tower [**migdal-ʿōz**]; the righteous run to it and are safe [**wəniśgāb**]" is followed by "A rich man's wealth is his fortified city [**qiryat ʿuzzô**]; it is like a high wall [**ḥômâ niśgābâ**] in his imagination" (Proverbs 18:11). The repetition of **ʿōz** ("strength") and **śāgab** ("be high, safe") sets two fortresses side by side. One is real; the other exists only **bəmaśkîtô** ("in his imagination").
- **Proverbs 15:16 → 15:17** and **Proverbs 16:8 within 16:1-11.** The two "better than" sayings of chapter 15 form a pair (fear of the LORD, then love). Proverbs 16:8 sits inside a run of sayings that name the LORD (Proverbs 16:1-7, 9, 11), and the run closes with the scales that are "the LORD's" (Proverbs 16:11).
- **Proverbs 11:24-26.** Three sayings on giving and withholding are linked by sound and sense: **məpazzēr / ḥôśēk** (scatter / withhold), **bərākâ** in both Proverbs 11:25 and 11:26, and **mōnēaʿ bār / mašbîr** (withholder of grain / seller).

**The close of Solomon I: a wealth frame.** **ʿāšîr** ("rich") appears three times in the last sixteen verses of Solomon I (Proverbs 22:2, 7, 16). The collection's final verse, Proverbs 22:16, ends with **ləmaḥsôr** ("to want"). The first saying in the collection to use **ʿāšîr**, Proverbs 10:15, also pairs it with **dallîm**. **Inference (tentative):** the rich/poor pairing may serve as a loose frame around the Solomonic collection. It is a pattern worth noting, not a proven editorial design.

**Variant repetitions.** Proverbs often repeats a saying with small changes; Daniel Snell's *Twice-Told Proverbs* (1993) catalogs the phenomenon. The wealth sayings contain an unusual number of these pairs. Their differences show what each version is meant to stress:

| First | Second | What is shared | What changes |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| Proverbs 10:15 | Proverbs 18:11 | Identical first line ("The wealth of the rich man is his fortified city") | 10:15 adds the poor man's ruin (observation); 18:11 adds "in his imagination" (evaluation) |
| Proverbs 19:1 | Proverbs 28:6 | Literally "better a poor man walking in his integrity than ..." | 19:1's foil is the fool of crooked lips; 28:6's is the rich man of "two ways" |
| Proverbs 14:31 | Proverbs 17:5 | "taunts his Maker" | Oppressing (14:31) versus mocking and gloating (17:5) |
| Proverbs 22:2 | Proverbs 29:13 | "X and Y meet; the LORD ..." | Rich and poor / poor and oppressor; "Maker of them all" / "gives light to the eyes of both" |
| Proverbs 20:16 | Proverbs 27:13 | Take the garment of one who stood surety for a stranger | Nearly verbatim (minor Ketiv/Qere) |
| Proverbs 11:1 | Proverbs 20:10, 23 | "abomination to the LORD" with false weights and scales | 11:1 contrasts false scales with the honest "whole stone"; 20:10 adds false measures (ephah); 20:23 repeats the charge within the same chapter |

The pairing of Proverbs 10:15 with 18:11 is the most important for the retribution question. **Observation:** the book first states, without comment, that wealth protects. It later repeats the same line word for word and adds the qualification that the protection is imagined. **Inference:** the editors appear to have built the qualification of the retribution principle into the structure of the book, so that a reader who meets the observation later meets its correction. The correction qualifies the observation rather than denying it: wealth does protect, but not as the rich man imagines.

**The "better than" sayings: where they actually are.** A scan of the Hebrew for **ṭôb** followed by a comparative **min** finds about 21 "better than" sayings (Proverbs 3:14; 8:11, 19; 12:9; 15:16, 17; 16:8, 16, 19, 32; 17:1; 19:1, 22; 21:9, 19; 22:1; 25:7, 24; 27:5, 10; 28:6). Eleven of them fall in chapters 15-21 and thirteen in Solomon I overall. Only four fall in chapters 25-27, plus Proverbs 28:6. **Observation:** this corrects the aside in Distinctions.md that comparative sayings "become more common in chapters 25–27." What does cluster in chapters 25-26 is the *simile* ("like ... so ..."), not the "better than" form. The wealth-related "better than" sayings are Proverbs 12:9; 15:16, 17; 16:8, 19; 17:1; 19:1, 22; 22:1; 28:6. Proverbs 3:14; 8:11, 19; 16:16 rank wisdom above gold. These sayings provide the book's main formal counterweight to a simple "wealth equals blessing" reading.

**Two chiastic structures worth seeing.**

Agur's prayer (Proverbs 30:8-9) names the two conditions and then takes up their dangers in reverse order:

```
A   poverty      (rēʾš)                           30:8
 B   riches      (wāʿōšer)                        30:8
     -- feed me the bread of my portion --        30:8
 B'  lest I be full and deny You ...              30:9
A'  lest I be poor and steal ...                  30:9
```

At the center stands the request for "the bread that is my portion" (BSB), framed by the twin dangers.

The acrostic poem (Proverbs 31:10-31) gives each verse in turn the next letter of the alphabet. The two verses on the woman's hands fall on the letters *yod* (Proverbs 31:19) and *kaph* (Proverbs 31:20). These letter names resemble the words **yād** ("hand") and **kap** ("palm"), and each verse opens with the matching word. The two verses also cross:

```
31:19  her HANDS (yādêhā) to the distaff  --  her PALMS (kappêhā) grasp the spindle
31:20  her PALM (kappāh) opened to the poor  --  her HANDS (yādêhā) reached to the needy
```

**Observation:** the hands that work are the same hands that give, and the chiasm makes the move from industry to generosity a single motion. The full portrait belongs to "Wisdom, Folly, and the Valiant Woman." Here it is enough to note that the book's final picture of wealth ends in an open palm.

### 5. Etymology of Key Terms

Etymology can show where a word came from. It does not determine what the word means in Proverbs, which is established by usage (see §2). The derivations below are the standard ones in the lexicons. Where they are uncertain, that is said.

| Term | Root and origin | Development and notes |
|:--|:--|:--|
| **ʿāšar / ʿōšer / ʿāšîr** | Root ע־שׁ־ר, "be rich" | Not related to **ʿeśer** ("ten") or **maʿăśēr** ("tithe"), which are spelled with *sin* (שׂ), not *shin* (שׁ). The popular link between "riches" and "tithe" has no basis in the Hebrew |
| **hôn** | Usually connected with הון, "be easy, sufficient" (compare the hapax hiphil **wattāhînû**, "you made light of it," Deuteronomy 1:41, H1951) | The sense "sufficiency" survives in Proverbs 30:15-16, where **hôn** means "Enough!" The connection is a lexicographers' proposal, not a certainty |
| **rûš / rāš / rêš** | Root ר־ושׁ, "be poor" | Sometimes spelled with a silent *aleph*: **rāʾš** (Proverbs 10:4), **rāʾšîm** (Proverbs 13:23), **rēʾš** (Proverbs 6:11; 30:8). In consonants alone, ראשׁ looks exactly like **rōʾš** ("head, chief"). That resemblance lies behind the "ruler" readings of Proverbs 28:3 (see §7) |
| **dal** | Root ד־ל־ל, "hang down, be low, thin" | The verb describes the dangling legs of the lame (Proverbs 26:7). The related **dallâ** (H1803) means both "loose threads" (Isaiah 38:12) and "the poorest of the land" (2 Kings 24:14). **dal** pictures social weakness: one who is "low" and without leverage |
| **ʾebyôn** | Usually derived from ʾ־ב־ה, "be willing, want," that is, "one in want"; other derivations have been proposed and remain debated | In the Psalms and Deuteronomy it is the classic word for the needy brother whom Torah protects (Deuteronomy 15:7-11) |
| **ʿānî / ʿānāw** | Root ע־נ־ה III, "be bowed down, afflicted" | **ʿānî** leans toward "afflicted, oppressed"; **ʿānāw** toward "humble." Since *yod* (י) and *waw* (ו) are easily confused in square script, the two alternate as Ketiv and Qere (Proverbs 3:34; 14:21; 16:19). The related **ʿănāwâ** ("humility," H6038) occurs three times in Proverbs (Proverbs 15:33; 18:12; 22:4) out of four in the Hebrew Bible |
| **maḥsôr / ḥeser** | Root ח־ס־ר, "lack" | The same root as the verb of Psalm 23:1 (**lōʾ ʾeḥsār**, literally "I will not lack") and as **ḥăsar-lēb** ("lacking sense," Proverbs 17:18, of the rash guarantor). Hebrew readers may have heard that the one who lacks sense is on the way to lacking bread (an inference from the shared root, not a play the text signals) |
| **ʿārab** | Root ע־ר־ב I, "stand surety, exchange" | Its noun **ʿērābôn** ("pledge," Genesis 38:17-20, H6162) passed, probably through Phoenician trade, into Greek as **ἀρραβών** (*arrabōn*, "down payment, first installment"). Paul uses it of the Spirit (2 Corinthians 1:22; 5:5; Ephesians 1:14). The cautionary word of Proverbs 6:1 thus has a cousin that became a word of assurance |
| **tāqaʿ kap** | "Strike / clap the palm" | A gesture that sealed an agreement. The handshake made the obligation binding; that is why Proverbs 6:2 can describe the guarantor as trapped by his own spoken words |
| **nešek** | From **nāšak**, "to bite" (of a serpent) | Interest is literally "a bite" taken out of the borrower |
| **tarbît / marbît** | From **rābâ**, "be many, increase" | Interest as "increase." Proverbs 28:8 opens with the participle **marbeh** ("one who increases") from the same root: the man who "increases" his wealth by "increase" |
| **mōʾzənayim** | Root א־ז־ן II, "weigh"; dual ending -ayim | A dual: the two pans of a balance. The weights were **ʾabnê kîs** ("stones of the bag," Proverbs 16:11) |
| **tôʿēbâ** | Root ת־ע־ב, "abhor" | Cultic and moral revulsion. Proverbs uses it 22 times, second only to Ezekiel, and three times for false weights (Proverbs 11:1; 20:10, 23) |
| **ʿeṣeb** | Root ע־צ־ב, "hurt, grieve" | Pain and painful labor. Genesis 3:16 uses **ʿeṣeb** and **ʿiṣṣābôn** (H6093) for the pain of childbirth and the toil of the ground (Genesis 3:17). This background matters for Proverbs 10:22 (see §6) |
| **maśkît** | From **śākâ**, "look, gaze" | Something looked at: a carved figure (Leviticus 26:1; Numbers 33:52; Ezekiel 8:12), a "setting" of silver (Proverbs 25:11), and the "imaginations" of the heart (Psalm 73:7). In Proverbs 18:11 the rich man's wall stands in his **maśkît**. The word carries a faint overtone of a self-made image |
| **gōʾēl** | Root ג־א־ל, "redeem, act as kin" | The relative who buys back land or persons (Leviticus 25:25; Ruth 3-4). Its only use in Proverbs makes the LORD the orphan's kinsman (Proverbs 23:11) |
| **ṭôb-ʿayin / raʿ ʿāyin** | Idioms built on **ʿayin** ("eye") | The eye as the seat of disposition toward others. The idiom continues in Greek as **ὀφθαλμὸς πονηρός** (*ophthalmos ponēros*, "evil eye," Matthew 6:23; 20:15), where it again means a grudging spirit. LXX Proverbs 23:6 renders **raʿ ʿāyin** by **βάσκανος** (*baskanos*, "envious, grudging") |

### 6. Translation Decisions

**Proverbs 10:22b: "sorrow" or "toil"?**

| Version | Rendering | Notes |
|:--|:--|:--|
| BSB | "and He adds no sorrow to it" | **ʿeṣeb** as grief; the LORD is the subject of "adds" |
| ESV / NASB / NRSV | "he adds no sorrow with it" / "to it" | Same reading |
| NIV | "without painful toil for it" | **ʿeṣeb** as labor; no explicit subject |
| CSB | "he adds no painful effort to it" | Combines pain and labor |
| JPS 1917 | "And toil addeth nothing thereto" | **ʿeṣeb** as *subject*: human toil adds nothing |
| LXX (Brenton) | "the blessing of the Lord is upon the head of the righteous ... grief of heart shall not be added to it" | Adds "the righteous" (see §7) |

The verb **yôsîp** (hiphil, "add") is masculine. It can have the LORD as its subject ("He adds no sorrow"), or **ʿeṣeb** itself ("toil adds nothing"). On the first reading, the LORD's blessing comes without the grief that wealth gained in other ways brings (compare Proverbs 10:2; 15:16; 21:6). On the second, human toil contributes nothing beyond what the blessing gives (compare Psalm 127:2). **Assessment:** both readings are grammatical, and both cut against a prosperity reading. The first denies that God's gifts come with anxiety; the second denies that striving can produce what only blessing gives. In view of the background in Genesis 3:16-17, the word probably carries both nuances.

**Proverbs 13:23: who sweeps the harvest away?**

| Version | Rendering | Notes |
|:--|:--|:--|
| BSB | "Abundant food is in the fallow ground of the poor, but without justice it is swept away." | **bəlōʾ mišpāṭ** as "without justice" |
| ESV | "The fallow ground of the poor would yield much food, but it is swept away through injustice." | Adds a conditional ("would yield") |
| NIV | "An unplowed field produces food for the poor, but injustice sweeps it away." | Makes injustice the subject |
| KJV | "but there is that is destroyed for want of judgment." | **mišpāṭ** as "judgment" = good management |
| JPS 1917 | "But there is that is swept away by want of righteousness." | Moral failing, unspecified |
| LXX (Brenton) | "The righteous shall spend many years in wealth: but the unrighteous shall perish suddenly." | A different saying altogether (see §7) |

The decision turns on **mišpāṭ** (H4941). If it means "justice," others' injustice destroys the poor man's crop. If it means "right judgment, proper order," the cause may be the poor man's own mismanagement, which is how the KJV tradition could be read. In Proverbs **mišpāṭ** usually means justice, as in "justice" in the gate or in the king's judgment (for example Proverbs 16:8, 11; 17:23; 29:4). The modern consensus (BSB, ESV, NIV, NASB, NET) is therefore well grounded. **Assessment:** the verse is the clearest case in the book of poverty caused by *injustice*, not vice.

**Proverbs 22:16: who comes to want?**

| Version | Rendering | Notes |
|:--|:--|:--|
| BSB | "Oppressing the poor to enrich oneself or giving gifts to the rich will surely lead to poverty." | **lô** = the oppressor; both acts end in want |
| ESV / NASB | "Whoever oppresses the poor to increase his own wealth, or gives to the rich, will only come to poverty." | Same |
| NIV / NET / CSB | "both come to poverty" | Makes the "both" explicit |
| JPS 1917 | "One may oppress the poor, yet will their gain increase; One may give to the rich, yet will want come." | **lô** = the poor; oppression (ironically) enriches the oppressed, while gifts to the rich bring want |
| LXX (Brenton) | "He that oppresses the poor, increases his own substance, yet gives to the rich so as to make it less." | A third construal |

**Assessment:** the majority reading (both acts lead to want) is the most natural, and it fits Proverbs 28:8, where exploitative wealth ends up with "one who is kind to the poor." The JPS construal shows that the Hebrew can also be read as a providential reversal, but it requires taking **lô** against its nearest antecedent. On either reading the saying belongs with the qualifications of the simple scheme: wealth gained by oppression does not keep.

**Proverbs 28:3: a poor man or a ruler?**

| Version | Rendering | Notes |
|:--|:--|:--|
| ESV / KJV / NASB / NET / JPS 1917 | "A poor man who oppresses the poor ..." | MT **geber rāš** as written |
| NIV / NRSV | "A ruler who oppresses the poor ..." | Emends **rāš** to **rōʾš** ("head") or **rāšāʿ** ("wicked") |
| BSB / CSB | "A destitute leader who oppresses the poor ..." | Combines both senses |

See §7 for the textual question. **Assessment:** the Masoretic Text makes sense as it stands. A poor man who preys on the poor is like a flooding rain that destroys the crops it should have watered. The image is all the more bitter because the oppressor is himself poor. The emendation produces a smoother but more conventional saying and moves the verse into royal justice, which "The King" covers.

**The "eye" idioms (Proverbs 22:9; 28:22).**

| Version | Proverbs 22:9 | Proverbs 28:22 |
|:--|:--|:--|
| BSB | "A generous man" | "A stingy man" |
| KJV / ESV | "He that hath a bountiful eye" / "Whoever has a bountiful eye" | "He that hasteth to be rich hath an evil eye" / "A stingy man" |
| NASB | "One who is generous" | "A person with an evil eye" |
| Young's Literal | "The good of eye" | "the man with an evil eye" |
| LXX (Brenton) | "He that has pity on the poor" | "An envious man" |

**Assessment:** "generous / stingy" correctly conveys the meaning. Keeping "eye" (as the KJV, ESV in 22:9, and NASB in 28:22 do) preserves the link to Deuteronomy 15:9 and to the Gospels' "evil eye" (Matthew 6:23; 20:15). Readers of a functional translation lose that link and should be told about it.

**Proverbs 11:24: "what is right" or "more than is right"?**

| Version | Rendering | Notes |
|:--|:--|:--|
| BSB / CSB | "another withholds what is right" | **min** as "from," that is, holding back from what is upright |
| ESV / NRSV | "another withholds what he should give" / "what is due" | Interpretive: the poor's due |
| KJV / NET | "withholdeth more than is meet" / "more than he should" | **min** as comparative |
| Young's Literal | "keeping back from uprightness" | Literal |

The phrase is **ḥôśēk miyyōšer** (Hebrew: חוֹשֵׂךְ מִיֹּשֶׁר). **Assessment:** whether **min** is privative ("from what is right") or comparative ("beyond what is right"), the point is the same. Proverbs does not condemn prudence or saving (see Proverbs 21:20). It condemns withholding what *ought* to be given, which matches Proverbs 3:27 ("good" owed "to its owners").

**Proverbs 3:27: whose "good" is it?**

| Version | Rendering | Notes |
|:--|:--|:--|
| BSB | "Do not withhold good from the deserving" | **baʿal** as "the one who deserves it" |
| ESV / NASB / KJV | "from those to whom it is due" | Same |
| Young's Literal / NAB | "from its owners" / "from the owner" | Literal: **mibbəʿālāyw**, "from its owners" |
| NET | "from those who need it" | Shifts from right to need |
| LXX (Brenton) | "Forbear not to do good to the poor" | Names the poor explicitly |

**Assessment:** the Hebrew pictures the good as already *belonging* to the one who needs it. Withholding it is thus close to keeping what belongs to someone else. NET's "those who need it" captures the context but loses that juridical edge.

**Proverbs 19:17: who is the lender?**

| Version | Rendering | Notes |
|:--|:--|:--|
| BSB | "Kindness to the poor is a loan to the LORD, and He will repay the lender." | Nominalizes the participles |
| ESV / NASB | "Whoever is generous to the poor lends to the LORD, and he will repay him for his deed." | **gəmûlô** = the giver's deed |
| NRSV | "and will be repaid in full" | Passive; agent left implicit |
| Young's Literal | "Whoso is lending to Jehovah is favouring the poor" | Reverses subject and predicate |

**Assessment:** the standard construal is right. Young's ordering shows the boldness of the construct **malwēh YHWH** (§3). Translations that keep "the LORD" in the first line keep the surprise.

**Proverbs 30:8: "my portion" or "daily bread"?**

| Version | Rendering | Notes |
|:--|:--|:--|
| BSB / NASB | "feed me with the bread that is my portion" / "the food that is my portion" | **ḥuqqî** as allotment |
| JPS 1917 / NET | "mine allotted bread" / "my allotted portion of bread" | Same |
| ESV / CSB | "the food that is needful for me" / "the food I need" | Need rather than allotment |
| NIV | "give me only my daily bread" | Echoes Matthew 6:11 |

**Assessment:** "portion, allotment" is the lexical sense of **ḥōq**. It implies a measure set by God, not only a minimum of need. The NIV's "daily bread" makes a fair canonical connection but brings it into the translation itself.

### 7. Textual Variants

The Masoretic Text (MT) of the wealth sayings is generally stable. The variants fall into three groups: Ketiv/Qere differences within the Hebrew tradition, readings where the LXX differs, and modern conjectural emendations.

| Reference | MT | Variant | Effect on teaching |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| Proverbs 3:27 | Ketiv **yādêkā** ("your hands") | Qere **yādəkā** ("your hand") | None |
| Proverbs 3:28 | Ketiv **ləreʿêkā** ("to your neighbors") | Qere **ləreʿăkā** ("to your neighbor") | None |
| Proverbs 3:9 | "from your wealth [**hôn**]" | LXX "from your just labors ... fruits of righteousness" | LXX makes explicit that only righteous wealth honors God; the teaching is sharpened, not changed |
| Proverbs 10:15b | "the ruin of the poor [**dallîm**] is their poverty" | LXX "poverty is the ruin of the ungodly" (*asebōn*) | LXX turns an observation about the poor into a moral verdict on the wicked; significant for the retribution question (see below) |
| Proverbs 10:22a | "the blessing of the LORD makes rich" | LXX adds "upon the head of the righteous" | LXX narrows the blessing to the righteous; a moralizing gloss |
| Proverbs 11:25 | "a soul of blessing will be made fat" | LXX "every sincere [*haplē*] soul is blessed," with a different second line | LXX departs from the Hebrew; MT preferred |
| Proverbs 13:23 | the poor man's fallow ground yields food, swept away "without justice" | LXX "the righteous will spend many years in wealth, but the unrighteous will perish suddenly" | **Significant.** LXX removes the only clear statement that injustice causes poverty and replaces it with a plain retribution saying. MT is the harder reading and is preferred |
| Proverbs 14:21; 16:19 | Ketiv **ʿăniyyîm** ("afflicted, poor") | Qere **ʿănāwîm** ("humble") | Slight: economic and spiritual lowliness overlap in Proverbs 16:19 either way |
| Proverbs 14:24 | "the crown of the wise is their riches [**ʿošrām**]" | LXX "a prudent man is the crown of the wise"; some propose **ʿormâ** ("shrewdness"), and NRSV reads "their wisdom" | If emended, the verse no longer promises wealth to the wise; the MT is defensible and fits Proverbs 3:16; 8:18; 22:4 |
| Proverbs 19:7c | Ketiv **lōʾ** ("not") / Qere **lô** ("to him") in a fragmentary third line | LXX has a longer, different couplet | A line may have been lost; the first two lines (the poor man abandoned) are secure |
| Proverbs 19:22a | **taʾăwat ʾādām ḥasdô** | Read as **ḥesed** I "loyal love" (most versions) or **ḥesed** II "shame" (JPS 1917: "The lust of a man is his shame"); LXX "a poor man is better than a *rich* liar" | Affects line a; line b ("better to be poor than a liar") is secure |
| Proverbs 20:21 | Ketiv **məbuḥelet** (H973, meaning disputed) | Qere **məbōhelet** (H926, "hastened"), followed by BSB ("gained quickly") | Either way an early or ill-gotten inheritance is "not blessed in the end"; no change |
| Proverbs 21:6b | **məbaqšê-māwet** ("seekers of death") | LXX and some Hebrew manuscripts reflect **môqšê-māwet** ("snares of death"), followed by ESV / NRSV | The image changes (pursuit versus trap); the teaching does not |
| Proverbs 22:8a | (no Hebrew) | LXX plus: "God blesses a cheerful and generous man" | Not part of the Hebrew canon; it appears to be echoed in 2 Corinthians 9:7, which shares the words *hilaros* and *dotēs*. Of interest for the history of the generosity theme, not for the MT's teaching |
| Proverbs 22:20 | Ketiv **šilšôm** ("formerly") | Qere **šālîšîm** (often repointed **šəlōšîm**, "thirty [sayings]") | Bears on the Amenemope comparison for 22:17-24:22, not on the wealth teaching itself |
| Proverbs 23:5 | Ketiv qal **hătāʿûp** / ועיף | Qere hiphil **hătāʿîp** / **yāʿûp** | None: riches fly either way |
| Proverbs 23:10 | **gəbûl ʿôlām** ("ancient boundary") | Conjecture **gəbûl ʾalmānâ** ("widow's boundary"), by analogy with Proverbs 15:25 | Unsupported by manuscripts; MT matches Proverbs 22:28. The protection of the vulnerable is already explicit in "the fields of the fatherless" |
| Proverbs 28:3 | **geber rāš** ("a poor man") | Emendation to **rōʾš** ("head, ruler") or **rāšāʿ** ("wicked man"), followed by NIV / NRSV; BSB / CSB "destitute leader" combine both | Changes *who* oppresses, not *that* oppression is condemned. MT is intelligible and preferred (see §6) |
| Proverbs 28:8 | Ketiv **ûbətarbît** ("and by increase") | Qere **wətarbît** ("and increase") | None |
| Proverbs 28:22b | **ḥeser** ("want") will come upon him | LXX "the merciful man [*eleēmōn*] will master him," reading **ḥesed** (*dalet*) for **ḥeser** (*resh*) | A classic confusion of two similar Hebrew letters; MT fits the context (haste after wealth ends in want) |

**A pattern in the Greek.** Several of the LXX departures move in the same direction. In Proverbs 10:15 the poor become "the ungodly." In Proverbs 10:22 the blessing is restricted to "the righteous." In Proverbs 13:23 an observation about injustice becomes a plain promise that the righteous will prosper. In Proverbs 3:9 the wealth offered to God is specified as "just" labor. **Observation:** in these places the Greek translator made the moral categories explicit, and in two of them (Proverbs 10:15; 13:23) this aligned poverty with wickedness more closely than the Hebrew does. **Inference:** the Hebrew text preserves the tension between the retribution principle and its qualifications more fully than its earliest translation does. The LXX is valuable evidence of how the sayings were being *read* in the Greek-speaking world. It is not evidence that the Hebrew originally taught a simpler scheme. In Proverbs 13:23 especially, the MT is the harder and more original reading.

**Do any variants change the teaching?** No variant overturns what Proverbs teaches about wealth, poverty and generosity. The variants that matter most each affect one *strand* of the topic:

- **Proverbs 13:23**: whether the book explicitly names injustice as a cause of poverty. The MT, which is preferred, does. The LXX does not.
- **Proverbs 22:16**: which party ends in want. Both main construals still teach that exploitative gain does not last.
- **Proverbs 28:3**: whether the oppressor is a poor man or a ruler. Oppression of the poor is condemned on every reading.
- **Proverbs 14:24**: whether riches are the "crown" of the wise. The MT reading is consistent with Wisdom's gifts in Proverbs 3:16; 8:18; 22:4.

On the preferred readings, the full shape of the teaching stands. Wealth is a real good and can be a gift of the LORD's blessing (Proverbs 10:22). It is also relativized, made transient, and judged by how it was gained and how it is shared (Proverbs 11:24-28; 13:23; 16:8; 18:11; 23:4-5). The poor share a Maker and an Advocate with the rich (Proverbs 14:31; 22:2, 22-23; 23:10-11).

---

## Literary Analysis

A note on method before the analysis. Quotations follow the Berean Standard Bible (BSB), checked against Bible Hub's BSB chapter pages. Hebrew observations rest on the Masoretic Text (the *Miqra according to the Masorah* edition, via Sefaria). Where the text has a Ketiv/Qere variant, the Qere (the reading the Masoretes vocalized) is followed unless noted. Occurrence counts come either from a consonantal search of that text or from the Strong's tagging of Bible Hub's interlinear. They are given as observations, and their weight is assessed where they are used. Throughout, **observation** marks what is on the page, **inference** marks a reading of it, and **tradition** marks a scholarly or interpretive proposal.

### 1. Genre and Sub-Genres

**Primary genre: wisdom literature, specifically the *māšāl*.** Everything Proverbs says about money belongs to its governing form, the **māšāl** (Hebrew: מָשָׁל, *māšāl*, H4912). A *māšāl* is a crafted, compressed saying that teaches by comparison, contrast or portrait. Wealth and poverty stand out among the book's themes in one literary respect (observation): the topic appears in almost every sub-genre the book uses. The sages did not file money under one form. They came at it as instruction, observation, paradox, comparison, prayer, self-praise of Wisdom, and praise poem. Each form makes a different kind of claim, so the first interpretive task is to notice which form one is reading.

| Sub-genre | Wealth examples | What the form does |
|---|---|---|
| **Instruction (admonition with direct address)** | Proverbs 3:9-10, 27-28; 6:1-5; 22:22-23, 26-27; 23:4-5, 10-11 | Imperatives and prohibitions, often with a "for" (*kî*) motive clause. It calls for an immediate decision: "Do not withhold good from the deserving" (Proverbs 3:27) |
| **Antithetical sentence proverb** | Proverbs 10:2, 15-16; 11:1, 4, 24, 26, 28; 13:7-8, 11; 14:20-21, 31; 15:6; 28:20, 27 | Sets two people or outcomes side by side and invites the hearer to place himself in one column |
| **"Better than" saying (*ṭôb ... min*)** | Proverbs 15:16-17; 16:8, 19; 17:1; 19:1, 22; 22:1; 28:6; cf. 12:9 | Ranks two goods or two combinations. By its form it concedes that the worse combination (wealth with injustice) really exists |
| **Wisdom-over-gold comparison** | Proverbs 3:14-15; 8:10-11, 19; 16:16; 20:15 | A sub-type of the "better than" saying that sets the value of wisdom against the standard measures of value |
| **Paradox ("there is one who ...", *yēš*)** | Proverbs 11:24; 13:7; cf. 13:23 | Opens with the existential particle **yēš** (Hebrew: יֵשׁ, *yēš*, H3426, "there is"). It reports a case that seems to defy expectation and makes the hearer stop |
| **Observational (descriptive) saying** | Proverbs 10:15; 14:20; 18:16, 23; 19:4, 6-7; 22:7; on bribes 17:8; 21:14 | States how things go, without approval. "The rich rule over the poor" (Proverbs 22:7) is a report, not a commandment |
| **YHWH saying (theological aphorism)** | Proverbs 10:22; 11:1; 14:31; 16:11; 17:5; 19:17; 20:10, 23; 22:2; 29:13 | Brings the LORD's evaluation, ownership or action into a commercial or social scene |
| **Blessing and curse formula** | Proverbs 11:26; 22:9; 28:20, 27 | Describes the social and divine response to generosity or hoarding in the vocabulary of blessing (*bərākâ*) and curse |
| **Speech of personified Wisdom** | Proverbs 3:16; 8:18-21 | Wisdom speaks of riches as gifts in her keeping: "With me are riches and honor, enduring wealth and righteousness" (Proverbs 8:18) |
| **Speech of the tempters** | Proverbs 1:13-14 | The gang's pitch, quoted so the son can hear the voice of easy money before he meets it |
| **Prayer** | Proverbs 30:7-9 | The only prayer in the book. It is introduced as a small numerical saying ("Two things I ask of You", Proverbs 30:7) and gives a first-person synthesis on riches and poverty |
| **Graded numerical saying** | Proverbs 30:15-16 | "Three ... four" sayings about insatiability, ending on **hôn** used in its sense "Enough!" |
| **Acrostic praise poem** | Proverbs 31:10-31, especially 31:11, 16, 20, 24 | Gives a portrait in place of a rule: wealth produced by skill and trade, with the palm opened to the poor |

**Testing the working notes on parallelism.** `Distinctions.md` uses the traditional three-way scheme of *synonymous*, *antithetical* and *synthetic* parallelism. The scheme goes back to Robert Lowth (*De sacra poesi Hebraeorum*, 1753). The wealth sayings confirm one part of that scheme and complicate another.

*What they confirm.* Antithesis does dominate Proverbs 10-15, and the wealth sayings there are almost all antithetical (Proverbs 10:2, 15-16; 11:1, 4, 24, 26, 28; 13:7-8, 11; 14:20-21, 31; 15:6).

*What they complicate.* James Kugel (*The Idea of Biblical Poetry*, 1981) and Robert Alter (*The Art of Biblical Poetry*, 1985) argued that the second line of a Hebrew couplet seldom just restates or just reverses the first. It typically sharpens, specifies or intensifies it, which is why "synthetic" works only as a catch-all. The wealth sayings show the point clearly:

- Proverbs 22:7, "The rich rule over the poor, and the borrower is slave to the lender." Line b is not a synonym of line a. It names the *mechanism* of the domination (debt) and hardens "rule" into "slave".
- Proverbs 18:11 repeats the first line of Proverbs 10:15 word for word ("A rich man's wealth is his fortified city"). It then adds a second line that exposes it: "it is like a high wall in his imagination." The second line does more than vary the first. It marks the limit of the protection Proverbs 10:15 reports: real as far as it goes, imagined when it is trusted as final security.
- Proverbs 14:31 moves from a prohibition-shaped case ("Whoever oppresses the poor taunts their Maker") to a positive one ("whoever is kind to the needy honors Him"). The contrast is not only between oppressing and helping. It is between insulting God and honoring him.
- Proverbs 17:5 widens its field: line a is about mocking the poor, and line b is about gloating over *any* calamity.
- Proverbs 11:24-25 builds a small ladder. "Gains even more" leads to "will prosper", which leads to "will himself be refreshed".

So "antithetical" is accurate as a label, but the interpretive payoff lies in *how* the second line moves.

**Testing the notes on "better than" sayings.** The notes say that comparative and "better than" sayings "become more common in chapters 25–27." For this topic the claim does not hold (observation). There are about twenty-one *ṭôb ... min* sayings in the book. They fall out as follows:

| Collection | "Better than" sayings | Of which about wealth |
|---|---|---|
| Prologue (1:1-9:18) | Proverbs 3:14; 8:11, 19 (3) | None in the narrow sense; all three are wisdom-over-gold comparisons (wisdom over silver, gold and corals) |
| Solomon I (10:1-22:16) | Proverbs 12:9; 15:16, 17; 16:8, 16, 19, 32; 17:1; 19:1, 22; 21:9, 19; 22:1 (13) | Proverbs 12:9; 15:16, 17; 16:8, 19; 17:1; 19:1, 22; 22:1 (9), plus the wisdom-over-gold comparison Proverbs 16:16 |
| Hezekiah (25:1-29:27) | Proverbs 25:7, 24; 27:5, 10; 28:6 (5) | Proverbs 28:6 only |

Here "about wealth" means a saying that weighs wealth or poverty against another good, which gives ten in all (the count used throughout this study). The four sayings that rank wisdom above precious metals (Proverbs 3:14; 8:11, 19; 16:16) are counted separately as wisdom-over-gold comparisons. Eleven of the thirteen in Solomon I sit in chapters 15-21. Six of them (Proverbs 15:16, 17; 16:8, 16, 19; 17:1) fall in the short stretch from Proverbs 15:16 to 17:1, which straddles the seam between chapters 15 and 16. That seam is where commentators commonly place the shift from the antithesis-heavy sub-collection (Proverbs 10-15) to the more varied one (Proverbs 16:1-22:16). What does cluster in chapters 25-26 is the *simile* ("like ... so ..."). The working notes appear to have merged the two kinds of comparison.

**What the notes omit.** `Distinctions.md` has no category for the alphabetic acrostic of Proverbs 31:10-31. For this topic that omission matters. The acrostic is the book's closing picture of wealth rightly gained ("She appraises a field and buys it", Proverbs 31:16) and rightly shared ("She opens her arms to the poor and reaches out her hands to the needy", Proverbs 31:20). Its full treatment belongs to "Wisdom, Folly, and the Valiant Woman".

**Rules of interpretation these genres call for.**

1. **A sentence proverb states a typical outcome, not a guarantee.** "One gives freely, yet gains even more" (Proverbs 11:24) describes how things tend to go in a world the LORD orders. It is not a contract that pays out on demand. Reading the saying as a promise of return on investment turns a māšāl into a vending machine.
2. **Observation is not commendation.** Proverbs 10:15, 14:20, 19:4 and 22:7 report social facts: wealth protects, wealth attracts friends, the poor are shunned, debt enslaves. They no more approve those facts than Proverbs 17:8 approves bribery when it calls a bribe "a charm to its giver". The Hebrew behind "to its giver" is literally "in the eyes of its owner" (*bəʿênê bəʿālāyw*). That qualifying phrase in Proverbs 17:8, and the parallel phrase "in his imagination" in Proverbs 18:11, mark these as views *from inside* a perspective the sage does not share (inference).
3. **Sayings are meant to be read in conversation with one another.** The book builds its own qualifications into the collection. Proverbs 18:11 answers Proverbs 10:15. Proverbs 13:23 (the poor man's harvest "swept away" by injustice) stands against any reading of poverty as always self-inflicted. The deliberately paired and opposite sayings of Proverbs 26:4-5 show that the sages expected proverbs to be *fitted* to cases, not applied mechanically. The retribution sayings (Proverbs 3:9-10; 10:22; 22:4) are therefore to be read alongside, not instead of, the sayings that qualify them.
4. **The "better than" form qualifies the retribution principle by its very grammar.** To say "Better a little with righteousness than great gain with injustice" (Proverbs 16:8) is to admit that righteousness sometimes comes with little and injustice sometimes comes with much. Each "better than" saying on wealth (Proverbs 15:16-17; 16:8, 19; 17:1; 19:1; 28:6) presupposes a world in which the act-consequence link is not immediate (observation about the form; its theological weight belongs to the Theological Analysis).
5. **Watch the time horizon and the agent.** Many consequence sayings stretch the frame: "the day of wrath" (Proverbs 11:4), the "end" of an inheritance (Proverbs 20:21), a "future" (**ʾaḥărît**, Hebrew: אַחֲרִית, *ʾaḥărît*, H319, Proverbs 23:18; 24:20), wealth passed on to grandchildren or to the righteous (Proverbs 13:22). Many also name the LORD as the one who acts: he "enriches" (Proverbs 10:22), "will repay" (Proverbs 19:17), "will take up their case" (Proverbs 22:23; 23:11). The outcome is described as personal and future, not automatic and immediate.
6. **Instruction and sentence work differently.** The instructions (Proverbs 6:1-5; 22:22-27; 23:4-5, 10-11) address "you", often "my son", and call for action now. The sentence proverbs invite slower reflection, and they are best read in the clusters where the editors placed them (section 2).
7. **Doublets are not filler.** Several wealth sayings occur twice, in identical or near-identical form (section 5). A repeated saying is shaped by its new neighbors, and the differences between the two forms are often the point.
8. **Prayer and portrait synthesize.** Agur's prayer (Proverbs 30:7-9) and the valiant woman's portrait (Proverbs 31:10-31) do not add new rules. They show the book's teaching on wealth held together in a single life, one as petition and one as example.

### 2. Literary Structure

**Distribution across the book (observation).** The research inventory for this study counts 133 wealth-related entries across the book (sayings or short units, some spanning several verses). They distribute by collection as follows:

| Collection | Entries | Dominant forms for this topic |
|---|---|---|
| Prologue (Proverbs 1:1-9:18) | 15 | Instruction; the speech of the gang; Wisdom's self-praise; wisdom-over-gold comparisons |
| Solomon I (Proverbs 10:1-22:16) | 76 | Antithesis (chapters 10-15); "better than" and YHWH sayings (chapters 15-22) |
| Sayings of the Wise (Proverbs 22:17-24:22) | 12 | Admonition with motive clause |
| Further Sayings (Proverbs 24:23-34) | 1 | Example story (the sluggard's field; defer to "The Sluggard") |
| Hezekiah (Proverbs 25:1-29:27) | 20 | Antithesis and doublet, densest in chapter 28 |
| Agur (Proverbs 30) | 3 | Prayer; numerical saying |
| Lemuel (Proverbs 31:1-9) | 2 | Royal instruction (defer to "The King") |
| Acrostic (Proverbs 31:10-31) | 4 | Praise poem |

The topic is concentrated in the two Solomonic sentence collections, which together hold nearly three-quarters of the entries. But it frames the whole book.

**The book-level frame: two ways to fill a house.** The first temptation the son meets in Proverbs is economic. The gang promises: "we will fill our houses with plunder" and "let us all share one purse" (Proverbs 1:13-14). The book's last portrait is of a woman whose husband "lacks nothing of value" (Proverbs 31:11) and whose hands are open to the needy (Proverbs 31:20). Between the two, Proverbs 24:3-4 describes a house that wisdom builds and knowledge fills "with every precious and beautiful treasure". Three verbal links bind these texts (observation):

- Proverbs 1:13 and Proverbs 24:4 share the phrase **kol-hôn yāqār** (Hebrew: כׇּל־הוֹן יָקָר, *kol-hôn yāqār*, "all precious wealth"), and both use the verb "fill" (*mālēʾ*) of a house. The gang fills houses by robbery. Wisdom fills them by knowledge.
- **šālāl** (Hebrew: שָׁלָל, *šālāl*, H7998, "plunder, spoil") is the gang's word in Proverbs 1:13. It recurs in Proverbs 16:19 ("than to divide the spoil with the proud") and in Proverbs 31:11, where it is the wife's honest "gain".
- **kîs** (Hebrew: כִּיס, *kîs*, H3599, "bag, purse") is the gang's "one purse" in Proverbs 1:14 and the merchant's "bag" of weights in Proverbs 16:11: "all the weights in the bag are His concern."

```
A   Proverbs 1:10-19   The gang: houses filled with plunder (šālāl),
                       one purse (kîs) for all; gain "takes the lives
                       of its possessors" (Proverbs 1:19)
  B   Proverbs 3:9-10, 13-16; 8:18-21
                       Wisdom's way: honor the LORD from your wealth;
                       barns filled; riches in Wisdom's left hand
    C   Proverbs 10:1-29:27   The sentence collections: rich and poor,
                       generosity, scales, surety, "better than";
                       within them Proverbs 24:3-4: wisdom fills the house
                       with "all precious wealth" (kol-hôn yāqār, as in
                       Proverbs 1:13)
  B'  Proverbs 30:7-9  Agur's prayer: neither poverty nor riches, only
                       "the bread that is my portion"
A'  Proverbs 31:10-31  The valiant woman: honest "gain" (šālāl, Proverbs 31:11),
                       fields bought, palm opened to the poor (Proverbs 31:20)
```

The diagram is an *inference* about editorial design. The verbal links are observations. The pairing of Wisdom's offer (B) with Agur's prayer (B') is the loosest element: they are linked by theme (wealth received from God and held rightly), not by shared vocabulary. Whether the final editors intended a deliberate frame cannot be proved, but the contrast between "unjust gain" that "takes the lives of its possessors" (Proverbs 1:19) and wealth that serves a household and the poor (Proverbs 31:11, 20) is plain on the page.

**Solomon I opens and closes with wealth.** After its title saying (Proverbs 10:1), the first Solomonic collection begins with four sayings about wealth and want: ill-gotten treasure (Proverbs 10:2), the LORD who does not let the righteous go hungry (Proverbs 10:3), and idle versus diligent hands (Proverbs 10:4-5, defer to "The Sluggard"). It ends (Proverbs 22:1-16) with the densest wealth cluster in the collection. That cluster contains a good name better than riches (Proverbs 22:1), rich and poor before their Maker (Proverbs 22:2), the rewards of humility (Proverbs 22:4), the rich ruling the poor (Proverbs 22:7), the "generous" (literally "good of eye") man (Proverbs 22:9), and the closing saying on oppressing the poor or giving to the rich (Proverbs 22:16). The collection is bracketed by money (observation).

Within Proverbs 22:1-16, the three occurrences of **ʿāšîr** (Hebrew: עָשִׁיר, *ʿāšîr*, H6223, "rich man") trace a progression (inference from their sequence):

```
Proverbs 22:2   rich and poor MEET before one Maker      (creation: equality)
      |
Proverbs 22:7   the rich RULE the poor; debt enslaves    (society: power)
      |
Proverbs 22:16  oppressing the poor / giving to the rich (judgment: want)
                "will surely lead to poverty"
```

The sequence moves from what is true before God, to what is true in society, to what the LORD's order does with those who exploit the gap.

**A concentric design in the Sayings of the Wise (Proverbs 22:22-23:11).** The opening sayings of the third collection are dominated by wealth. Their verbal repetitions suggest a ring:

```
A   Proverbs 22:22-23  Do not rob the poor; "the LORD will take up their
                       case" (yārîb rîbām)
  B   Proverbs 22:26-27  Surety: if you cannot pay, your bed is taken
    C   Proverbs 22:28     "Do not move an ancient boundary stone"
                           (ʾal-tassēg gəbûl ʿôlām)
      D   Proverbs 23:1-3    At a ruler's table: "do not crave his
                             delicacies" (ʾal-tittʾāw ləmaṭʿammōtāyw)
        E   Proverbs 23:4-5    CENTER: "Do not wear yourself out to get
                               rich" -- wealth flies off like an eagle
      D'  Proverbs 23:6-8    At a stingy man's table: "do not crave his
                             delicacies" (same Hebrew line)
    C'  Proverbs 23:10a    "Do not move an ancient boundary stone"
                           (same Hebrew line)
A'  Proverbs 23:10b-11 The orphans' fields; "their Redeemer is strong;
                       He will take up their case" (yārîb ʾet-rîbām)
```

The verbal matches are observations. Proverbs 22:28a and Proverbs 23:10a are identical in Hebrew. Proverbs 23:3a and Proverbs 23:6b are nearly identical (23:6b adds "and"). Proverbs 22:23 and Proverbs 23:11 share "take up their case" (*yārîb rîbām*). The ring itself is an inference, and it is not tidy. Proverbs 22:24-25 (anger), Proverbs 22:29 (the skilled worker) and Proverbs 23:9 (the fool) have no partners. Still, the design places the warning against exhausting oneself for riches (Proverbs 23:4-5) between two scenes of dangerous hospitality. It is framed by the LORD as advocate for the poor and the orphan. That gives the block a clear message: the pursuit of wealth sits inside a world where the LORD defends the weak. The ruler's table (Proverbs 23:1-3) belongs to "The King". The parallels with the Egyptian *Instruction of Amenemope* in this section belong to the Historical & Cultural Analysis.

**Juxtaposition: the true and the imagined fortress (Proverbs 18:10-11).**

```
Proverbs 18:10  "The name of the LORD is a strong tower (migdal-ʿōz);
                 the righteous run to it and are safe (niśgāb)."
Proverbs 18:11  "A rich man's wealth is his fortified city (qiryat ʿuzzô);
                 it is like a high wall (ḥômâ niśgābâ) in his imagination."
```

Two catchwords bind the pair (observation): **ʿōz** (Hebrew: עֹז, *ʿōz*, H5797, "strength") and the root **śgb** (Hebrew: שָׂגַב, *śāgab*, H7682, "be high, be safe"). The righteous are "safe" (*niśgāb*) in the LORD's name. The rich man's wall is "high" (*niśgābâ*) only in his **maśkît** (Hebrew: מַשְׂכִּית, *maśkît*, H4906, "imagination, image"). The first line of Proverbs 18:11 is word for word the first line of Proverbs 10:15. Placed after Proverbs 18:10, the same observation becomes an exposure. This is the clearest case in the book of a saying corrected by its neighbor (inference about editorial intent; the verbal facts are observation).

**Chapter 28: the densest wealth chapter.** Of the twenty-eight verses in Proverbs 28, about a dozen touch wealth and poverty: Proverbs 28:3, 6, 8, 11, 15-16, 19, 20, 21, 22, 24, 25, 27. The chapter opens its wealth material with a poor man (*rāš*) who oppresses the poor (Proverbs 28:3, MT; text crux, see the Linguistic Analysis). It closes it with the giver to the poor (*rāš*) who "will not be in need" (Proverbs 28:27). Two phrases with **rab** ("many") set the end of the chapter in counterpoint (observation): the faithful man "will abound with blessings" (*rab-bərākôt*, literally "many of blessings", Proverbs 28:20), and the one who hides his eyes from the poor receives "many curses" (*rab-məʾērôt*, Proverbs 28:27).

**Chiastic couplets.** Several individual sayings are built as small chiasms (observation from the Hebrew word order):

```
Proverbs 22:7
A   ʿāšîr          the rich
  B   bərāšîm        over the poor
    C   yimšôl         rules
    C'  wəʿebed        and slave (is)
  B'  lōweh          the borrower
A'  ləʾîš malweh   to the lender
```

```
Proverbs 14:31
A   ʿōšēq dāl       one who oppresses the poor
  B   ḥērēp ʿōśēhû    taunts his Maker
  B'  ûməkabbədô      but honoring Him (is)
A'  ḥōnēn ʾebyôn    one who is gracious to the needy
```

```
Proverbs 30:8-9 (Agur's prayer)
A   rēʾš            poverty
  B   wāʿōšer         and riches  -- "give me neither"
    C   "feed me with the bread that is my portion"
  B'  pen-ʾeśbaʿ      lest I be full (have too much) and deny You
A'  ûpen-ʾiwwārēš   or lest I become poor ... and steal
```

In Proverbs 30:8-9 the prayer names poverty first and riches second. It then answers them in reverse order, riches' danger first and poverty's second. At the center is the request for "the bread that is my portion" (*leḥem ḥuqqî*). The form enacts the content: the petitioner asks to be placed in the middle, and the prayer puts that request at its center (inference).

**The surety instruction (Proverbs 6:1-5) and the hand.** The five-verse instruction is structured around the hand and the palm (observation):

```
Proverbs 6:1   you struck your palms (kappêkā) for a stranger    -- the pledge
Proverbs 6:2   trapped ... ensnared by the words of your mouth   -- the trap
Proverbs 6:3   you have come into your neighbor's palm (bəkap)   -- the captivity
Proverbs 6:4   no sleep to your eyes                             -- the urgency
Proverbs 6:5   escape like a gazelle from the hand (miyyād),
               like a bird from the hand (miyyad) of the fowler  -- the escape
```

The palm that struck the bargain has delivered its owner into another man's palm. The verses that follow (Proverbs 6:6-11) move straight to the sluggard, and catchwords link the two units: no sleep for the guarantor's eyes (Proverbs 6:4), but "a little sleep" for the sluggard (Proverbs 6:10), whose end is poverty (Proverbs 6:11). Two kinds of folly lead to the same ruin, one rash and one idle. The sluggard material belongs to "The Sluggard".

### 3. Voice, Mood, and Style

**Voice.** The wealth material is unusually polyphonic for Proverbs. Several voices speak, and the reader is meant to tell them apart:

| Voice | Where | Function |
|---|---|---|
| The father-teacher ("my son") | Proverbs 3:9-10, 27-28; 6:1-5; 22:22-27; 23:4-5, 10-11 | Second-person command with motive clause; urgent and personal |
| The sage-observer | Most of Proverbs 10-29 (e.g., Proverbs 10:15; 14:20; 18:23; 22:7) | Third-person report of how the world works, often without comment |
| Lady Wisdom | Proverbs 3:16 (described); 8:18-21 (first person) | Wealth spoken of as hers to give: "With me are riches and honor" (Proverbs 8:18) |
| The gang | Proverbs 1:11-14 | First-person plural sales pitch for easy money |
| The procrastinating neighbor | Proverbs 3:28 | Quoted excuse: "Come back tomorrow and I will provide" |
| The buyer | Proverbs 20:14 | Quoted haggling: "Worthless, worthless!" -- then he boasts on the way out |
| The poor man | Proverbs 18:23; 19:7 | Heard only as "pleads for mercy" (*taḥănûnîm*) and as pursuit with words that find no one |
| The rich man | Proverbs 18:23; 28:11 | Answers "harshly"; "wise in his own eyes" |
| The imagined apostate | Proverbs 30:9 | "Who is the LORD?" -- the words Agur fears that fullness would put in his mouth |
| Agur | Proverbs 30:7-9 | The only prayer in the book, addressed to God in the second person |
| The LORD (implied evaluator) | Proverbs 11:1; 16:11; 20:10, 23 | Never speaks, but his "abomination" and "delight" frame commerce |

The poor never speak in their own words. They "plead" (Proverbs 18:23) and "cry" (Proverbs 21:13), and others answer or refuse to answer. The book is addressed to those who have something to give, lend or withhold (inference from the voices present). Proverbs 30:9 shows that the poverty and the riches the reader is warned about are possibilities for the reader himself.

**Mood.** Five moods recur:

- **Sober realism.** Proverbs 10:15; 14:20; 19:4, 7; 22:7 speak without sentiment about the protection wealth gives and the loneliness of poverty.
- **Irony.** Proverbs 18:11 ("in his imagination"), Proverbs 17:8 (a bribe is "a charm" literally "in the eyes of its owner"), Proverbs 28:11 ("wise in his own eyes") and Proverbs 20:14 (the buyer's "Worthless, worthless!") puncture the self-image of the wealthy and the shrewd.
- **Urgency.** Proverbs 6:3-5 piles up imperatives ("Go, humble yourself, and press your plea", "Allow no sleep to your eyes", "Free yourself").
- **Indignation and protection.** Proverbs 22:22-23 and Proverbs 23:10-11 speak with a hard edge. The LORD "will plunder those who rob them". The orphans' "Redeemer is strong".
- **Contentment.** The "better than" sayings and Agur's prayer have a quiet, settled tone. They describe a dish of vegetables shared in love (Proverbs 15:17), a dry morsel in quietness (Proverbs 17:1), and the bread that is one's portion (Proverbs 30:8).

**Style.** Several stylistic features stand out:

- **Compression.** The sayings of Proverbs 10:1-22:16 average about seven Hebrew words (counting words joined by a hyphen-like *maqqēp* as one), and the wealth sayings are typical. Proverbs 22:2 is six: *ʿāšîr wārāš nipgāšû ʿōśēh kullām YHWH*.
- **The language of quantity.** The topic is carried by **rab** (Hebrew: רַב, *rab*, H7227, "much, many") and **məʿaṭ** (Hebrew: מְעַט, *məʿaṭ*, H4592, "a little"). Proverbs 15:16 and Proverbs 16:8 open with the identical words *ṭôb-məʿaṭ bə-* ("Better a little with ...") and set *rab*/*rōb* ("great treasure", "great gain") in the second half. "Many" also gathers around the rich: "many are those who love the rich" (Proverbs 14:20); wealth "attracts many friends" (Proverbs 19:4); "many seek the favor of the prince" (Proverbs 19:6). It returns at the end of chapter 28, where the faithful man "will abound with blessings" and the one who hides his eyes "will receive many curses" (Proverbs 28:20, 27; both use *rab*). The book uses "much" and "little" to ask what really counts as much (inference).
- **Sound.** Several sayings rely on sound play (section 5).
- **Concreteness.** Barns, vats, grain, scales, stone weights, a cloak, a bed, a boundary stone, a field, a crust, an ox. The economics of Proverbs is the economics of the farm, the market and the gate, and it is pictured in its objects.

### 4. Rhetorical Devices

**Antithesis.** This is the default device of Proverbs 10-15. It sets the generous against the withholding (Proverbs 11:24), the one who trusts in riches against the righteous (Proverbs 11:28), and the oppressor against the one who is gracious (Proverbs 14:31). The choice of opposite matters. The one who "trusts in his riches" (Proverbs 11:28) is set against "the righteous", not against "the poor". The real alternative to trusting riches is a moral and religious posture, not a lower income (inference from the pairing).

**Comparison of value ("better than").** The *ṭôb ... min* sayings rank combinations. "Better a poor man who walks with integrity than a rich man whose ways are perverse" (Proverbs 28:6). The rhetoric works by making the hearer choose between two packages, each mixing an economic and a moral quality. The hearer is never asked to choose poverty for its own sake. He is asked to rank integrity above wealth when the two come apart.

**Paradox.** The *yēš* ("there is ...") sayings report surprising cases. In Proverbs 11:24, "One gives freely, yet gains even more; another withholds what is right, only to become poor." In Proverbs 13:7, "One pretends to be rich, but has nothing; another pretends to be poor, yet has great wealth." Both rely on the gap between appearance or expectation and outcome.

**Irony.** See section 3. It is concentrated in the sayings about how the rich see themselves (Proverbs 18:11; 28:11) and about bribes (Proverbs 17:8).

**Motive clauses.** The instructions give reasons, almost always introduced by **kî** (Hebrew: כִּי, *kî*, "for, because"): "for the LORD will take up their case" (Proverbs 22:23), "for their Redeemer is strong" (Proverbs 23:11), "for it makes wings for itself" (Proverbs 23:5), "for riches are not forever" (Proverbs 27:24). Proverbs does not command generosity or restraint without giving a reason. It persuades.

**Rhetorical question.** Proverbs 22:27, "If you have nothing with which to pay, why should your bed be taken from under you?" The question makes the hearer picture the loss for himself.

**Measure for measure (talion).** The punishment mirrors the act:

- Proverbs 21:13: the one who shuts his ears to the cry of the poor "too shall cry out and receive no answer".
- Proverbs 22:23: the LORD "will plunder those who rob them". The same rare verb, **qābaʿ** (Hebrew: קָבַע, *qābaʿ*, H6906), is used of the robbers and of the LORD's response.
- Proverbs 11:25: "he who refreshes others will himself be refreshed."

Proverbs 11:25 and Proverbs 21:13 share the phrase **gam-hûʾ** ("he too", "he himself") at the hinge of the reversal (observation). The same small formula serves the blessing on the generous and the judgment on the deaf.

**Merism.** Agur's "neither poverty nor riches" (Proverbs 30:8) names the two extremes to mark off the middle. Proverbs 22:2 and Proverbs 29:13 use pairs (rich and poor; the poor man and the oppressor) to say "everyone": "The LORD is Maker of them all" (Proverbs 22:2); "The LORD gives light to the eyes of both" (Proverbs 29:13).

**Appeal to the LORD as ground.** The rhetoric of justice to the poor reaches its peak when it names God. Proverbs 14:31 and Proverbs 17:5 share the identical phrase **ḥērēp ʿōśēhû** (Hebrew: חֵרֵף עֹשֵׂהוּ, *ḥērēp ʿōśēhû*, literally "taunts his Maker"; BSB "taunts their Maker" in Proverbs 14:31). Proverbs 19:17 makes kindness to the poor "a loan to the LORD". The argument moves the hearer from a horizontal relationship (me and the poor man) to a vertical one (me and the poor man's Maker).

**Second-line intensification.** See section 1 for Proverbs 22:7, 18:11, 14:31, 17:5 and 11:24-25. In the wealth sayings line b regularly tightens the screw. It names the mechanism, adds the theological dimension, or exposes the illusion.

**Quotation as self-exposure.** The delayer's "Come back tomorrow" (Proverbs 3:28), the buyer's "Worthless, worthless!" (Proverbs 20:14) and the imagined "Who is the LORD?" (Proverbs 30:9) let a character reveal himself in his own words.

### 5. Literary Devices

**Metaphor and simile.** The main images, with their force:

- *Fortified city and high wall* (Proverbs 10:15; 18:11): wealth as defense, real in Proverbs 10:15 and imagined in Proverbs 18:11. Set against the LORD's name as "strong tower" (Proverbs 18:10).
- *The eagle* (Proverbs 23:5): wealth "makes wings for itself and flies like an eagle to the sky". It is the swiftest and least catchable of birds.
- *Gazelle and bird* (Proverbs 6:5): the guarantor as hunted prey who must escape the hunter's and fowler's hand.
- *Foliage* (Proverbs 11:28): "the righteous will thrive like foliage", against the one who trusts in riches and "will fall". Leaf against fall, living growth against collapse.
- *Vapor* (**hebel**, Hebrew: הֶבֶל, *hebel*, H1892): wealth gained "from vapor" (Proverbs 13:11, BSB "dishonest wealth") and a fortune made by lying as "a vanishing mist" (Proverbs 21:6).
- *Driving rain* (Proverbs 28:3): the oppressor of the poor "is like a driving rain that leaves no food". Rain should bring bread, but this rain washes it away.
- *Crown* (Proverbs 14:24; 27:24): wealth as the visible adornment of the wise, and yet a crown that does not "endure to every generation".
- *The leech* (Proverbs 30:15): its two daughters, "Give and Give", picture an appetite that never says "Enough".

**Personification.** Wealth acts on its own: it "makes wings for itself" (Proverbs 23:5). Righteousness "brings deliverance from death" (Proverbs 10:2; 11:4). Wisdom holds "riches and honor" in her left hand (Proverbs 3:16). Blessing "will crown the one who sells" grain (Proverbs 11:26, literally "blessing is on the head of the seller").

**Wordplay and sound (from the Hebrew; observation unless noted).**

| Text | Play | Effect |
|---|---|---|
| Proverbs 22:22 | *ʾal-tigzol-dāl kî dal-hûʾ* ("do not rob the poor because he is poor") | The repeated *dal* exposes the motive: his weakness is what makes him an easy target |
| Proverbs 22:23 | *wəqābaʿ ʾet-qōbəʿêhem* ("He will rob their robbers") | Polyptoton: the same verb, turned back on the doers |
| Proverbs 23:5 | *hătāʿîp ʿênêkā* ... *yāʿûp* ("will you make your eyes fly to it? ... it flies") | The verb *ʿûp* ("fly") is used of the eye's darting glance and of the wealth's flight. The BSB "glance" smooths over the pun |
| Proverbs 11:25 | *ûmarweh gam-hûʾ yôreʾ* ("the one who waters will himself be watered") | Sound echo between giver and receiver. The form *yôreʾ* is unusual and its derivation is discussed (see the Linguistic Analysis) |
| Proverbs 11:15 | *raʿ-yērôaʿ* ("he will surely suffer harm") | Assonance on *rʿ* stresses the certainty of harm for the rash guarantor |
| Proverbs 20:10 | *ʾeben wāʾeben ʾêpâ wəʾêpâ* ("a stone and a stone, an ephah and an ephah") | The doubled words *enact* the double standard they condemn. The form imitates the fraud |
| Proverbs 13:7 | *mitʿaššēr* / *mitrôšēš* (reflexive verbs: "makes himself rich" / "makes himself poor") | Twin verb forms of opposite roots portray two kinds of pretense |
| Proverbs 30:8 | *rēʾš wāʿōšer* ("poverty and riches") | The two words share *r* and *š* in reverse order (*r-š* / *š-r*). Whether intended or not, the ear hears two sides of one coin |
| Proverbs 22:7 with Proverbs 19:17 | *malweh* ("lender") | In Proverbs 22:7 the lender is master and the borrower is slave. In Proverbs 19:17 the one who is kind to the poor is *malwēh YHWH*, "a lender to the LORD". The LORD steps into the debtor's place, and the lender of Proverbs 19:17 cannot be enslaved by the debt (inference about the effect of the shared term; its theology belongs to the Theological Analysis) |
| Proverbs 13:11 with Proverbs 28:8 | *qābaṣ* ("gather") and *rābâ* ("increase") | In Proverbs 13:11 the one who "gathers" by hand "increases". In Proverbs 28:8 the one who "increases" his wealth by interest finds that another "gathers" it, "for one who is kind to the poor". The two verbs change hands (observation; the reversal is the point) |
| Proverbs 10:4 with Proverbs 3:14; 8:19; 16:16 | **ḥārûṣ** (Hebrew: חָרוּץ, *ḥārûṣ*, H2742): "diligent" and "fine gold" | A homonym. Whether the sages exploit it (e.g., in Proverbs 12:27) is possible but not certain. Defer the diligence material to "The Sluggard" |

**Doublets and recurring lines.** The editors repeated a striking number of wealth sayings, or parts of them (observation):

| First occurrence | Repetition | What changes |
|---|---|---|
| Proverbs 10:2 ("righteousness brings deliverance from death") | Proverbs 11:4 | Same line b. Line a moves from "ill-gotten treasures" to "riches" in general on "the day of wrath": from *how* wealth is gained to *what* wealth can do |
| Proverbs 10:15a ("The wealth of the rich man is his fortified city") | Proverbs 18:11a | Line b turns from the poor man's ruin to the rich man's illusion; placed after Proverbs 18:10 |
| Proverbs 19:1 ("Better a poor man who walks with integrity than ...") | Proverbs 28:6 | The first five Hebrew words are identical. The foil changes from "a fool whose lips are perverse" to "a rich man whose ways are perverse", so the second form makes the wealth contrast explicit |
| Proverbs 14:31a ("taunts their Maker") | Proverbs 17:5a | From oppressing (*ʿāšaq*) the poor to mocking (*lāʿag*) them: from deed to attitude |
| Proverbs 22:2 ("have this in common", *nipgāšû*) | Proverbs 29:13 | From "the rich and the poor" to "the poor man and the oppressor"; from "Maker of them all" to "gives light to the eyes of both" |
| Proverbs 11:1 / Proverbs 20:10 | Proverbs 20:23 | "Abomination to the LORD" applied to false weights three times. Proverbs 20:10 and Proverbs 20:23 bracket the middle stretch of chapter 20 |
| Proverbs 20:16 ("Take the garment ...") | Proverbs 27:13 | Near-identical surety saying (Ketiv/Qere difference in the last word) |
| Proverbs 22:3 ("The prudent see danger ...") | Proverbs 27:12 | In both places the saying sits beside wealth material. In chapter 27 it is followed at once by the surety saying (Proverbs 27:13): the guarantor is the "simple" who walks into danger (inference from juxtaposition) |
| Proverbs 22:28 ("Do not move an ancient boundary stone") | Proverbs 23:10a | The second form adds "the fields of the fatherless" and the Redeemer |
| Proverbs 15:16 ("Better a little with ...") | Proverbs 16:8 | Same opening; "the fear of the LORD" becomes "righteousness", and "turmoil" becomes "injustice" |

**Juxtaposition as a device.** The editors often make meaning by placing sayings next to each other. Proverbs 18:10-11 is the clearest case (section 2). Others:

- Proverbs 14:20-21 sets the observation that "the poor man is hated even by his neighbor" beside the moral verdict that "blessed is he who shows kindness to the poor". The second saying judges the social fact reported by the first.
- Proverbs 13:22-23 sets the good man's inheritance passing to grandchildren beside the poor man's field swept away "without justice". The retribution principle stands next to its exception.
- Proverbs 22:2-4: rich and poor before their Maker; the prudent who see danger; the rewards of humility and the fear of the LORD.

### 6. Characters and Images

**The cast.** The wealth sayings populate Proverbs with types, never with named individuals:

| Character | Hebrew | Role | Key texts |
|---|---|---|---|
| The rich man | **ʿāšîr** (Hebrew: עָשִׁיר, *ʿāšîr*, H6223) | Protected, courted and powerful, but self-deceived. Nearly always paired with a poor man | Proverbs 10:15; 14:20; 18:11, 23; 22:2, 7, 16; 28:6, 11 |
| The poor man (economic) | **rāš** (Hebrew: רָשׁ, *rāš*, H7326) | Shunned and pleading, yet often the man of integrity | Proverbs 13:7-8, 23; 14:20; 17:5; 18:23; 19:1, 7, 22; 22:2, 7; 28:6, 27; 29:13 |
| The poor, low, weak | **dal** (Hebrew: דַּל, *dal*, H1800) | Object of oppression and of generosity; the word of the justice sayings | Proverbs 14:31; 19:17; 21:13; 22:9, 16, 22; 28:8, 11; 29:7 |
| The needy, the afflicted | **ʾebyôn** (Hebrew: אֶבְיוֹן, *ʾebyôn*, H34); **ʿānî** (Hebrew: עָנִי, *ʿānî*, H6041) | Paired terms for the vulnerable | Proverbs 14:21, 31; 22:22; 30:14; 31:9, 20 |
| The generous ("good of eye") | **ṭôb-ʿayin** (Hebrew: טוֹב־עַיִן, *ṭôb-ʿayin*) | Shares bread with the poor and is blessed | Proverbs 22:9; cf. 11:25 ("a generous soul", literally "a soul of blessing") |
| The stingy ("evil of eye") | **raʿ ʿayin** (Hebrew: רַע עַיִן, *raʿ ʿayin*) | A calculating host; hastens after wealth | Proverbs 23:6; 28:22 |
| The hoarder of grain | *mōnēaʿ bar* | Holds back grain to drive up the price; "the people will curse" him | Proverbs 11:26 |
| The guarantor | **ʿōrēb** (from **ʿārab**, Hebrew: עָרַב, *ʿārab*, H6148); *tōqēaʿ kap*, "one who strikes the palm" | Pledges for another's debt; "lacking judgment" | Proverbs 6:1-5; 11:15; 17:18; 20:16; 22:26-27; 27:13 |
| The stranger / foreigner | **zār** (Hebrew: זָר, *zār*, H2114); *nokrî* | The party for whom the rash pledge is made | Proverbs 6:1; 11:15; 20:16; 27:13 |
| Lender and borrower | **malweh** / **lōweh** (root *lāwâ*, H3867) | Master and servant of debt | Proverbs 22:7; cf. 19:17 |
| The oppressor | *ʿōšēq*; *ʾîš təkākîm* | Exploits the poor for gain | Proverbs 14:31; 22:16; 28:3; 29:13 |
| The one hasty to be rich | *ʾāṣ ləhaʿăšîr*; *nibhāl lahôn* | Rushes after wealth and "will not go unpunished" | Proverbs 28:20, 22; cf. 20:21; 21:5 |
| The pleasure-lover | *ʾōhēb śimḥâ* | Loves "wine and oil" and "will never be rich" | Proverbs 21:17; cf. 23:20-21 |
| Widow and orphan | *ʾalmānâ*; *yətômîm* | Owners of vulnerable land whose boundaries the LORD guards | Proverbs 15:25; 23:10-11 |
| The gang | *ḥaṭṭāʾîm*, "sinners" | Tempters to violent gain | Proverbs 1:10-19 |
| Lady Wisdom | *ḥokmâ* | Dispenser of "enduring wealth and righteousness" | Proverbs 3:16; 8:18-21 |
| Agur | *ʾāgûr* | The man who prays for the middle | Proverbs 30:7-9 |
| The valiant woman | *ʾēšet-ḥayil* | Trader and giver (defer to "Wisdom, Folly, and the Valiant Woman") | Proverbs 31:11, 16, 20, 24 |

Two observations about the cast:

- **The rich man rarely stands alone.** Of the nine occurrences of ʿāšîr, eight set him beside a poor man in the same saying (Proverbs 10:15; 14:20; 18:23; 22:2, 7, 16; 28:6, 11). The exception, Proverbs 18:11, sets him beside the LORD (Proverbs 18:10). In Proverbs the rich are always defined in relation to someone: the poor, or God.
- **The poor are not one type.** The poor man can be a man of integrity (Proverbs 19:1; 28:6), a man of discernment who "sees through" the rich (Proverbs 28:11), a victim of injustice (Proverbs 13:23), or an oppressor of his fellows (Proverbs 28:3, MT). The book has no stereotype of the poor.

**The LORD as a character.** The LORD is never pictured, but he takes a remarkable range of roles in the wealth sayings (observation):

| Role | Text |
|---|---|
| Maker of rich and poor alike | Proverbs 14:31; 17:5; 22:2 |
| Giver of light to the eyes of both | Proverbs 29:13 |
| Source of enrichment | Proverbs 10:22 |
| Owner and maker of honest scales | Proverbs 16:11 |
| Evaluator of commerce ("abomination ... delight") | Proverbs 11:1; 20:10, 23 |
| Borrower and repayer | Proverbs 19:17 |
| Advocate at law | Proverbs 22:23 |
| Redeemer (*gōʾēl*) of the orphan | Proverbs 23:11 |
| Destroyer of the proud man's house, protector of the widow's boundary | Proverbs 15:25 |

In the economy of Proverbs the LORD is creditor, debtor, judge, kinsman and owner of the scales all at once. The image is literary, and its theological weight is developed in the Theological Analysis.

**Testing the working notes' character chart.** The chart at the end of `Distinctions.md` traces a progression: the Simple, then either the Sluggard (rejecting discipline) or the Prudent (accepting it), then either the Mocker or the Wise. The wealth sayings show both where the chart fits and where it needs a second axis.

*Where it fits (observation).* The *simple* and the *prudent* appear in the wealth material at decisive points. "The prudent see danger and take cover, but the simple keep going and suffer the consequences" (Proverbs 22:3) sits inside the wealth cluster of Proverbs 22:1-16. Its doublet (Proverbs 27:12) is followed immediately by the surety saying (Proverbs 27:13). The guarantor is described as "a man lacking judgment" (*ḥăsar-lēb*, Proverbs 17:18), the same phrase used of the sluggard in Proverbs 24:30. So economic foresight is one of the places where the simple either becomes prudent or does not.

*Where it needs adjustment (inference).* The chart has no slot for rich or poor, and the wealth sayings suggest why. Economic status in Proverbs is not a stage of character but a *condition* in which character is revealed and tested. The sluggard ends poor (Proverbs 6:11; 24:34), but so do the pleasure-lover (Proverbs 21:17), the drunkard and glutton (Proverbs 23:21), the hasty (Proverbs 21:5) and the grasping (Proverbs 28:22). So does the victim of injustice (Proverbs 13:23), who has done nothing wrong. Rich and poor alike can be wise or foolish. The rich man is "wise in his own eyes", while the poor man "with discernment sees through him" (Proverbs 28:11). Agur's prayer states the testing function outright: riches tempt toward denial, poverty toward theft (Proverbs 30:9). The man who "mocks the poor" (Proverbs 17:5) uses the verb *lāʿag*, not the root *lîṣ* of the chart's "Mocker", so he should not be identified with that type without further argument. One way to represent this is to lay the conditions across the chart's paths:

```
              [ The Simple ]
                    |
        +-----------+-----------+
        |                       |
  [ Sluggard ] ...        [ Prudent ] ...
        |                       |
  [ Mocker ]              [ The Wise ]

  <-- RICHES ------------------------------------------- POVERTY -->
  test:  "Who is the LORD?" (Proverbs 30:9)   test:  "steal" (Proverbs 30:9)
  risk:  false security (Proverbs 18:11)      risk:  shunned by others (Proverbs 14:20)
  call:  give; do not trust (Proverbs 11:28)  call:  integrity (Proverbs 19:1; 28:6)

  Either condition can be found on either path (Proverbs 28:6, 11).
```

This is offered as a proposal for the user's chart, not as the book's own diagram. The claim that the sluggard "hardens" into the mocker is for "The Sluggard" and "The Two Paths" to test.

**The images, grouped.**

- *House and city*: houses filled with plunder (Proverbs 1:13) or with precious wealth (Proverbs 24:4); barns and vats (Proverbs 3:10); "a house full of feasting with strife" (Proverbs 17:1); the proud man's house torn down (Proverbs 15:25); the fortified city and high wall (Proverbs 10:15; 18:11); the strong tower (Proverbs 18:10).
- *Land and harvest*: firstfruits (Proverbs 3:9); grain held back (Proverbs 11:26); the poor man's fallow ground (Proverbs 13:23); the ancient boundary and the orphans' fields (Proverbs 22:28; 23:10); flocks and herds (Proverbs 27:23-27).
- *Table and bread*: a dish of vegetables and a fattened ox (Proverbs 15:17); a dry morsel (Proverbs 17:1); the stingy host's bread (Proverbs 23:6); bread shared with the poor (Proverbs 22:9); "the bread that is my portion" (Proverbs 30:8).
- *The body*: the palm that strikes the pledge (Proverbs 6:1; 17:18; 22:26) and the palm opened to the poor (Proverbs 31:20); the eye that is good (Proverbs 22:9), evil (Proverbs 23:6; 28:22), darting (Proverbs 23:5) or hidden (Proverbs 28:27); the ear stopped against the cry (Proverbs 21:13).
- *Creatures*: eagle (Proverbs 23:5), gazelle and bird (Proverbs 6:5), leech (Proverbs 30:15).

The body imagery is especially telling (inference). Generosity and greed in Proverbs are matters of where one looks, what one hears and what one does with one's hand. The same palm can be struck in a rash pledge or spread out to the needy.

### 7. Numerology and Symbolic Elements

**Explicit numbers.** Few, and none carries a hidden meaning:

- **Two.** "Two things I ask of You" (Proverbs 30:7) frames Agur's prayer as a small numerical saying. Doubling is also the image of fraud in Proverbs 20:10 ("a stone and a stone, an ephah and an ephah"), where "both" (*gam-šənêhem*) are detestable.
- **Three ... four.** The graded numerical saying of Proverbs 30:15-16 lists insatiable things that "never say, 'Enough!'" The Hebrew word for "Enough" there is **hôn** (Hebrew: הוֹן, *hôn*, H1952), elsewhere the book's commonest word for "wealth". The appetites that never say *hôn* sit beside a book that uses *hôn* for wealth sixteen times (eighteen occurrences in all) (observation; whether the irony was intended is uncertain).
- **Sevenfold.** The caught thief "must pay sevenfold" (Proverbs 6:31). The Torah's restitution laws require double, fourfold or fivefold (Exodus 22:1-9). Sevenfold here is best taken as the number of completeness: he pays in full, "all the wealth of his house" (inference; the legal background belongs to the Historical & Cultural Analysis).
- **Thirty.** "Have I not written for you thirty sayings?" (Proverbs 22:20). The Hebrew is a crux: the Ketiv *šlšwm* ("formerly") and the Qere *šālîšîm* (perhaps "excellent things") are commonly re-read as *šəlōšîm*, "thirty", which the BSB follows. The BSB divides Proverbs 22:17-24:22 into thirty numbered sayings, and most of the wealth admonitions of that collection fall among them. The connection to the thirty chapters of *Amenemope* belongs to the Historical & Cultural Analysis.

**Quantity as theme.** The more pervasive "numerology" of the topic is the language of amount: *rab* ("much, many") against *məʿaṭ* ("a little"), *maḥsôr* ("want") against *śābāʿ* ("plenty, fullness"). The root **śbʿ** (Hebrew: שָׂבַע, *śābaʿ*, H7646, "be full, satisfied") is a revealing thread (observation):
