- Barns "filled with plenty" (*śābāʿ*) for the one who honors the LORD (Proverbs 3:10).
- Agur's fear: "I may have too much and deny You" (Proverbs 30:9), where "have too much" renders *ʾeśbaʿ*, "be full".
- The leech's daughters and the grave are "never satisfied" (*lōʾ tiśbaʿnâ*, Proverbs 30:15-16).

Fullness is a blessing, a danger and a hunger that cannot be filled. The same root carries all three.

**Word counts (observation).** Counts are from a consonantal search of the Masoretic Text (verified here) or from Strong's tagging of the Bible Hub interlinear (marked "tagged").

| Term | Count in Proverbs | Distribution and note |
|---|---|---|
| **hôn** "wealth, substance; 'enough'" (H1952) | 18 (verified) | Proverbs 1:13; 3:9; 6:31; 8:18; 10:15; 11:4; 12:27; 13:7, 11; 18:11; 19:4, 14; 24:4; 28:8, 22; 29:3; 30:15, 16. The book's preferred wealth word, spread across every major collection |
| **ʿāšîr** "rich man" (H6223) | 9 (verified) | Proverbs 10:15; 14:20; 18:11, 23; 22:2, 7, 16; 28:6, 11. Only in the two Solomonic collections; eight of nine paired with a poor term |
| **ʿōšer** "riches" (H6239) | 9 (tagged) | Proverbs 3:16; 8:18; 11:16, 28; 13:8; 14:24; 22:1, 4; 30:8. A gift of Wisdom (Proverbs 3:16; 8:18; 22:4), an object of misplaced trust (Proverbs 11:28), and something to be prayed away from (Proverbs 30:8) |
| **rāš** "poor man" (H7326, participle) | 16 (tagged) | All within Proverbs 10:1-29:27 |
| **dal** "poor, weak" (H1800) | 15 tokens (verified) | Proverbs 10:15; 14:31; 19:4, 17; 21:13; 22:9, 16, 22 (twice); 28:3, 8, 11, 15; 29:7, 14. The vocabulary of the justice and generosity sayings |
| **ʾebyôn** "needy" (H34) | 4 (verified) | Proverbs 14:31; 30:14; 31:9, 20. Always paired with *dal* or *ʿānî* |
| **maḥsôr** "want, lack" (H4270) | 8 (tagged) | Proverbs 6:11; 11:24; 14:23; 21:5, 17; 22:16; 24:34; 28:27. The usual "outcome" word of the consequence sayings |
| **ʿārab** "be surety" (H6148) | 6 in the surety sense (tagged; homonyms excluded) | Proverbs 6:1; 11:15; 17:18; 20:16; 22:26; 27:13 |
| "Better than" (*ṭôb ... min*) sayings | about 21, of which 10 concern wealth | See section 1 |

The counts support three modest conclusions. First, wealth vocabulary saturates the sentence collections but is not confined to them. Second, the vocabulary for "the poor" is far richer than the vocabulary for "the rich". That fits the observation that the poor are not one type. Third, the book talks about "want" (*maḥsôr*) mainly as a *consequence*, which is why its causes must be traced saying by saying (laziness, pleasure, haste, stinginess, oppression; see "The Sluggard" for the first).

**Numerical word values (gematria).** No wealth saying in Proverbs shows a gematria pattern that the text itself signals, and none should be read into it. The one widely discussed numerical-value proposal in Proverbs is structural rather than topical. Patrick W. Skehan noted that the first Solomonic collection (Proverbs 10:1-22:16) contains 375 verses, which equals the numerical value of the name Solomon, **šəlōmōh** (Hebrew: שְׁלֹמֹה, *šəlōmōh*: ש 300 + ל 30 + מ 40 + ה 5). The verse count is confirmed by the Masoretic Text used here. The significance is debated (tradition). It bears on this topic only in that most of the book's wealth sayings sit inside that collection.

**Micro-patterns within the book (observation).**

1. **Rich and poor in pairs.** The ʿāšîr sayings almost always put the rich man beside the poor (section 6).
2. **"He too."** The *gam-hûʾ* reciprocity formula marks measure-for-measure in both the blessing on the generous (Proverbs 11:25) and the judgment on the deaf (Proverbs 21:13).
3. **The Maker formula.** "Taunts their Maker" (Proverbs 14:31) and "insults their Maker" (Proverbs 17:5), the same Hebrew phrase in both, and "the LORD is Maker of them all" (Proverbs 22:2) form a small chain that grounds justice to the poor in creation.
4. **"Abomination to the LORD" and commerce.** Three of the four honest-weight sayings use *tôʿăbat YHWH* (Proverbs 11:1; 20:10, 23). The fourth (Proverbs 16:11) says positively that the scales are "from the LORD".
5. **Twin openings.** Proverbs 15:16 and Proverbs 16:8 begin identically; Proverbs 19:1 and Proverbs 28:6 begin identically.
6. **Exchange of verbs.** Gather and increase trade places between Proverbs 13:11 and Proverbs 28:8. Lender and borrower trade places between Proverbs 22:7 and Proverbs 19:17.
7. **The eye.** Good (Proverbs 22:9), evil (Proverbs 23:6; 28:22), darting (Proverbs 23:5), hidden (Proverbs 28:27), "wise in his own eyes" (Proverbs 28:11), and given light by the LORD (Proverbs 29:13).

Canon-wide threads (the "evil eye" of Deuteronomy 15:9 and Matthew 6:22-23; the double weights of Deuteronomy 25:13; the rare verb *qābaʿ* in Malachi 3:8; Proverbs 22:8 in the Greek tradition behind 2 Corinthians 9:7) are handed to the Hermeneutic section.

### 8. Colors, Items, and Significance

Proverbs names no colors in its wealth sayings. Its symbolic world is one of materials, objects and places. The precious materials carry the book's comparisons of value. The common objects carry its ethics.

| Symbol | Meaning | Biblical Precedent |
|---|---|---|
| Scales and balances (*mōʾzənayim*, *peles*; Proverbs 11:1; 16:11; 20:23) | Commercial honesty as a matter between the merchant and the LORD | Leviticus 19:35-36; Job 31:6; Amos 8:5; Micah 6:11 |
| Stone weights and the bag (*ʾeben*, *kîs*; Proverbs 11:1; 16:11; 20:10, 23) | "All the weights in the bag are His concern" (Proverbs 16:11); double weights are fraud | Deuteronomy 25:13-16 ("a stone and a stone"); Micah 6:11 |
| The ephah (*ʾêpâ*, Proverbs 20:10) | The dry measure, the grain-trade counterpart of the weight-stone | Leviticus 19:36; Amos 8:5; Ezekiel 45:10 |
| Firstfruits, barns and vats (Proverbs 3:9-10) | Wealth honored as the LORD's by giving him the first, not the leftover; abundance as his response | Exodus 23:19; Deuteronomy 26:1-11; Malachi 3:10 |
| Grain held back (Proverbs 11:26) | Hoarding to raise the price in time of need; the community's curse | Genesis 41:56-57 (Joseph sells grain); Amos 8:5-6 |
| Ancient boundary stone (*gəbûl ʿôlām*; Proverbs 22:28; 23:10) | The family's inherited land and its protection; moving the marker is theft of a livelihood | Deuteronomy 19:14; 27:17; Hosea 5:10; Job 24:2 |
| The widow's boundary (Proverbs 15:25) | The LORD as guarantor of the land of the defenseless | Deuteronomy 10:18; Psalm 68:5; 146:9 |
| The struck palm (*tōqēaʿ kap*; Proverbs 6:1; 17:18; 22:26) | The gesture that seals a pledge for another's debt | Job 17:3 (striking hands as surety) |
| The garment taken in pledge (Proverbs 20:16; 27:13) | Collateral demanded from the rash guarantor | Exodus 22:26-27; Deuteronomy 24:12-13; Amos 2:8 |
| The bed taken (Proverbs 22:27) | The last possession lost to debt, a picture of total ruin | Exodus 22:27 (the cloak as the poor man's bedding) |
| Gold, fine gold, silver (*zāhāb*, *ḥārûṣ*, *paz*, *kesep*; Proverbs 3:14; 8:10, 19; 16:16; 22:1) | The standard measure of value, which wisdom, a good name and favor all exceed | Job 28:15-19; Psalm 19:10; 119:72 |
| Corals or rubies (*pənînîm*; Proverbs 3:15; 8:11; 20:15; 31:10) | Rare red treasure; wisdom and the valiant woman are worth more | Job 28:18; Lamentations 4:7 (where *pənînîm* is the standard of ruddiness, the one hint of color) |
| Crown (*ʿăṭeret*, *nēzer*; Proverbs 14:24; 27:24) | Wealth as the visible adornment of the wise, but not permanent | Proverbs 12:4; 16:31; 17:6 (other "crowns" in Proverbs) |
| Fortified city and high wall (Proverbs 10:15; 18:11) | The security wealth seems to give | Psalm 49:6-12; 52:7; 62:10 |
| Strong tower (Proverbs 18:10) | The security the LORD's name really gives | Psalm 18:2; 61:3; 144:2 |
| Ransom (*kōper*; Proverbs 13:8; cf. 6:35) | What wealth can sometimes buy (a life from a kidnapper or a court), and what it cannot (a wronged husband's forgiveness) | Exodus 21:30; Psalm 49:7-8 |
| Dish of vegetables / fattened ox (Proverbs 15:17) | The poor man's meal against the rich man's feast; love outweighs the menu | Daniel 1:12-16 (vegetables); 1 Kings 4:23 (Solomon's fattened cattle) |
| Dry morsel / house full of feasting (Proverbs 17:1) | Quiet poverty against a household of quarrels. "Feasting" is literally "sacrifices" (*zibḥê-rîb*, "sacrifices of strife"), perhaps the feasts that followed peace offerings | Leviticus 7:15-16; 1 Samuel 1:3-8 (a sacrificial feast full of rivalry) |
| Bread of my portion (*leḥem ḥuqqî*, Proverbs 30:8) | Enough, received as an allotment from God | Exodus 16:16-18 (manna); Matthew 6:11 |
| Eagle's wings (Proverbs 23:5) | The speed and finality with which wealth departs | Deuteronomy 28:49; Job 9:26; contrast Exodus 19:4 (the LORD's eagle wings that carry Israel) |
| The leech (Proverbs 30:15) | Insatiable appetite, the opposite of contentment | Isaiah 5:14 and Habakkuk 2:5 (Sheol's endless appetite, as in Proverbs 30:16) |
| The open palm (Proverbs 31:20) | Generosity as a gesture of the whole person | Deuteronomy 15:8, 11 (the command to open the hand to the poor brother) |

**Significance in sum (inference).** The items fall into three groups, and together they map the whole topic:

1. **Instruments of exchange.** Scales, weights, ephah, pledge, garment, bed. They show that Proverbs locates godliness in ordinary transactions. The LORD's "delight" is "an accurate weight" (Proverbs 11:1).
2. **Measures of value.** Gold, silver and corals are always the *lesser* term in the book's comparisons. Proverbs uses the language of treasure to say that something else is the treasure: wisdom (Proverbs 3:14; 16:16), a good name (Proverbs 22:1), "lips of knowledge" (Proverbs 20:15), a valiant wife (Proverbs 31:10).
3. **Pictures of security and its illusion.** City, wall, tower, crown, ransom, eagle. The question these images ask is where a person's safety really lies, and Proverbs 18:10-11 gives the book's answer in a single pair.

The detailed theology of these images belongs to the Theological Analysis. Their canonical development (Deuteronomy 15, Psalm 49, Luke 12:13-21, 1 Timothy 6:6-19) belongs to the Hermeneutic. Laziness as a cause of poverty belongs to "The Sluggard", and royal justice to the poor (Proverbs 16:10-15; 29:14; 31:8-9) to "The King".

---

## Theological Analysis

Proverbs has no single chapter on money. It has well over a hundred sayings that touch riches, poverty, giving, lending, buying and selling, scattered across every collection from the prologue (Proverbs 1-9) to the valiant woman (Proverbs 31:10-31). Read one at a time, some of them sound like a promise that the godly will be rich (Proverbs 3:9-10; 22:4). Others sound like the opposite: the righteous poor are better off than the wicked rich (Proverbs 16:8; 28:6). Read together, they form a theology of wealth with a definite shape. The LORD owns and gives. Conduct and consequence are linked under his rule. Wealth is a real good but a dangerous trust. The poor are his creatures and his clients. How a person gets money and what he does with it shows whom he fears. This section sets out that theology, weighs the book's retribution principle against the qualifications Proverbs itself attaches to it, and follows the theme into the wider canon.

Three related subjects belong to sibling studies. Laziness as a cause of poverty belongs to "The Sluggard". The king's duty to judge the poor (for example Proverbs 29:14; 31:8-9) belongs to "The King". The theology of friendship behind the sayings on the rich man's many friends (Proverbs 14:20; 19:4, 6-7) belongs to "The Friend and Neighbor". Here those texts are used only as evidence about wealth.

Unless noted otherwise, Scripture quotations are from the Berean Standard Bible (BSB). Labels in italics mark *observation* (what the text says), *inference* (what follows from it), *tradition* (how the church has read it) and *application*.

### 1. What the Original Audience Would Have Understood

**Wealth comes from the LORD and is to be acknowledged as his.** The first time the prologue mentions money, it is an act of worship: "Honor the LORD with your wealth and with the firstfruits of all your crops; then your barns will be filled with plenty, and your vats will overflow with new wine" (Proverbs 3:9-10). The word for "wealth" is **hôn** (Hebrew: הוֹן, *hôn*, H1952), Proverbs' favorite word for substance. "Firstfruits" is **rēʾšît** (Hebrew: רֵאשִׁית, *rēʾšît*, H7225), "beginning, first portion", the same word as in the book's motto, "The fear of the LORD is the beginning of knowledge" (Proverbs 1:7). *Observation:* the verse commands an offering and attaches a promise. *Inference:* an Israelite hearer would have heard the Torah's firstfruits law here (Exodus 23:19; Deuteronomy 26:1-11). Firstfruits were brought before the harvest was secure, as a confession that the land and its yield belonged to the LORD. Moses had warned Israel that prosperity tempts people to say, "The power and strength of my hands have made this wealth for me," and he gave the answer: "But remember that it is the LORD your God who gives you the power to gain wealth" (Deuteronomy 8:17-18). Proverbs says the same thing in one line: "The blessing of the LORD enriches, and He adds no sorrow to it" (Proverbs 10:22). The word **ʿeṣeb** (Hebrew: עֶצֶב, *ʿeṣeb*, H6089) can mean "sorrow" or "painful toil". On either reading the verse cuts against self-made wealth. Either the LORD's gift comes without the grief that ill-gotten gain carries, or human striving adds nothing to what his blessing gives (the Linguistic Analysis sets out the options).

**Wisdom brings wealth, but as a by-product.** Lady Wisdom holds "riches and honor" in her left hand (Proverbs 3:16), and she says, "With me are riches and honor, enduring wealth and righteousness" (Proverbs 8:18). She walks "along the paths of justice, bestowing wealth on those who love me and making their treasuries full" (Proverbs 8:20-21). In the same speech, though, she insists, "My fruit is better than gold, pure gold" (Proverbs 8:19). *Observation:* wealth is listed among Wisdom's gifts, and it is always ranked below her. *Inference:* the first hearers would have recognized the pattern of Solomon's own story. He asked for wisdom rather than riches and received riches as well (1 Kings 3:11-13). In Proverbs, wealth is what Wisdom gives to those who love her, not what they love her for.

**The world has a moral grain, and the LORD is behind it.** The sentence collections link conduct and outcome again and again. "Disaster pursues sinners, but prosperity is the reward of the righteous" (Proverbs 13:21). "One gives freely, yet gains even more; another withholds what is right, only to become poor" (Proverbs 11:24). "Whoever gives to the poor will not be in need, but he who hides his eyes will receive many curses" (Proverbs 28:27). Ancient Near Eastern wisdom generally shared this sense of order. Egypt spoke of *maʿat*, the cosmic order of truth and justice. The difference in Proverbs is that the order is personal. "The LORD does not let the righteous go hungry, but He denies the craving of the wicked" (Proverbs 10:3). Klaus Koch's influential 1955 thesis described an "act-consequence connection" in which deeds carry their own fruit like seed. That captures part of the picture. *Inference:* an Israelite hearer would not have heard an impersonal mechanism. He would have heard the covenant God of Deuteronomy 28, whose blessings and curses follow obedience and disobedience, now speaking in the voice of the sages.

**Yet the first hearers would also have heard the qualifications.** The same collections that state the pattern also limit it. "Better a little with righteousness than great gain with injustice" (Proverbs 16:8) presupposes that the righteous may have little and the unjust much. Most of the "better than" sayings that relativize wealth sit in Proverbs 15-21 (Proverbs 15:16-17; 16:8, 19; 17:1; 19:1, 22), with others at Proverbs 12:9; 22:1 and 28:6. (They do not cluster in Proverbs 25-27, as an aside in the working notes in Distinctions.md suggests; what clusters in Proverbs 25-26 is the simile.) One saying names injustice as the cause of a poor man's poverty: "Abundant food is in the fallow ground of the poor, but without justice it is swept away" (Proverbs 13:23). *Observation:* the book sets these sayings side by side with the retribution sayings and does not try to resolve them. *Inference:* the first hearers would have understood the retribution sayings as reliable generalizations about how God governs the world. They would not have read them as a guarantee that every rich man is righteous or every poor man is a sinner. Section 3 develops this.

**The poor share a Maker with the rich.** Three sayings ground the treatment of the poor in creation. "Whoever oppresses the poor taunts their Maker, but whoever is kind to the needy honors Him" (Proverbs 14:31). "He who mocks the poor insults their Maker" (Proverbs 17:5). "The rich and the poor have this in common: The LORD is Maker of them all" (Proverbs 22:2). A fourth applies the same logic to the poor man and his oppressor: "The LORD gives light to the eyes of both" (Proverbs 29:13). The participle "Maker" comes from **ʿāśâ** (Hebrew: עָשָׂה, *ʿāśâ*, H6213), "to make". The verb "taunts" or "insults" is **ḥārap** (Hebrew: חָרַף, *ḥārap*, H2778), the word used of Goliath defying the armies of the living God (1 Samuel 17:10, 26). *Inference:* a hearer in Judah would have taken this as a very strong claim. To exploit or ridicule a poor person was to defy God to his face. The ground offered is not Israel's exodus history, which is the Torah's usual reason for generosity (Deuteronomy 15:15; 24:18). It is the shared createdness of all human beings. That gave the sages' teaching a reach beyond Israel's borders, and it suited a book in conversation with the wisdom of the nations.

**The LORD is the advocate of the defenseless.** The "sayings of the wise" warn, "Do not rob a poor man because he is poor, and do not crush the afflicted at the gate, for the LORD will take up their case and will plunder those who rob them" (Proverbs 22:22-23). The "gate" was the place of the village court. To "take up their case" is **rîb** (Hebrew: רִיב, *rîb*, H7378), the lawsuit verb of the prophets. Shortly afterward comes the warning about the orphan's land: "Do not move an ancient boundary stone or encroach on the fields of the fatherless, for their Redeemer is strong; He will take up their case against you" (Proverbs 23:10-11). The **gōʾēl** (Hebrew: גֹּאֵל, *gōʾēl*, participle of H1350) was the kinsman who bought back family land and defended a relative in trouble (Leviticus 25:25; Ruth 4:1-10). *Observation:* the sage names the LORD himself as the orphan's kinsman-redeemer. Jeremiah 50:34 uses almost the same words of the LORD as Israel's Redeemer. *Inference:* these sayings stand close to the Egyptian *Instruction of Amenemope*, which also warns against robbing the poor and moving boundary stones. But in Proverbs the protector is not the moon-god or an abstract order. He is Israel's God, acting as next-of-kin to those who have none. The widow is covered in the same way: "The LORD tears down the house of the proud, but He protects the boundaries of the widow" (Proverbs 15:25).

**The marketplace belongs to the LORD.** "Dishonest scales are an abomination to the LORD, but an accurate weight is His delight" (Proverbs 11:1). "Honest scales and balances are from the LORD; all the weights in the bag are His concern" (Proverbs 16:11). Proverbs 20:10 and 20:23 repeat the point. **Tôʿēbâ** (Hebrew: תּוֹעֵבָה, *tôʿēbâ*, H8441), "abomination", is a word from Israel's worship, used of idolatry and of detestable offerings (Deuteronomy 7:25; 17:1). *Inference:* the first hearers would have been struck to hear a temple word used of a merchant's weights. In Proverbs the stall in the market is as much under the LORD's eye as the altar (compare Leviticus 19:35-36; Deuteronomy 25:13-16; Amos 8:5).

**Some sayings describe the world without approving it.** "The wealth of the rich man is his fortified city, but poverty is the ruin of the poor" (Proverbs 10:15). "The poor man is hated even by his neighbor, but many are those who love the rich" (Proverbs 14:20). "The poor man pleads for mercy, but the rich man answers harshly" (Proverbs 18:23). "The rich rule over the poor, and the borrower is slave to the lender" (Proverbs 22:7). *Observation:* these are statements of fact about social life. *Inference:* a hearer schooled in proverbs would not mistake them for commendations. The book corrects them from within. Proverbs 10:15 says wealth is the rich man's fortified city. Proverbs 18:11 repeats the line and adds the barb: "it is like a high wall in his imagination." The saying just before it points to the real fortress: "The name of the LORD is a strong tower; the righteous run to it and are safe" (Proverbs 18:10). Proverbs 14:20 reports that the poor are hated. Proverbs 14:21 follows at once with a moral verdict: "He who despises his neighbor sins, but blessed is he who shows kindness to the poor."

**The person addressed has something to give.** The Historical & Cultural Analysis notes that the son of Proverbs is assumed to have means. He has firstfruits to offer (Proverbs 3:9). He has the power to help his neighbor today rather than tomorrow (Proverbs 3:27-28). He might be asked to stand surety (Proverbs 6:1-5; 22:26-27). *Inference:* the wealth teaching was aimed mainly at people with some property and influence. They were told that they answer to the LORD for how they treat those beneath them. The poor are mostly spoken *about*. When they are spoken *for*, the LORD himself is the speaker's guarantee (Proverbs 22:23; 23:11).

### 2. Spiritual Disciplines in View

Proverbs does not talk about "disciplines" in the modern sense. It does call for settled habits of heart and hand, and many of them concern money. The table summarizes them; the paragraphs that follow take up the most important.

| Discipline | Key Proverbs texts | Contrary vice the book names |
|:--|:--|:--|
| Honoring the LORD with the first portion | Proverbs 3:9-10 | Self-made pride (compare Deuteronomy 8:17) |
| Prompt, open-handed giving | Proverbs 3:27-28; 11:24-26; 21:26; 22:9; 28:27; 31:20 | Withholding, hoarding, the "evil eye" (Proverbs 11:24, 26; 23:6; 28:22) |
| Hearing the cry and knowing the cause of the poor | Proverbs 21:13; 29:7 | Stopping the ears; "hiding the eyes" (Proverbs 21:13; 28:27) |
| Honest dealing | Proverbs 11:1; 16:11; 20:10, 14, 23 | Rigged scales; buyer's deceit (Proverbs 20:14) |
| Refusing unjust gain and bribes | Proverbs 10:2; 15:27; 16:8; 17:23; 28:8 | Gain by violence, interest and fraud (Proverbs 1:13-19; 20:17; 21:6) |
| Restraint and contentment | Proverbs 15:16-17; 17:1; 23:4-5; 30:7-9 | Haste to be rich, love of pleasure (Proverbs 21:17; 28:20, 22) |
| Prayer about one's economic life | Proverbs 30:7-9 | Forgetting God in fullness, stealing in want (Proverbs 30:9) |
| Prudence about debt and surety | Proverbs 6:1-5; 11:15; 17:18; 22:7, 26-27 | Rash pledges "in the presence of" a neighbor (Proverbs 17:18) |
| Placing trust in the LORD, not in riches | Proverbs 11:28; 18:10-11; 28:25 | Trusting in riches; the rich man wise in his own eyes (Proverbs 11:28; 28:11) |
| Refusing to envy the prosperous wicked | Proverbs 23:17-18; 24:19-20 | Fretting and envy |

**Firstfruits giving as worship.** The first discipline is to give God the first portion before the rest is secure (Proverbs 3:9-10). *Inference:* this is a discipline of trust as much as of generosity. The offering is made while the barns are still empty. The promise of full barns is real, and it should not be dismissed. But it is the promise of a Father to a son who is learning to fear him (Proverbs 3:1-12), not a formula for multiplying investments. The rest of the book keeps it from becoming one (see Section 3).

**Generosity without delay.** "Do not withhold good from the deserving when it is within your power to act. Do not tell your neighbor, 'Come back tomorrow and I will provide'—when you already have the means" (Proverbs 3:27-28). The phrase "from the deserving" is literally "from its owners", using **baʿal** (Hebrew: בַּעַל, *baʿal*, H1167), "owner, master". "Withhold" is **mānaʿ** (Hebrew: מָנַע, *mānaʿ*, H4513). *Observation:* the good is described as already belonging to the person who needs it. *Inference:* in Proverbs generosity to a neighbor in need is a debt being paid, not an optional extra. Delay is treated as a form of withholding. The paradox of giving follows from this: "One gives freely, yet gains even more; another withholds what is right, only to become poor. A generous soul will prosper, and he who refreshes others will himself be refreshed" (Proverbs 11:24-25). The grain-hoarder who holds back his stock to drive up the price is cursed by the people (Proverbs 11:26). The righteous person, by contrast, gives "without restraint" (Proverbs 21:26). The valiant woman, who trades profitably, also "opens her arms to the poor and reaches out her hands to the needy" (Proverbs 31:20). Her portrait belongs to "Wisdom, Folly, and the Valiant Woman", but her open hand belongs here.

**The good eye.** "A generous man will be blessed, for he shares his bread with the poor" (Proverbs 22:9). The BSB's footnote gives the Hebrew idiom: the generous man is the one "whose eye is good", **ṭôb-ʿayin** (Hebrew: טוֹב־עַיִן, *ṭôb-ʿayin*; H2896 + H5869). The stingy man is the one whose eye is evil (Proverbs 23:6; 28:22). This is not the folk-magic "evil eye". It is the grudging look that Deuteronomy 15:9 forbids, the warning not to "look upon your poor brother begrudgingly and give him nothing". *Inference:* generosity starts with how one *sees* the poor. The discipline is to look at need with a willing eye before the hand moves.

**Hearing the cry.** "Whoever shuts his ears to the cry of the poor, he too shall cry out and receive no answer" (Proverbs 21:13). "The righteous consider the cause of the poor, but the wicked have no regard for such concerns" (Proverbs 29:7). *Observation:* the first saying matches the punishment to the sin. The one who would not hear will not be heard. *Inference:* the saying ties attention to the poor to the sinner's own standing before God in prayer. Deuteronomy 15:9 warns that the poor man "will cry out to the LORD against you". Proverbs adds that the one who ignores that cry will find his own cry unanswered. To "consider the cause" uses the legal term **dîn** (Hebrew: דִּין, *dîn*, H1779), "a legal claim". The discipline is not only sympathy. It means taking the trouble to understand a poor person's situation as one would a case in court.

**Honest dealing as holiness in the market.** Because the LORD "delights" in an accurate weight (Proverbs 11:1), ordinary commerce becomes an arena of faithfulness. Proverbs even notices the small dishonesties of the buyer, who runs down the goods while haggling and then boasts of his bargain once he has gone (Proverbs 20:14). *Application:* the discipline extends to every pricing decision, contract and invoice.

**Restraint and contentment.** "Do not wear yourself out to get rich; be wise enough to restrain yourself. When you glance at wealth, it disappears, for it makes wings for itself and flies like an eagle to the sky" (Proverbs 23:4-5). *Observation:* the command is not a ban on work. It warns against exhausting oneself for riches and calls the hearer to know when to stop. The "better than" sayings train the same contentment by changing what the heart values: "Better a little with the fear of the LORD than great treasure with turmoil" (Proverbs 15:16). Proverbs 15:17 sets a dish of vegetables shared in love above a fattened ox served with hatred, and Proverbs 17:1 sets a dry morsel in quietness above a house full of feasting with strife. Contentment in Proverbs is not resignation. It is a judgment about what is truly good.

**Prayer about money.** Agur's prayer is the one place in Proverbs where someone speaks to God about wealth: "Two things I ask of You—do not refuse me before I die" (Proverbs 30:7). The second request is: "Give me neither poverty nor riches; feed me with the bread that is my portion. Otherwise, I may have too much and deny You, saying, 'Who is the LORD?' Or I may become poor and steal, profaning the name of my God" (Proverbs 30:8-9). "My portion" is **ḥōq** (Hebrew: חֹק, *ḥōq*, H2706), an allotted share. *Inference:* Agur models a discipline the rest of the book assumes. He asks God to set the amount, and his reason is spiritual rather than economic. He is afraid of what too much or too little would do to his faithfulness.

**Prudence about debt and surety.** The book repeatedly warns against standing surety, the pledge to cover someone else's debt sealed by striking hands (Proverbs 6:1-5; 11:15; 17:18; 20:16; 22:26-27; 27:13). The verb is **ʿārab** (Hebrew: עָרַב, *ʿārab*, H6148). The remedy in Proverbs 6:3-5 is humbling: go, humble yourself, press your neighbor, and free yourself like a gazelle from the hunter. *Inference:* this is a discipline of honest self-knowledge. One should not promise what one cannot pay. "If you have nothing with which to pay, why should your bed be taken from under you?" (Proverbs 22:27). It is not a ban on generosity. The same book calls kindness to the poor a loan to the LORD (Proverbs 19:17) and condemns profiting from the poor by interest (Proverbs 28:8). What it forbids is the rash pledge that puts one's household at the mercy of a creditor, especially on behalf of an outsider, a **zār** (Hebrew: זָר, *zār*, participle of H2114). Proverbs 22:7 supplies the sober reason: the borrower becomes the lender's servant.

**Trust rightly placed.** "He who trusts in his riches will fall, but the righteous will thrive like foliage" (Proverbs 11:28). The verb is **bāṭaḥ** (Hebrew: בָּטַח, *bāṭaḥ*, H982), the same word as in "Trust in the LORD with all your heart" (Proverbs 3:5). Proverbs 28:25 sets the two objects of trust side by side: "A greedy man stirs up strife, but he who trusts in the LORD will prosper." *Inference:* the deepest discipline concerning wealth is a matter of faith: where one runs when threatened (Proverbs 18:10-11).

### 3. Timeless Theological Principles

**First, the LORD owns the world's goods and distributes them.** The blessing of the LORD enriches (Proverbs 10:22). The scales and weights are "from the LORD" (Proverbs 16:11). He is Maker of rich and poor alike (Proverbs 22:2). *Systematic category:* this is the doctrine of providence applied to economics. Possessions are a trust, not an absolute property. The human owner is a steward who honors the true owner with the first portion (Proverbs 3:9) and owes good to those in need (Proverbs 3:27).

**Second, conduct and consequence are linked by God's governance, but not mechanically.** This is the principle most often misread, so it needs the most care. On one side stands a large body of sayings in which righteousness, generosity and the fear of the LORD lead to plenty, and wickedness, stinginess and haste lead to loss. On the other side stand sayings in which Proverbs qualifies its own pattern. The two sets are not hidden from one another. They often sit in the same chapter.

| The pattern | The book's own qualifications |
|:--|:--|
| Righteousness and generosity bring plenty: Proverbs 3:9-10; 10:3, 22; 11:24-25; 13:21-22; 22:4, 9; 28:25, 27 | **Righteous poverty and wicked wealth exist.** Proverbs 15:16-17; 16:8, 19; 17:1; 19:1; 28:6, 11 rank integrity in poverty above crooked wealth, which they assume is real |
| Wickedness, stinginess and haste bring want: Proverbs 11:24; 21:17; 22:16; 28:20, 22 | **Poverty can be caused by others' injustice, not one's own vice.** Proverbs 13:23; 22:22-23; 23:10-11; 30:14 |
| Ill-gotten wealth does not last: Proverbs 10:2; 13:11; 20:17; 21:6 | **The time-frame is long.** Riches fail "in the day of wrath" (Proverbs 11:4); the sinner's wealth "is passed to the righteous" (Proverbs 13:22; compare Proverbs 28:8); "there is a future" (Proverbs 23:18; compare Proverbs 24:19-20) |
| Wealth protects the rich (Proverbs 10:15; 13:8) | **The outcome rests with the LORD, not a mechanism.** He repays (Proverbs 19:17), pleads the case (Proverbs 22:23; 23:11) and is the true strong tower (Proverbs 18:10-11) |

*Observation:* the "better than" sayings would make no sense if the righteous were always rich. "Better a poor man who walks with integrity than a rich man whose ways are perverse" (Proverbs 28:6) assumes that both kinds of people are found in the world. *Inference:* the retribution sayings are best read as true generalizations about the way God has ordered the world. They describe the direction in which conduct tends under his rule. They are not unconditional promises to every individual on every occasion. That is how proverbs work as a genre: Proverbs 26:4-5 sets two opposite instructions side by side to show that sayings must be applied with judgment. Raymond Van Leeuwen's article "Wealth and Poverty: System and Contradiction in Proverbs" (*Hebrew Studies* 33, 1992) makes a similar point. The "system" of act and consequence is real, and the "contradictions" are deliberate reminders that the system is not the whole truth in any given moment.

Two misreadings are excluded by this. The first is a prosperity reading, which treats Proverbs 3:9-10 or 22:4 as a transaction: give, and God must make you rich. That reading ignores the book's own counter-sayings. It makes the poor righteous man of Proverbs 19:1 and 28:6 impossible, and Proverbs 13:23 unintelligible. It also turns the fear of the LORD into a tool for gain, which comes close to the danger Agur names, fullness that forgets God (Proverbs 30:9). The second misreading is a skeptical one, which takes the "better than" sayings as cancelling the rest, so that Proverbs ends up saying nothing about consequence at all. That reading ignores the book's consistent confidence that the LORD rewards and judges (Proverbs 10:3; 11:31; 19:17; 22:23). *Inference:* the book holds both together. God's moral government is real and will be vindicated, but its working is often slow, and its final settlement lies beyond what can be seen now. Job, Psalm 73 and Ecclesiastes press the same tension further. Proverbs does not ignore it. It flags it and leaves it under the LORD's hand.

**Third, wealth and poverty are conditions that test character. They do not measure it.** The working notes in Distinctions.md include a chart of character progressions (the simple becoming either the sluggard and then the mocker, or the prudent and then the wise). The chart has no place for the rich or the poor, and the wealth sayings explain why. In Proverbs a poor man can have integrity (Proverbs 19:1; 28:6) and discernment (Proverbs 28:11). A rich man can be "wise in his own eyes" (Proverbs 28:11). Poverty comes to the sluggard (the subject of "The Sluggard"), but also to the pleasure-lover (Proverbs 21:17), the drunkard and glutton (Proverbs 23:20-21), the hasty (Proverbs 21:5; 28:22) and the victim of injustice (Proverbs 13:23). Even appearances deceive: "One pretends to be rich, but has nothing; another pretends to be poor, yet has great wealth" (Proverbs 13:7). *Inference:* economic standing is not a stage on the path from simple to wise. It is better pictured as terrain that every character type crosses, and the terrain exposes what the traveler is. Agur's prayer states the test exactly:

```text
                Agur's prayer (Proverbs 30:8-9)

     TOO MUCH                 MY PORTION               TOO LITTLE
   ("riches")               ("the bread")             ("poverty")
       |                         |                          |
  "I may ... deny You,     Daily dependence on       "I may become poor
   saying, 'Who is          the LORD who gives         and steal,
   the LORD?'"                                         profaning the name
       |                         |                     of my God"
       v                         v                          v
   Pride: forgetting         The fear of the          Desperation:
   the Giver                 LORD sustained           dishonoring the Giver
```

*Observation:* both dangers end in the same place, a sin against the LORD's name. *Inference:* the measure of wealth in Proverbs is theological. The question is not how much a person has but whether the amount draws him toward God or away from him.

**Fourth, how one treats the poor is how one treats God.** Oppressing the poor "taunts their Maker"; kindness to the needy "honors Him" (Proverbs 14:31). "Kindness to the poor is a loan to the LORD, and He will repay the lender" (Proverbs 19:17). The word "kindness" is **ḥānan** (Hebrew: חָנַן, *ḥānan*, H2603), "to be gracious, show favor", and the "lender" is a participle of **lāwâ** (Hebrew: לָוָה, *lāwâ*, H3867). *Inference:* the vertical and horizontal dimensions of faith cannot be separated. The LORD so identifies with the poor that he treats a gift to them as a debt he himself owes. *Systematic category:* this is part of theological anthropology. Every human being bears a dignity rooted in having God as Maker (compare Genesis 1:27; 9:6).

**Fifth, justice and generosity are distinct but inseparable.** Proverbs forbids taking from the poor (Proverbs 22:16, 22-23; 23:10-11; 28:8) and commands giving to them (Proverbs 3:27-28; 22:9; 28:27). The first is justice, the refusal to exploit. The second is generosity, the willingness to share. *Observation:* Proverbs does not let either stand alone. It treats withholding what is due as a wrong (Proverbs 3:27; 11:24, 26), and it calls exploitation of the poor an attack on God (Proverbs 14:31). *Inference:* charity cannot cover for injustice, and refraining from injustice does not discharge the duty of generosity. Proverbs 28:8 joins them: "He who increases his wealth by interest and usury lays it up for one who is kind to the poor." The terms **nešek** (Hebrew: נֶשֶׁךְ, *nešek*, H5392), literally "a bite", and **tarbît** (Hebrew: תַּרְבִּית, *tarbît*, H8636), "increase", are the Torah's terms for lending at interest to a poor brother (Leviticus 25:36). *Inference:* the target is exploitative lending to the needy, not every commercial loan. The saying expects God's providence to move such wealth into generous hands in the end.

**Sixth, integrity outranks wealth.** "Better a little with righteousness than great gain with injustice" (Proverbs 16:8). "A good name is more desirable than great riches; favor is better than silver and gold" (Proverbs 22:1). "It is better to be lowly in spirit among the humble than to divide the spoil with the proud" (Proverbs 16:19). *Inference:* the "better than" sayings set out an ordering of goods. Righteousness, the fear of the LORD, love, peace, humility and a good name all rank above money. Wealth is a genuine good, but a lesser one, and it must never be bought at the price of the greater.

**Central theological claim:** In Proverbs, wealth is a real but lesser good that the LORD gives, tests and judges, so that how a person gains it, trusts it and shares it with the poor whom God made and defends reveals whether that person fears the LORD.

### 4. Key Theological Themes

**Who God is in this theme.** Proverbs speaks of God almost entirely as **the LORD** (Hebrew: יְהוָה, *YHWH*, H3068), the covenant name. In the wealth sayings he appears in a remarkable range of roles:

| Role | What he does | Proverbs texts |
|:--|:--|:--|
| Maker | Creates rich and poor alike; gives sight to poor and oppressor | Proverbs 14:31; 17:5; 22:2; 29:13 |
| Giver | His blessing enriches without added sorrow | Proverbs 10:22; compare Proverbs 3:9-10; 10:3 |
| Owner of commerce | Honest scales are "from the LORD"; true weights are his delight, false ones an abomination | Proverbs 11:1; 16:11; 20:10, 23 |
| Debtor who repays | Treats kindness to the poor as a loan to himself | Proverbs 19:17 |
| Advocate and judge | Takes up the poor man's case and plunders those who rob him | Proverbs 22:22-23; 15:25 |
| Redeemer | The orphan's strong **gōʾēl** | Proverbs 23:10-11 |
| Refuge | His name is the strong tower that riches only imagine themselves to be | Proverbs 18:10-11 |
| Judge of the end | Riches fail in the "day of wrath"; there is a "future" | Proverbs 11:4; 23:17-18; 24:19-20 |

*Observation:* his attributes here are justice (he hates false weights and defends the defenseless), generosity (he blesses and repays), faithfulness (he guarantees the future of those who fear him), and sovereignty over outcomes. He is not neutral between the strong and the weak. He takes the side of the poor in court against those who exploit them (Proverbs 22:23), while still requiring honesty from everyone (Proverbs 11:1).

*Christ.* Proverbs does not name the Messiah, and the wealth sayings should not be forced into predictions. The canonical connections are real but indirect. (1) Wisdom personified, who holds "enduring wealth and righteousness" (Proverbs 8:18), is the background for the New Testament's presentation of Christ as the wisdom of God (1 Corinthians 1:24, 30) in whom all the treasures of wisdom and knowledge are said to be hidden (Colossians 2:3). *Inference:* the true riches Wisdom offers are found in Christ. (2) The God who identifies himself with the poor (Proverbs 14:31; 19:17) is matched by Christ's own words: "whatever you did for one of the least of these brothers of Mine, you did for Me" (Matthew 25:40). (3) The one who was rich in every sense chose poverty for others: "though He was rich, yet for your sake He became poor, so that you through His poverty might become rich" (2 Corinthians 8:9). Paul uses that fact to motivate the generosity Proverbs commands. (4) Proverbs warns against rash surety, but Scripture also knows a surety of love. Judah pledged himself for Benjamin with the same verb, **ʿārab** (Genesis 43:9: "I will guarantee his safety"), and Hebrews says that "Jesus has become the guarantee of a better covenant" (Hebrews 7:22). The Greek translation of Proverbs uses the same word family for surety (*engyaō*, *engyē*) that Hebrews uses for Christ as **ἔγγυος** (Greek: ἔγγυος, *engyos*, "guarantor"). *Inference, held with care:* the sages forbade a man to pledge what he could not pay. The gospel announces a guarantor who could pay and did. This is a canonical reading, not the original meaning of Proverbs 6:1-5.

*The Spirit.* The Spirit is not named in the wealth sayings, and it would overstate the evidence to find him there. The New Testament, though, presents Spirit-filled community as marked by exactly the generosity Proverbs commends: the Jerusalem believers shared their goods so that no one among them was needy (Acts 2:44-45; 4:32-35), and Paul speaks of the Corinthians' giving as a work of grace (2 Corinthians 8:1-7). *Inference:* what Proverbs commands, the Spirit produces in the new covenant community.

**The poor and the image of God.** Proverbs' "Maker" sayings (Proverbs 14:31; 17:5; 22:2; 29:13) are the wisdom form of the creation theology of Genesis 1:27 and 9:6. James argues the same way about speech: it is inconsistent to bless God and curse people made in his likeness (James 3:9). *Systematic category:* human dignity is grounded in creation, not in economic productivity. *Progression of revelation:* the Torah grounds care for the poor in redemption from Egypt (Deuteronomy 15:15). Proverbs grounds it in creation. The New Testament grounds it in both and adds the incarnation: Christ shared the condition of the poor (2 Corinthians 8:9), and God has chosen "the poor of this world to be rich in faith and to inherit the kingdom" (James 2:5).

**Stewardship and the first portion.** Proverbs 3:9-10 stands in line with the firstfruits laws (Exodus 23:19; Deuteronomy 26:1-11), with David's confession that everything given to God already came from his hand (1 Chronicles 29:14), and with Malachi's challenge to bring the full tithe (Malachi 3:10). *Tradition:* John Wesley's sermon "The Use of Money" summarized the Christian stewardship tradition as "gain all you can, save all you can, give all you can", and its third rule governs the first two. *Inference:* that maxim is close to Proverbs' balance. Diligence and prudence are honored, but the end of wealth is honoring God and helping the neighbor.

**Retribution, providence and the widening horizon.** Proverbs' sayings about the "day of wrath" (Proverbs 11:4), the righteous inheriting the sinner's wealth (Proverbs 13:22) and the "future" that will not be cut off (Proverbs 23:18) point beyond the visible present. *Observation:* Proverbs does not spell out what that future is. *Progression of revelation:* later Scripture fills it in. Psalm 49 says that "No man can possibly redeem his brother or pay his ransom to God" (Psalm 49:7) and that the rich man "will carry nothing away" at death (Psalm 49:17). That deepens Proverbs 11:4 and 13:8. Zephaniah 1:18 applies Proverbs' "day of wrath" to the Day of the LORD. Jesus' parable of the rich fool ends, "This is how it will be for anyone who stores up treasure for himself but is not rich toward God" (Luke 12:21), and the parable of the rich man and Lazarus (Luke 16:19-31) shows the reversal that Proverbs 21:13 hints at. Paul tells Timothy to instruct the rich "not to put their hope in the uncertainty of wealth, but in God" and to be "generous and ready to share, treasuring up for themselves a firm foundation for the future" (1 Timothy 6:17-19). *Inference:* the New Testament does not abolish Proverbs' retribution principle. It moves the final reckoning to the day of judgment and the age to come. That is where Proverbs' own qualifications were already pointing.

**Generosity as lending to God.** Proverbs 19:17 makes the LORD the debtor of the generous. The Torah attaches a similar promise to lending to the poor brother: "Give generously to him ... And because of this the LORD your God will bless you in all your work" (Deuteronomy 15:10). Jesus says, "Give, and it will be given to you. A good measure, pressed down, shaken together, and running over will be poured into your lap" (Luke 6:38), and he commends inviting those who cannot repay, because they will be repaid when the righteous are raised (Luke 14:12-14). In 2 Corinthians 9:6-11 Paul uses the sowing-and-reaping logic of Proverbs 11:24-25 and quotes Psalm 112:9, which uses the same verb for "scattering" to the poor as Proverbs 11:24. His "God loves a cheerful giver" (2 Corinthians 9:7) appears to echo a line found in the Greek text of Proverbs 22:8 but not in the Hebrew (see the Linguistic Analysis). *Inference:* the New Testament keeps Proverbs' promise of repayment, but keeps it in the hands of the Giver. It is not a technique for gain.

**The good eye and the single heart.** The "good eye" of Proverbs 22:9 and the "evil eye" of Proverbs 23:6 and 28:22 run back to Deuteronomy 15:9 and forward to the Sermon on the Mount. "The eye is the lamp of the body. If your eyes are good, your whole body will be full of light. But if your eyes are bad, your whole body will be full of darkness" (Matthew 6:22-23). Jesus places those words between "where your treasure is, there your heart will be also" (Matthew 6:21) and "You cannot serve both God and money" (Matthew 6:24). The same idiom returns in the parable of the vineyard workers (Matthew 20:15). *Inference:* the Hebrew idiom for generosity has become, in Jesus' teaching, a test of undivided devotion.

**Trust in riches as idolatry.** Proverbs says the rich man's fortress exists "in his imagination" (Proverbs 18:11), while the LORD's name is the true strong tower (Proverbs 18:10). The word **maśkît** (Hebrew: מַשְׂכִּית, *maśkît*, H4906), "imagination", is used elsewhere of carved images (Leviticus 26:1; Numbers 33:52). *Inference, offered cautiously:* the overtone of an idol fits the context, though the sense here is "fancy, imagination". Job makes the link explicit: "If I have put my trust in gold or called pure gold my security ... I would have denied God on high" (Job 31:24, 28). The New Testament names greed as idolatry outright (Colossians 3:5) and warns that "the love of money is the root of all kinds of evil" (1 Timothy 6:10). Agur's fear that fullness would make him say "Who is the LORD?" (Proverbs 30:9) is the same danger in prayer form.

**Daily bread and contentment.** Agur's request for "the bread that is my portion" (Proverbs 30:8) recalls the manna, gathered daily, each according to need (Exodus 16:16-18), and it anticipates the Lord's Prayer: "Give us this day our daily bread" (Matthew 6:11). Paul calls "godliness with contentment" "great gain" and adds, "if we have food and clothing, we will be content with these" (1 Timothy 6:6, 8). Hebrews ties contentment to God's promise: "Keep your lives free from the love of money and be content with what you have, for God has said: 'Never will I leave you, never will I forsake you'" (Hebrews 13:5). *Progression of revelation:* Proverbs grounds contentment in the fear of the LORD (Proverbs 15:16). The New Testament grounds it in the LORD's presence with his people.

**Debt, interest and the surety warnings in the canon.** The Torah forbids interest on loans to the poor and requires a pledged cloak to be returned by nightfall (Exodus 22:25-27; Leviticus 25:35-37; Deuteronomy 23:19-20). Nehemiah confronts nobles whose lending had driven fellow Jews into debt-slavery (Nehemiah 5:1-13). The prophets condemn false scales and the crushing of the poor (Amos 8:4-6; Micah 6:10-11). Proverbs gathers these concerns into short sayings (Proverbs 20:16; 22:7, 26-27; 28:8). Paul's command to leave no debt outstanding except the debt of love (Romans 13:8) carries the same wariness of debt into a command about love. *Tradition:* the medieval church long treated all interest-taking as usury, partly on the basis of texts like Proverbs 28:8. Many Protestant interpreters since the Reformation, Calvin among them, have distinguished exploitative lending to the needy from fair commercial lending. *Inference:* the Hebrew context supports that distinction. The Torah texts behind Proverbs 28:8 concern loans to a poor brother, and the saying itself ends with the poor.

**Tradition on the poor and the rich.** Early Christian preachers took Proverbs' teaching very seriously. *Tradition:* sermons such as Basil the Great's homily on the rich fool of Luke 12 argued that surplus goods withheld from the hungry belong in justice to them, which is close to Proverbs 3:27, where good is not to be withheld from those who are literally its "owners". In the modern period, liberation theologies have stressed the LORD's advocacy for the poor (Proverbs 22:22-23). Prosperity preaching has stressed the promises of plenty (Proverbs 3:9-10). *Inference:* each picks up a real strand, and each goes wrong when it drops the others. Proverbs holds together the LORD's blessing on the diligent and generous, his defense of the oppressed poor, the warning against trusting riches and the call to contentment.

**Cross-reference summary**

| Theme | OT Foundation | NT Development |
|:--|:--|:--|
| The LORD as giver of wealth | Proverbs 3:9-10; 10:22; Deuteronomy 8:17-18; 1 Samuel 2:7 | 1 Timothy 6:17 (God "richly provides all things for us to enjoy"); James 1:17 |
| The poor share a Maker | Proverbs 14:31; 17:5; 22:2; 29:13; Genesis 1:27; Job 31:15 | James 2:1-7; 3:9; Matthew 25:40 |
| The LORD as advocate and redeemer | Proverbs 22:22-23; 23:10-11; 15:25; Jeremiah 50:34; Psalm 68:5 | Luke 1:52-53; 6:20-26; James 5:1-6 |
| Generosity repaid by God | Proverbs 11:24-25; 19:17; 22:9; 28:27; Deuteronomy 15:7-11; Psalm 112:9 | Luke 6:38; 14:12-14; 2 Corinthians 9:6-11 |
| Withholding is wrong | Proverbs 3:27-28; 11:26; 21:13 | James 2:15-16; 1 John 3:17 |
| The good and evil eye | Proverbs 22:9; 23:6; 28:22; Deuteronomy 15:9 | Matthew 6:22-24; 20:15 |
| Honest weights | Proverbs 11:1; 16:11; 20:10, 23; Leviticus 19:35-36; Deuteronomy 25:13-16; Amos 8:5 | Luke 19:8; 1 Thessalonians 4:6 |
| Interest, debt and surety | Proverbs 6:1-5; 22:7, 26-27; 28:8; Exodus 22:25-27; Leviticus 25:35-37; Nehemiah 5; Genesis 43:9 | Romans 13:8; Philemon 18-19; Hebrews 7:22 |
| Riches fail; righteousness delivers | Proverbs 10:2; 11:4, 28; 18:11; 23:4-5; Psalm 49; Zephaniah 1:18 | Luke 12:13-21; 16:19-31; 1 Timothy 6:17-19 |
| Integrity better than wealth | Proverbs 15:16-17; 16:8, 19; 19:1; 28:6; Psalm 37:16 | Mark 8:36; Mark 12:41-44 |
| Contentment and daily bread | Proverbs 30:7-9; Exodus 16:16-18; Deuteronomy 8:11-14 | Matthew 6:11; Philippians 4:11-12; 1 Timothy 6:6-10; Hebrews 13:5 |
| Trust in riches as idolatry | Proverbs 11:28; 18:10-11; Job 31:24-28; Psalm 62:10 | Matthew 6:24; Mark 10:17-27; Colossians 3:5 |
| Christ who became poor | Proverbs 8:18-21 (Wisdom's true riches) | 2 Corinthians 8:9; Colossians 2:3 |

**How the theme progresses revelation.** Proverbs takes the Torah's economic laws (firstfruits, honest weights, interest, boundary stones, care for the poor) and turns them into the habits of a wise life. It grounds them not only in covenant history but in creation and in the LORD's personal government of the world. It states a confident pattern of consequence and, in the same breath, admits its limits: the righteous poor, the prosperous wicked, the injustice that sweeps away a poor man's harvest. That tension stays open in Proverbs. Job, Psalm 73 and Ecclesiastes press it. The New Testament resolves it without denying either side. God's justice is real and will be shown at the final judgment. Riches are not the measure of his favor, since the Son of God became poor. Generosity is lending to the Lord, who will repay when the righteous are raised. The wise person of Proverbs, who honors the LORD with his substance, deals honestly, opens his hand to the poor and prays for daily bread, is in the end a disciple of the One who said, "one's life does not consist in the abundance of his possessions" (Luke 12:15).

---

## Hermeneutic

A note on method. This section traces the topic across the canon. Patterning inside Proverbs (the book frame, the rings in Proverbs 22:22-23:11, the chiasm of Proverbs 30:8-9, the pairing of Proverbs 18:10-11) is set out in the Literary Analysis, and the vocabulary is mapped in the Linguistic Analysis; they are referred to here, not repeated. Quotations of Proverbs are from the Berean Standard Bible (BSB). Other passages are cited by reference, or given in a literal rendering of the Hebrew or Greek marked "literally". The Hebrew and Greek wording of each cross-reference named as a *verbal* link below was checked on the Bible Hub text-analysis pages. The Septuagint (LXX) readings of Proverbs 6:1; 17:18; 22:8a, 26-27 and 30:8-9 were checked against the Greek text published by Ellopos. Labels follow the project's convention: *observation* (what the text says), *inference* (what follows from it), *tradition* (how the church or synagogue has read it), and *application*, which belongs mainly to the Application section.

### 1. Canonical Threads and Themes

**The governing observation.** Proverbs speaks about money in the voice of the gate, the market and the household. It never names the exodus, the sabbatical year of release or the jubilee. Yet nearly every sub-theme of its economic teaching has a counterpart in Torah, and the shared vocabulary is often exact: "scales of deceit" and "stone and stone" (Proverbs 11:1; 20:10, 23; Deuteronomy 25:13-15), *nešek* and *tarbît* (Proverbs 28:8; Leviticus 25:36), the "evil eye" toward the needy (Proverbs 28:22; Deuteronomy 15:9), the "ancient boundary" (Proverbs 22:28; Deuteronomy 19:14). **Inference:** Proverbs reads like covenant economics translated into the idiom of observation and counsel. Torah *commands* the open hand and grounds it in redemption: Israel is to remember that it was a slave in Egypt (Deuteronomy 15:15). Proverbs *commends* the open hand and grounds it in creation: the poor have a "Maker" (Proverbs 14:31; 17:5; 22:2). The two groundings meet in the canon, and the threads below follow them from Genesis to Revelation.

**Thread 1: Wealth as the LORD's gift, and the test of fullness.**

| Stage | Passage | Development |
|:--|:--|:--|
| Ownership | Leviticus 25:23 | The land may not be sold permanently, "for the land is Mine" (literally); Israel holds property as tenant, not owner |
| Gift and warning | Deuteronomy 8:12-18 | "Lest you eat and be satisfied [*wəśābāʿtā*]" (Deuteronomy 8:12, literally) ... remember the LORD, "for He is the one giving you power to make wealth [*ḥāyil*]" (Deuteronomy 8:18, literally) |
| Firstfruits | Exodus 23:19; Deuteronomy 26:1-11 | The first of the harvest is brought to the LORD with a confession of His saving history |
| Sovereign reversal | 1 Samuel 2:7 | "The LORD makes poor [*môrîš*] and makes rich [*maʿăšîr*]" (literally), the same hiphil of *ʿāšar* as Proverbs 10:22 |
| Wisdom first, wealth added | 1 Kings 3:11-13 | Solomon asks for discernment and is given riches and honor besides, the narrative form of Proverbs 3:16; 8:18 |
| The sage's version | Proverbs 3:9-10; 10:22 | "Honor the LORD with your wealth"; "The blessing of the LORD enriches, and He adds no sorrow to it" |
| The test named | Proverbs 30:8-9 | "Give me neither poverty nor riches ... Otherwise, I may have too much and deny You, saying, 'Who is the LORD?'" |
| The test failed | Deuteronomy 17:17; 1 Kings 10:14-11:8 | The king is not to multiply silver and gold; Solomon's gold is counted, and his heart turns (royal side deferred to "The King") |
| The test failed, national scale | Ezekiel 28:4-5 | Tyre's prince: "by the abundance of your wisdom, in your trading, you increased your wealth, and your heart was lifted up in your wealth" (Ezekiel 28:5, literally) |
| Daily bread | Matthew 6:11; Luke 12:13-21 | The disciple prays for the day's bread; the rich man who stores up for himself is called "Fool!" (*aphrōn*, Luke 12:20) |
| Contentment | 1 Timothy 6:6-10, 17-19 | "Godliness with contentment [*autarkeia*] is great gain" (1 Timothy 6:6, literally); the rich are to hope in God and be generous |
| Self-deceived wealth | Revelation 3:17-18 | Laodicea says "I am rich and have grown rich" (literally) and is told it is poor, and is urged to "buy" refined gold from Christ |

**Observation:** the thread holds two claims together from start to finish: wealth is a gift of God, and wealth is a test of whether the gift will be confused with the Giver. Agur's prayer (Proverbs 30:8-9) states the second claim in the very terms of Deuteronomy 8 (see section 2). Ezekiel 28 shows the danger realized in one of the clearest places where the canon links *wisdom* to *trading* and to wealth: wisdom that produces wealth can be undone by the pride that wealth produces. **Inference:** Proverbs' promise that Wisdom brings riches (Proverbs 3:16; 8:18-21) is never naive inside the canon. The same Scripture that offers the promise records the failure of its first patron.

**Thread 2: Trust in riches, or refuge in the LORD.**

| Stage | Passage | Development |
|:--|:--|:--|
| The first test of trust | Proverbs 3:5, 9 | "Trust [*bəṭaḥ*] in the LORD with all your heart" (literally) is followed, four verses later, by "Honor the LORD with your wealth" |
| The false trust | Proverbs 11:28 | The one "trusting [*bôṭēaḥ*] in his riches" will fall, using the same verb as Proverbs 3:5 |
| Two fortresses | Proverbs 10:15; 18:10-11 | "The wealth of the rich man is his fortified city"; repeated in Proverbs 18:11 with "in his imagination," right after the LORD's name as strong tower (see the Literary Analysis) |
| The psalmist's verdict | Psalm 52:7 | The man "who did not make God his refuge, and trusted in the abundance of his riches [*bərōb ʿāšrô*]" (literally); the same verb and noun as Proverbs 11:28 |
| Job's oath | Job 31:24-28 | If he had made gold his confidence, "I would have denied [*kiḥaštî*] God above" (Job 31:28, literally) |
| The ransom no one can pay | Psalm 49:6-9; Proverbs 11:4; 13:8 | Wealth may buy off a threat (Proverbs 13:8) but is "worthless in the day of wrath" (Proverbs 11:4); no one can pay God the ransom (*kōper*) for a life (Psalm 49:7-8) |
| Boasting forbidden | Jeremiah 9:23-24 | Let not the rich boast in his riches, but in knowing the LORD |
| The camel and the needle | Mark 10:17-27 | Riches as the obstacle to entering the kingdom, and God able to do what man cannot |
| The apostolic charge | 1 Timothy 6:17 | The rich are not to set their hope on the uncertainty of riches, but on God |

**Observation:** Proverbs makes the theme visible through the verb *bāṭaḥ* ("trust"). The father calls for trust in the LORD (Proverbs 3:5). The sentence sayings warn against trust in riches (Proverbs 11:28). The Hezekiah collection says that the one "trusting in the LORD" is the one who "will be made fat" (Proverbs 28:25, literally). **Inference:** Job 31:28 and Proverbs 30:9 share the verb *kāḥaš* ("deny"). Both treat misplaced trust in wealth as a theological act, a quiet denial of God, and not as a mere error of financial judgment.

**Thread 3: The open hand.**

| Stage | Passage | Development |
|:--|:--|:--|
| The divine hand | Psalm 145:16 | "Opening [*pôtēaḥ*] Your hand and satisfying the desire of every living thing" (literally) |
| The commanded hand | Deuteronomy 15:7-11 | "You shall surely open [*pātōaḥ tiptaḥ*] your hand to him" and lend "enough for his lack [*maḥsōrô*]" (Deuteronomy 15:8, literally); beware an "evil eye" toward the needy (Deuteronomy 15:9); "the poor will not cease" (Deuteronomy 15:11, literally) |
| Wages not delayed | Leviticus 19:13; Deuteronomy 24:14-15 | The hired man's wage is not to stay with you "until morning"; pay it "on his day," lest he cry to the LORD |
| The sage's hand | Proverbs 3:27-28 | "Do not withhold good from the deserving when it is within your power to act. Do not tell your neighbor, 'Come back tomorrow'" |
| The paradox | Proverbs 11:24-25 | "One gives freely, yet gains even more; another withholds what is right, only to become poor" |
| The good eye | Proverbs 22:9; 28:27 | The "good of eye" is blessed for giving bread to the poor; the giver "will have no lack [*maḥsôr*]" (Proverbs 28:27, literally) |
| The valiant woman | Proverbs 31:20 | "Her palm she spreads out to the poor" (literally) |
| The righteous man's song | Psalm 112:5, 9 | He "scattered [*pizzar*], he gave to the needy" (Psalm 112:9, literally), the verb of Proverbs 11:24 |
| The healthy and the evil eye | Matthew 6:22-23; 20:15 | The eye that is *haplous* ("single, generous") or *ponēros* ("evil"), set inside Jesus' teaching on treasure and Mammon; the landowner asks, "Is your eye evil because I am good?" (Matthew 20:15, literally) |
| Repaid at the resurrection | Luke 14:12-14 | Invite the poor, who cannot repay, "for it will be repaid to you at the resurrection of the righteous" (Luke 14:14, literally) |
| Cheerful giving | 2 Corinthians 9:6-9 | Sowing sparingly and bountifully (compare Proverbs 11:24-25); "God loves a cheerful giver," apparently echoing LXX Proverbs 22:8a, which reads "God *blesses* a cheerful and giving man" (literally); Psalm 112:9 quoted |
| Love in deed | 1 John 3:17; James 2:15-16 | Closing one's heart to a brother in need contradicts the love of God |

**Observation:** the generosity thread runs on two images, the hand and the eye. The *hand* is opened by God (Psalm 145:16), commanded open in Torah (Deuteronomy 15:8, 11) and shown open in the valiant woman (Proverbs 31:20). The *eye* is "good" or "evil" in Deuteronomy 15:9, Proverbs 22:9; 23:6; 28:22, and Matthew 6:22-23; 20:15. **Observation:** Deuteronomy 15:8 and Proverbs 28:27 share the word *maḥsôr* ("lack"). Torah tells the giver to supply the poor man's lack. Proverbs promises that the giver will have none of his own. **Inference:** Proverbs 19:17 ("Kindness to the poor is a loan to the LORD, and He will repay the lender") and Luke 14:14 express the same economy. The LORD stands in for a debtor who cannot pay. Luke places the repayment explicitly at the resurrection. Proverbs leaves the timing open.

**Thread 4: The Maker, Advocate and Redeemer of the poor.**

| Stage | Passage | Development |
|:--|:--|:--|
| Common creation | Genesis 1:26-27; 9:6 | Every human bears God's image; violence against a human is an offense against that image |
| The cry heard | Exodus 22:21-27 | The widow, orphan and poor borrower cry out, and the LORD hears; the pledged cloak is returned by sunset |
| The kinsman-redeemer | Leviticus 25:25; Ruth 4:1-10 | The *gōʾēl* buys back a poor relative's land |
| Job's reasoning | Job 31:13-15 | "Did not He who made me in the womb make him?" (Job 31:15, literally); the servant has the same Maker |
| The sage's grounding | Proverbs 14:31; 17:5; 22:2; 29:13 | "Whoever oppresses the poor taunts their Maker"; "The rich and the poor have this in common: The LORD is Maker of them all" |
| The LORD at the gate | Proverbs 22:22-23; 15:25 | "The LORD will plead their case" (Proverbs 22:23, literally); He maintains the widow's boundary |
| The strong Redeemer | Proverbs 23:10-11; Jeremiah 50:34 | "Their Redeemer is strong" (*gōʾălām ḥāzāq*), the first two words of Jeremiah 50:34, which follows them with the same "plead their case" formula |
| Prophetic indictment | Isaiah 5:8; Amos 2:6-7; Micah 2:1-2; 1 Kings 21 | Field joined to field, the poor sold, Naboth's vineyard taken through a perverted court |
| The King's verdict | Matthew 25:31-46 | What is done to the least of the King's brothers is done to the King |
| The church's warning | James 2:1-7; 5:1-6 | Favoritism toward the rich dishonors the poor whom God chose; withheld wages cry out to the Lord of hosts |

**Observation:** Proverbs grounds justice to the poor in God's work as *Maker* and His office as *Advocate* and *Redeemer*. It does not use the word "image" (*ṣelem*) anywhere. **Inference:** "Maker" does the same work in Proverbs that "image" does in Genesis 9:6 and James 3:9. An act against the person reaches the One who made the person. The thread climbs from Job's "same Maker" (Job 31:15) to Jesus' identification of Himself with the hungry, the stranger and the prisoner (Matthew 25:40). In Proverbs 22:23 and 23:11 the LORD takes the *poor man's* side of the lawsuit. In Matthew 25 He takes the *poor man's* identity.

**Thread 5: Honest weights and ancient boundaries.**

| Stage | Passage | Development |
|:--|:--|:--|
| The law of the scale | Leviticus 19:35-36; Deuteronomy 25:13-16 | Just balances and weights; "stone and stone" forbidden; dishonest dealing is an "abomination" (*tôʿēbâ*) |
| The law of the boundary | Deuteronomy 19:14; 27:17 | The neighbor's landmark set by former generations must not be moved; a curse on the one who moves it |
| The sage's scale | Proverbs 11:1; 16:11; 20:10, 23 | "Dishonest scales are an abomination to the LORD, but an accurate weight is His delight"; the scales and every weight in the bag are "His work" (Proverbs 16:11, literally) |
| The God who weighs | Proverbs 16:2; 21:2; 24:12 | The LORD "weighs" (*tōkēn*) spirits and hearts |
| The sage's boundary | Proverbs 15:25; 22:28; 23:10-11 | The widow's boundary kept; the ancient boundary and the orphan's field guarded by the Redeemer |
| The merchant exposed | Hosea 12:7-8 | "In his hand are scales of deceit [*mōʾzənê mirmâ*]; he loves to oppress" (Hosea 12:7, literally), the exact phrase of Proverbs 11:1; then Ephraim's boast, "How rich I have become!" (Hosea 12:8) |
| Market fraud | Amos 8:4-6; Micah 6:10-11 | Shrunken ephah, enlarged shekel, deceitful weights: the prophets prosecute what the sage forbade |
| The king weighed | Daniel 5:27 | "You have been weighed in the balances [*mōʾzanyāʾ*] and found lacking [*ḥassîr*]" (literally) |

**Observation:** the scale is the most consistent economic image in the canon. It moves from the law of the market (Leviticus 19:35-36) to the God who owns the scales (Proverbs 16:11) and weighs hearts (Proverbs 16:2), to the king weighed and found "lacking" (Daniel 5:27). The Aramaic *ḥassîr* there is cognate with the Hebrew root *ḥsr*, the root of *maḥsôr* ("lack, want") in Proverbs. **Inference:** the one who cheats with the scale in the market will be weighed on God's. Proverbs 11:1 and 16:2, 11 already hold the two scales together inside one book.

**Thread 6: Surety, from forbidden pledge to divine Guarantor.**

| Stage | Passage | Development |
|:--|:--|:--|
| Judah's pledge | Genesis 43:9; 44:32-33 | Judah "stands surety" (*ʿārab*) for Benjamin and then offers himself as a slave in the boy's place |
| The sage's warning | Proverbs 6:1-5; 11:15; 17:18; 20:16; 22:26-27; 27:13 | "Do not be one who gives pledges, who puts up security for debts. If you have nothing with which to pay, why should your bed be taken from under you?" |
| Job's appeal | Job 17:3 | "Lay down my pledge [*ʿārəbēnî*] with Yourself; who is there who will strike hands [*yittāqēaʿ*] with me?" (literally); both surety terms of Proverbs 6:1, now addressed to God |
| The psalmist's appeal | Psalm 119:122 | "Be surety [*ʿărōb*] for Your servant for good; let not the arrogant oppress me" (literally) |
| Hezekiah's appeal | Isaiah 38:14 | "O Lord, I am oppressed; be my surety [*ʿārəbēnî*]" (literally) |
| The apostle's pledge | Philemon 18-19 | Paul writes "with my own hand: I will repay [*apotisō*]" (literally); LXX Proverbs 22:27 uses the same verb for the guarantor who cannot "repay," and Philemon 19 is its only New Testament use |
| The Guarantor | Hebrews 7:22 | Jesus has become the "guarantor" (*engyos*) of a better covenant; LXX Proverbs uses the same word family (*engyaō*, *engyē*) in Proverbs 6:1; 17:18; 22:26 |

**Observation:** this is one of the most striking threads in the canon on this topic. The sage forbids a man to strike hands for another's debt, because a man cannot guarantee what he cannot pay (Proverbs 22:26-27). Job, a psalmist and King Hezekiah then ask *God* to do exactly that (Job 17:3; Psalm 119:122; Isaiah 38:14). Hebrews names Jesus as the One who has done it (Hebrews 7:22). **Inference:** the canon does not overturn Proverbs' counsel. Rash surety is still folly for a man with "nothing with which to pay." But the canon shows *why* it is folly: only one Guarantor can always pay. The surety warnings, read canonically, become a negative image of the gospel. This is the Spirit's wider use of the vocabulary. It is not the sage's intent (see section 5), and it should not be read back into each surety saying.

**Thread 7: Lending, debt and interest.**

| Stage | Passage | Development |
|:--|:--|:--|
| No interest from the poor | Exodus 22:25-27; Leviticus 25:35-37; Deuteronomy 23:19-20 | No *nešek* or *tarbît* from a poor brother; the pledged cloak returned at night |
| Lending as blessing | Deuteronomy 28:12, 44 | Obedient Israel will lend to many nations but not borrow; disobedient Israel will be the borrower |
| The sage's realism | Proverbs 22:7 | "The rich rule over the poor, and the borrower is slave to the lender" |
| The sage's reversal | Proverbs 28:8 | Wealth multiplied by interest is gathered "for one showing favor to the poor" (literally) |
| Debt slavery | 2 Kings 4:1; Nehemiah 5:1-13 | A creditor comes for a widow's sons; Nehemiah forces the nobles to stop charging interest and to restore fields |
| The upright man | Psalm 15:5; Ezekiel 18:8, 13, 17 | He does not lend at interest or take a bribe |
| Lending without return | Luke 6:34-35 | Lend without hope of repayment, and be sons of the Most High |
| The one debt that remains | Romans 13:8 | Owe no one anything except to love one another |

**Inference:** Proverbs 22:7 is not a command. It describes what Deuteronomy 28:44 counts as a curse and Nehemiah 5 shows in action. Proverbs 28:8 places the interest-taker under the Torah prohibition without citing it, and promises a providential transfer of his gains to the generous.

**Thread 8: The retribution principle and its qualifications.**

| Stage | Passage | Development |
|:--|:--|:--|
| Covenant sanctions | Deuteronomy 28:1-14 | Obedience brings rain, harvest and surplus; the pattern behind Proverbs 3:9-10 |
| Tendency stated | Proverbs 3:9-10; 10:22; 22:4; 28:25 | "Then your barns will be filled with plenty, and your vats will overflow with new wine" |
| Exception admitted | Proverbs 13:23 | "Abundant food is in the fallow ground of the poor, but without justice it is swept away" |
| Value ranked | Proverbs 15:16-17; 16:8; 19:1; 28:6 | "Better a little with righteousness than great gain with injustice" |
| The psalmist's ranking | Psalm 37:16 | "Better the little of the righteous than the abundance of many wicked" (literally), the same *ṭôb ... min* form |
| The time-frame stretched | Proverbs 11:4; 23:17-18; 24:19-20 | The "day of wrath" and a "future" (*ʾaḥărît*) that the wicked do not have |
| The scandal faced | Psalm 73; Job 21:7 | "Why do the wicked live on, grow old, and grow mighty in wealth [*ḥāyil*]?" (Job 21:7, literally) |
| The skeptic's report | Ecclesiastes 9:11 | "Not to the wise is bread, and not to the discerning is wealth [*ʿōšer*]" (literally) |
| Faith without the harvest | Habakkuk 3:17-18 | Though fig, vine, olive and fold fail, the prophet rejoices in God |
| The great reversal | Luke 1:52-53; 6:20-26; 16:19-31 | The hungry filled and the rich sent away empty; blessings and woes; Lazarus and the rich man reversed after death |
| The Rich One made poor | 2 Corinthians 8:9 | Christ, being rich, became poor for others' sake, so that through His poverty they might become rich |
| Poverty and true riches | Hebrews 11:26; James 2:5; Revelation 2:9 | The reproach of Christ is greater wealth than Egypt's treasures; the poor are rich in faith; Smyrna is poor in goods yet called rich |

**Observation:** the canon neither denies nor absolutizes the act-consequence pattern. It confirms the pattern as a covenant tendency (Deuteronomy 28; Proverbs 3:9-10). Inside Proverbs it qualifies the pattern (Proverbs 13:23; 16:8). Job, Psalm 73 and Ecclesiastes press it hard (Job 21:7; Ecclesiastes 9:11). The Gospel resolves it eschatologically (Luke 16:19-31) and Christologically (2 Corinthians 8:9). **Inference:** read within this thread, Proverbs 3:9-10 cannot be a guarantee of prosperity for every faithful giver, for Habakkuk 3:17-18 and Hebrews 11:26 honor faith that receives no harvest. Nor do the qualifications cancel the pattern, for the New Testament keeps the promise that God repays (Luke 14:14; 2 Corinthians 9:6-11). The theology of this tension is developed in the Theological Analysis. The general pattern of the two ways belongs to "The Two Paths."

**Thread 9: Wisdom worth more than gold, and the treasure hidden in Christ.**

| Stage | Passage | Development |
|:--|:--|:--|
| The search | Proverbs 2:4 | Seek wisdom "like silver" and "like hidden treasures" (literally) |
| The comparison | Proverbs 3:14-15; 8:10-11, 19; 16:16 | Wisdom's yield outranks silver, gold and corals |
| The price that cannot be paid | Job 28:15-19 | Wisdom "cannot be given for gold, nor silver weighed out as its price" (Job 28:15, literally) |
| The word more precious | Psalm 19:10 | The LORD's judgments are more desirable than gold |
| The kingdom treasure | Matthew 13:44-46 | A man sells all he has for treasure hidden in a field, and a merchant for one pearl |
| Wisdom in person | 1 Corinthians 1:24, 30; Colossians 2:3 | Christ the wisdom of God, "in whom are hidden all the treasures [*thēsauroi*] of wisdom and knowledge" (Colossians 2:3, literally) |

**Inference:** this thread explains why the "better than" sayings do not despise wealth. They rank it. Something is always worth more than gold: wisdom (Proverbs 16:16), a name (Proverbs 22:1), integrity (Proverbs 28:6). In the New Testament that "something" takes a face. Colossians 2:3 is often read as an echo of the "hidden treasures" of Proverbs 2:4. The LXX of Proverbs 2:4 shares the noun *thēsauroi*, and the Hebrew *maṭmōnîm* means "hidden treasures," but the echo is a probable inference, not a quotation.

### 2. Wordplay and Idioms

The Linguistic and Literary Analyses list the sound-plays within individual sayings (for example *dal* in Proverbs 22:22, *qābaʿ* in Proverbs 22:23, *ʿûp* in Proverbs 23:5, "stone and stone" in Proverbs 20:10). This subsection treats the idioms and plays whose meaning depends on the wider canon.

**"Good of eye" and "evil of eye."** **ṭôb-ʿayin** (Hebrew: טוֹב־עַיִן, *ṭôb-ʿayin*, H2896 + H5869) in Proverbs 22:9 and **raʿ ʿāyin** (Hebrew: רַע עָיִן, *raʿ ʿāyin*, H7451 + H5869) in Proverbs 23:6; 28:22 are idioms for generosity and stinginess. They are not the folk belief in a magical "evil eye." **Observation:** Deuteronomy 15:9 uses the same idiom as a verb. Israel is warned lest "your eye be evil [*wərāʿâ ʿênəkā*] against your needy brother" (literally) as the year of release approaches. In Proverbs 28:22 the "evil-eyed" man hurries after wealth (*hôn*) and does not know that want (*ḥeser*) will come on him. **Observation:** the Gospels carry the idiom into Greek. The eye is *haplous* ("single, sound, generous") or *ponēros* ("evil") in Matthew 6:22-23, a saying set between "treasures on earth" (Matthew 6:19-21) and "God and money" (Matthew 6:24). In Matthew 20:15 the grumbling laborers are asked whether their eye is "evil" because the landowner is "good." **Inference:** in that context Matthew 6:22-23 is at least partly about generosity, as in Proverbs. The "evil eye" is a grudging gaze, and the "good eye" a gaze that sees the poor and shares bread. Proverbs 28:27 adds the negative image: the one who "hides his eyes" (*maʿlîm ʿênāyw*) from the poor gathers curses.

**"Striking the palm."** **tāqaʿ kap** (Hebrew: תָּקַע כַּף, *tāqaʿ kap*, H8628 + H3709) is the gesture that seals a guarantee (Proverbs 6:1; 11:15; 17:18; 22:26). **Observation:** outside Proverbs the idiom appears in Job 17:3, where Job asks God, "who is there who will strike hands [*yittāqēaʿ*] with me?" (literally). The gesture that Proverbs tells the son to avoid becomes, in Job, a gesture Job longs for God to make. **Inference:** the idiom carries the whole surety thread of section 1 (Thread 6) in a single physical image. The hand that strikes the bargain puts its owner into another man's hand (Proverbs 6:3, 5). Only a hand that cannot fail is safe to strike.

**"A lender to the LORD."** Proverbs 19:17 begins **malwēh YHWH** (Hebrew: מַלְוֵה יְהוָה, *malwēh YHWH*, "one lending to the LORD"). The participle *malweh* (H3867) is the "lender" who rules the borrower in Proverbs 22:7. **Inference:** the play works across the book and the canon. In Proverbs 22:7 the lender is master. In Proverbs 19:17 the generous man becomes a lender whose debtor is God, and Deuteronomy 28:12 promised that the blessed people would lend to many nations. Luke 14:14 brings the idiom to its end: the poor "cannot repay," so "it will be repaid to you at the resurrection" (literally).

**The *ʿeṣeb* of Proverbs 10:22.** "The blessing of the LORD enriches, and He adds no sorrow to it." The noun **ʿeṣeb** (Hebrew: עֶצֶב, *ʿeṣeb*, H6089) can mean "toil" or "sorrow." The Linguistic Analysis sets out the two readings. **Observation:** the root *ʿṣb* is the root of **ʿiṣṣābôn** (Hebrew: עִצָּבוֹן, *ʿiṣṣābôn*, H6093), the "painful toil" by which the cursed ground yields bread (Genesis 3:17). The same noun as Proverbs 10:22 appears in Psalm 127:2: it is vain to rise early and sit late "eating the bread of toils [*leḥem hāʿăṣābîm*]," for the LORD gives to His beloved sleep (literally). **Inference:** read canonically, Proverbs 10:22 sets the LORD's blessing over against the curse-toil of Genesis 3.
