Either reading works with this echo. Human toil adds nothing to what the LORD gives (Psalm 127:2), or the LORD's gift carries none of the grief that the curse attached to bread. The echo also explains Proverbs 23:4 ("Do not wear yourself out to get rich"). Anxious striving for wealth is the curse-pattern, not the blessing-pattern. The Sluggard's opposite error, refusing work, is treated in "The Sluggard."

**"Lest I be full ... 'Who is the LORD?'"** Agur's prayer is woven from Torah idiom (observation from the Hebrew):

| Proverbs 30:9 | Torah | Effect |
|:--|:--|:--|
| *pen ʾeśbaʿ* ("lest I be full") | *pen-tōʾkal wəśābāʿtā* ("lest you eat and be full," Deuteronomy 8:12) | Agur prays Moses' warning back to God as a petition |
| *mî YHWH* ("Who is the LORD?") | Pharaoh's *mî YHWH* ("Who is the LORD, that I should listen to His voice?", Exodus 5:2, literally) | The full man risks speaking Pharaoh's line |
| *wəgānabtî* ("and steal") | The eighth commandment, against stealing (Exodus 20:15) | Poverty tempts the hand |
| *wətāpaśtî šēm ʾĕlōhāy* ("and seize the name of my God") | The third commandment on the misuse of the Name (Exodus 20:7) | Poverty tempts the tongue too |

**Inference:** in two lines Agur shows that both riches and poverty can lead to breaking the Decalogue. Riches lead to denying the LORD, as Pharaoh did. Poverty leads to theft and to profaning the Name. The LXX, interestingly, renders "Who is the LORD?" as "Who sees me?" (Greek: τίς με ὁρᾷ, *tis me hora*), which puts the stress on practical atheism. Job 31:28 uses Agur's verb *kāḥaš* ("deny") of trust in gold (Thread 2).

**"The bread of my portion."** **leḥem ḥuqqî** (Hebrew: לֶחֶם חֻקִּי, *leḥem ḥuqqî*, H3899 + H2706) in Proverbs 30:8 means "my allotted bread." **Observation:** *ḥōq* is the word for the fixed food allowance the Egyptian priests received from Pharaoh (Genesis 47:22). The idea, though not the word, matches the manna, gathered as "a day's portion in its day" (*dəbar-yôm bəyômô*, Exodus 16:4, literally) as a test of whether Israel would walk in God's law. **Observation:** the LXX renders the line "appoint for me what is needful and sufficient" (Greek: τὰ δέοντα καὶ τὰ αὐτάρκη, *ta deonta kai ta autarkē*). The adjective *autarkēs* belongs to the same family as *autarkeia* ("contentment") in 1 Timothy 6:6. **Inference:** Agur asks for the manna-life: provision from God's hand, measured to the day, which leaves no room either for hoarding or for theft. Matthew 6:11 and 1 Timothy 6:6-8 ("having sustenance and coverings, with these we will be content," 1 Timothy 6:8, literally; the verb *arkeō* belongs to the same family as *autarkēs*) carry the same posture into the New Testament. Some interpreters also connect the LXX's "what is needful" with the rare *epiousios* of the Lord's Prayer. That connection is possible, but it is speculative.

**Hôn as "Enough."** The book's favorite wealth word, **hôn** (Hebrew: הוֹן, *hôn*, H1952), is the very word the four insatiable things never say: "Enough!" (Proverbs 30:15-16). **Observation:** Ecclesiastes 5:10 makes the same point with Agur's verb *śābaʿ*: "The lover of silver will not be satisfied with silver" (literally). **Inference:** the wordplay suggests that the word for "wealth" also carries the idea of "sufficiency," and that greed is the refusal to let wealth be *hôn* in that sense. Agur's prayer for "neither poverty nor riches" (Proverbs 30:8) is a prayer to be able to say *hôn*, "enough."

**Ḥayil: wealth, strength and worth.** **ḥayil** (Hebrew: חַיִל, *ḥayil*, H2428) means "wealth," "strength" and "valor" or "worth." It is the "power to gain wealth" that the LORD gives (Deuteronomy 8:18), the wealth that lifted up Tyre's heart (Ezekiel 28:5), and the wealth in which the wicked grow mighty (Job 21:7). It is also the *ʾēšet-ḥayil*, the "woman of worth," of Proverbs 31:10, and of Ruth 3:11. Boaz is a *gibbôr ḥayil* (Ruth 2:1), "a man of standing" or "a man of substance." **Inference:** the one word joins wealth and character. Proverbs ends by showing *ḥayil* in its true sense: a woman whose worth includes her trading (Proverbs 31:16-18, 24) and whose palm is open to the poor (Proverbs 31:20). The character side of the portrait belongs to "Wisdom, Folly, and the Valiant Woman."

**The rich man's "image" (Proverbs 18:11).** The rich man's wall is high only **bəmaśkîtô** (Hebrew: בְּמַשְׂכִּיתוֹ, *bəmaśkîtô*, H4906), "in his imagination." Elsewhere *maśkît* can name a carved figure, as in the "figured stone" Israel was forbidden to set up (Leviticus 26:1), and the "figures" to be destroyed in Canaan (Numbers 33:52). **Inference (possible, not certain):** a Hebrew ear may hear a double sense. The rich man's security is a picture he has made for himself, and it comes close to an idol. The setting supports the hint, since Proverbs 18:10 has just named the true refuge, "the name of the LORD." The primary sense in Proverbs 18:11 is still "imagination" or "fancy."

**"Their Redeemer is strong."** **gōʾălām ḥāzāq** (Hebrew: גֹּאֲלָם חָזָק, *gōʾălām ḥāzāq*) in Proverbs 23:11 is the book's only use of *gōʾēl* (H1350). It reappears as the first two words of Jeremiah 50:34, where the redeemer is Israel's God against Babylon and the same "He will plead their case" formula follows. **Inference:** the idiom joins a family obligation (the kinsman who buys back land, Leviticus 25:25) to the LORD's own title. The orphan's field in Proverbs 23:10 has the same Defender that exiled Israel has.

### 3. Micro and Macro Structures

**Micro: the hermeneutical unit is the cluster, not the single saying.** The Literary Analysis documents the editorial pairs and rings. The hermeneutical consequence is drawn here. Several wealth sayings are *placed* so that one qualifies another (observation):

| Saying | Its qualifier | How the pair should be read |
|:--|:--|:--|
| Proverbs 10:15, wealth as "fortified city" | Proverbs 18:11, the same line plus "in his imagination," after Proverbs 18:10 | An observation about how wealth works is corrected by what wealth truly is |
| Proverbs 14:20, the poor man shunned | Proverbs 14:21, "the one showing favor to the poor, blessed is he" (literally) | A social fact is judged by a moral verdict |
| Proverbs 13:22, the good man's inheritance | Proverbs 13:23, the poor man's crop "swept away" "without justice" | The retribution principle is set beside its exception |
| Proverbs 22:2, rich and poor meet before one Maker | Proverbs 22:7, the rich rule the poor; Proverbs 22:16, the oppressor comes to want | Creation equality, social inequality, divine judgment |
| Proverbs 3:9-10, barns filled | Proverbs 30:8-9, "neither poverty nor riches" | The promise is framed at the book's end by a prayer that refuses to make wealth the goal |

**Inference:** a saying in Proverbs is a part of a conversation. Reading Proverbs 10:15 or Proverbs 22:7 alone, as a recommendation, misreads the book's own design. The same rule governs the paired sayings of Proverbs 26:4-5, discussed in "The Fools." Interpreters such as Raymond Van Leeuwen have described this as "system and contradiction." The sayings form a system of moral order, and the system contains its own deliberate counter-sayings.

**Micro: the "better than" saying as a ranking of packages.** The *ṭôb ... min* sayings (Proverbs 15:16-17; 16:8, 19; 17:1; 19:1, 22; 22:1; 28:6) never ask the hearer to choose poverty. They ask him to rank two combinations: little plus righteousness against much plus injustice (Proverbs 16:8). **Observation:** the distribution tests the user's working notes on Proverbs (Distinctions.md). Those notes say comparative sayings "become more common in chapters 25–27." That holds for the *simile* ("like ... so ...") in Proverbs 25-26. It does not hold for the "better than" sayings. Of roughly twenty-one in the book, eleven sit in Proverbs 15-21, and all but one of the wealth-related ones (Proverbs 28:6) sit in Proverbs 12-22. **Canonical observation:** the same form appears in Psalm 37:16 and Ecclesiastes 4:6; 7:1 (Ecclesiastes 7:1 echoes Proverbs 22:1 on the good name). The "better than" form is a shared Wisdom tool for relativizing wealth, not a peculiarity of one collection.

**Macro: the topic's shape across the book.** The Literary Analysis sets out the frame from the gang's "one purse" (Proverbs 1:13-14) to the valiant woman's open palm (Proverbs 31:20). For the canonical reading, note three placements (observation):

1. **Economic life enters the book as the first concrete test of trust.** "Trust in the LORD with all your heart" (Proverbs 3:5, literally) is followed by "Honor the LORD with your wealth" (Proverbs 3:9) and "Do not withhold good" (Proverbs 3:27). The order is vertical trust, then worship with wealth, then justice to the neighbor. It is the order of the Decalogue's two tables applied to money.
2. **The densest material sits where the book becomes most observational.** That is Solomon I (Proverbs 10:1-22:16) and Hezekiah's Proverbs 28. These are the collections that most need the qualifying pairs described above.
3. **The topic ends in petition and portrait, not in rule.** Agur prays (Proverbs 30:7-9), and the valiant woman acts (Proverbs 31:10-31). The last word on wealth is a prayer for sufficiency and a picture of wealth in wise and open hands.

**Macro: the canonical conversation.** Across the canon the topic is distributed by genre. Each part of the canon does something different with the same material (inference from the threads in section 1):

```
 TORAH            commands with motives        Deuteronomy 15; 24; 25; Leviticus 19; 25
   |              open the hand to the poor; remember Egypt
   v
 PROPHETS         prosecutes the breach        Hosea 12:7-8; Amos 8:4-6; Micah 6:10-11;
   |              "scales of deceit in his hand"     Isaiah 5:8; 1 Kings 21
   v
 WRITINGS         observes, ranks, tests, prays
   |   Proverbs   the probable pattern + its own qualifiers (13:23; 16:8; 30:8-9)
   |   Psalms     the pattern prayed and sung (37; 49; 73; 112)
   |   Job        the pattern under pressure (21:7; 31:13-28)
   |   Eccles.    the pattern's limits (5:10; 9:11)
   |   Ruth       the pattern enacted (gleaning, gōʾēl, ʾēšet ḥayil)
   |              (bare references in this row group are to the book named)
   v
 GOSPELS          re-founds it on the kingdom  Matthew 6:19-34; Luke 12:13-21; 14:12-14; 16:19-31
   |              and the Rich One made poor   2 Corinthians 8:9
   v
 EPISTLES /       applies it to the church     1 Timothy 6; James 2; 5; Hebrews 7:22; 13:5
 REVELATION       and to the last weighing     Revelation 3:17-18; 18:11-19
```

**Macro: Proverbs' place among its neighbors.** **Observation (tradition):** the canonical order varies. In the order of the Babylonian Talmud (Bava Batra 14b), Proverbs follows Job. In the Tiberian manuscripts behind most printed Hebrew Bibles (the Leningrad Codex), the order is Psalms, Job, Proverbs, Ruth. In the English order, Proverbs stands between Psalms and Ecclesiastes, after Job; in the main Septuagint codices it is followed by Ecclesiastes and the Song, with Job placed later. **Inference:** in every arrangement Proverbs' wealth sayings sit next to books that test them. Job is the righteous man stripped of his wealth. Ecclesiastes observes that riches do not reliably go to the discerning (Ecclesiastes 9:11). In the Tiberian order, the valiant woman (Proverbs 31:10) is followed at once by Ruth. There a poor widow is called *ʾēšet ḥayil* (Ruth 3:11) and a wealthy man lets her glean and redeems her family's land (Ruth 2:15-16; 4:1-10). Ruth puts into narrative what Proverbs 23:10-11 and 31:20 command: wealth used to protect a widow's field.

### 4. Image-Bearer and Wisdom Elements

**The poor as image-bearers.** Proverbs' most theological sayings on poverty are its "Maker" sayings (Proverbs 14:31; 17:5; 22:2; 29:13). "Whoever oppresses the poor taunts their Maker, but whoever is kind to the needy honors Him" (Proverbs 14:31). **Observation:** the poor man is never defined by his poverty in these sayings. He is defined by his Maker. **Inference:** this is image-bearer theology without the word "image." It parallels Genesis 9:6 and James 3:9, where harm or curse against a human reaches God because humans are made in His likeness. The Linguistic Analysis notes that the person-words for the poor (*rāš*, *dal*) are almost never used in sayings that blame the poor. **Inference:** Proverbs teaches the reader to see a poor neighbor first as God's creature, and only afterwards, if at all, as a cautionary tale. Job 31:15 makes the same argument in the first person.

**The rich as image-bearers, and as stewards.** The rich man shares the same Maker (Proverbs 22:2). His wealth is held under ownership, not as ownership. The scales and every weight are "His work" (Proverbs 16:11). The harvest's first portion belongs to Him (Proverbs 3:9). The land is His (Leviticus 25:23). **Inference:** the image-bearer's dominion (Genesis 1:28) includes economic life. To manage wealth is to exercise a delegated rule. Hence the first wealth command in the book is an act of worship ("Honor the LORD with your wealth," Proverbs 3:9), not a rule of thrift. Proverbs 3:27 even calls the needy neighbor the "owner" of the good one is withholding (*mibbəʿālāyw*, "from its owners," literally). What the rich man holds may already belong, in God's sight, to someone else.

**Generosity as imitation of God.** The main generosity verb, **ḥānan** (Hebrew: חָנַן, *ḥānan*, H2603, "be gracious"), is the verb of God's own character (*ḥannûn*, "gracious," Exodus 34:6). **Observation:** the canon shows the same open hand at three levels. God opens His hand to every living thing (Psalm 145:16). Israel is commanded to open its hand to the poor (Deuteronomy 15:8, 11). The valiant woman spreads her palm to the poor (Proverbs 31:20). **Inference:** the image-bearer is most like God when giving. A "theology of the hand" runs through the topic. There is the hand that strikes a rash pledge (Proverbs 6:1), the hand that gathers little by little (Proverbs 13:11), the hand closed in withholding (Proverbs 3:27, literally "in the power of your hand"), and the palm opened to the needy (Proverbs 31:20). The slack hand that leads to poverty belongs to "The Sluggard."

**The fallen image-bearer and wealth.** **Observation:** the canon locates the economic problem at the fall. The ground yields bread through *ʿiṣṣābôn* ("painful toil," Genesis 3:17). That is why Proverbs 10:22 and Psalm 127:2 matter: the LORD's blessing is the counter-sign to the curse (section 2). The temptations Agur names are the two ways the fallen heart misuses wealth. In fullness it forgets God. In want it grasps (Proverbs 30:9). **Inference:** wealth and poverty are not moral stages in Proverbs. They are *conditions* that test and reveal what is in the heart.

**Testing the character-progression chart (Distinctions.md).** The user's working notes on Proverbs end with a chart: the Simple branch, by rejecting or accepting discipline, toward the Sluggard and then the Mocker, or toward the Prudent and then the Wise. Measured against the wealth sayings, the chart is useful but incomplete (observation and inference):

```
                    WEALTH AND POVERTY ARE CONDITIONS, NOT STAGES

   [ The Simple ] -------- first test is economic: the gang's "one purse" (1:13-14)
        |
        +-- rejects discipline --> poverty and disgrace (13:18)
        |        |                 [ The Sluggard ] ends poor (6:11; 24:34)
        |        |                 -- but so do the pleasure-lover (21:17),
        |        |                    the drunkard (23:21), the hasty (21:5; 28:22)
        |        v
        |   [ The Mocker ] -- no distinctive wealth sayings
        |
        +-- accepts discipline --> [ The Prudent ] sees danger and hides (22:3; 27:12)
                 |                 -- placed beside the surety warning (27:13)
                 v
            [ The Wise ] -- wealth as "crown" (14:24), yet "neither poverty nor riches" (30:8)

   CROSS-CUTTING: the RICH man "wise in his eyes" (28:11, literally) looks further along than he is;
                  the POOR man of integrity (19:1; 28:6) or discernment (28:11) may be the wise one;
                  the poor VICTIM of injustice (13:23; 22:22) is at no stage of folly at all.

   (All references in this diagram are to Proverbs.)
```

**Inference:** the chart treats folly and wisdom as a path of character. The wealth material adds a second axis that the chart lacks: *circumstance*. Wealth can disguise a fool as one of the wise (Proverbs 28:11). Poverty can hide a wise man (Proverbs 28:11; compare the poor wise man forgotten in Ecclesiastes 9:15-16). Some poverty has nothing to do with the poor person's character (Proverbs 13:23). A faithful use of the chart would add a note: economic status is never evidence of a person's place on the path. Whether the Sluggard "hardens" into the Mocker, as the chart proposes, is a question for "The Sluggard" and "The Two Paths." The wealth sayings neither confirm nor refute it.

**Wisdom elements.** The topic displays the main habits of biblical Wisdom (inference, summarizing sections 1-3):

| Wisdom habit | Wealth example | Canonical companion |
|:--|:--|:--|
| Observation without endorsement | Proverbs 10:15; 14:20; 19:4; 22:7 | Ecclesiastes 5:13-17 (riches kept to the owner's hurt) |
| Ranking of goods | Proverbs 15:16; 16:8; 22:1; 28:6 | Psalm 37:16; Ecclesiastes 7:1 |
| Tendency, not mechanism | Proverbs 3:9-10 beside 13:23 | Job; Psalm 73 |
| Long time-horizon | Proverbs 11:4; 23:17-18; 24:19-20 | Psalm 73:17; Luke 16:25 |
| Multiple causes of poverty | Folly, pleasure, haste, injustice, circumstance (Proverbs 21:5, 17; 13:23; 22:2); laziness deferred to "The Sluggard" | Ruth 1 (famine and bereavement); John 9:2-3 (a related caution against assigning blame for suffering) |
| Fear of the LORD as frame | Proverbs 15:16; 22:4 | Deuteronomy 8; see "The Fear of the LORD" |
| Prayer as the end of wisdom | Proverbs 30:7-9 | Matthew 6:11 |

### 5. Plenary Authorship Intent

**The human authors' intent.** The wealth sayings come from several hands: Solomon (Proverbs 1:1; 10:1), the anonymous "wise" (Proverbs 22:17; 24:23), the men of Hezekiah who copied Solomon's proverbs (Proverbs 25:1), Agur (Proverbs 30:1) and Lemuel's mother (Proverbs 31:1). The historical setting of each is discussed in the Historical & Cultural Analysis. **Inference:** across these hands the intent is consistent, and it is practical and formative. The authors want to shape a young man, and through him a household and a community, who will:

- honor the LORD with his property and keep trust in Him rather than in riches (Proverbs 3:5, 9; 11:28);
- deal honestly in the market and the court (Proverbs 11:1; 22:22-23);
- give readily and without delay (Proverbs 3:27-28; 11:24-25);
- avoid rash pledges and the slavery of debt (Proverbs 6:1-5; 22:7, 26-27);
- rank integrity above income when the two come apart (Proverbs 16:8; 28:6);
- and pray to be kept from both extremes (Proverbs 30:8-9).

**Observation:** the authors persuade more than they command. They use motive clauses ("for the LORD will plead their case," Proverbs 22:23, literally), rhetorical questions (Proverbs 22:27) and placed counter-sayings (section 3). **Inference:** the editors meant the sayings to be read together. The paired arrangement of Proverbs 10:15 and 18:11, or 13:22 and 13:23, is hard to explain otherwise. The authors did not mean "the righteous will be rich" as a guarantee. They meant it as a true tendency under a just God, and they themselves recorded its exceptions.

**The Spirit's canonical purpose.** The Christian reading confesses one divine Author behind the many human ones. In that reading, the Spirit's wider purpose can be seen in what the canon does with this material (inference, offered with the caution that it extends but must not override the human authors' meaning):

1. **To set the pattern inside the covenant.** Placed after Torah, Proverbs' economic counsel is heard as the wisdom form of Deuteronomy 8 and 15. The LORD who gives "the power to gain wealth" (Deuteronomy 8:18) is the LORD whose blessing "enriches" (Proverbs 10:22).
2. **To keep the pattern from becoming a mechanism.** The Spirit gave Proverbs to the church alongside Job, Psalm 73 and Ecclesiastes. In the canon the retribution sayings cannot be heard as a formula for prosperity, and their qualifiers cannot be heard as cynicism. Both readings are excluded by the books that stand beside Proverbs.
3. **To point the thread forward to Christ.** The canon gathers several of Proverbs' economic images into the person and work of Jesus. He is the Wisdom more precious than gold, in whom the treasures are hidden (Colossians 2:3). He is the Rich One made poor (2 Corinthians 8:9). He is the Guarantor who strikes hands for debts others cannot pay (Hebrews 7:22; compare Job 17:3). He is the King who counts kindness to the least as kindness to Himself (Matthew 25:40), as Proverbs 14:31 and 19:17 count it kindness to their Maker. **Caution:** these are canonical *fulfillments of a pattern*. They are not the hidden meaning of each saying. Proverbs 6:1-5 is still a warning against rash surety, and it is not an allegory of the atonement.
4. **To keep the poor in view until the end.** The thread runs from the LORD as the poor man's Maker (Proverbs 22:2) and Redeemer (Proverbs 23:11) to the resurrection repayment (Luke 14:14) and the great reversal (Luke 16:25). The Spirit's purpose includes the assurance that God's accounts with the poor will be settled.

**Why the topic sits where it does in the book.** **Observation and inference:**

- **The prologue (Proverbs 1-9)** sets the frame. The first temptation is economic (Proverbs 1:13-14). Wealth enters as worship (Proverbs 3:9-10) and justice (Proverbs 3:27-28). Wisdom holds riches as her gift (Proverbs 3:16; 8:18-21). Every later saying about wealth is therefore heard as part of Wisdom's household, not as free-floating market advice.
- **The sentence collections (Proverbs 10:1-22:16; 25-29)** carry the observational load, with the qualifying pairs placed inside them (section 3).
- **The sayings of the wise (Proverbs 22:17-24:22)** recast international instruction material. The Historical & Cultural Analysis discusses the parallels with the Egyptian *Instruction of Amenemope*. The result is that the LORD, as *gōʾēl*, becomes the poor man's Defender (Proverbs 22:23; 23:11).
- **Agur (Proverbs 30)** turns the topic into a prayer near the book's end. **Inference:** the placement matters. After all the counsel, the sage confesses that the right relation to money is finally something to ask God for, not a technique to master.
- **The valiant woman (Proverbs 31:10-31)** closes the book with wealth rightly held: earned honestly, managed wisely and shared with the poor (Proverbs 31:11, 16-18, 20, 24).

**Why the topic sits where it does in the canon.** Proverbs stands in the Writings, among the books that reflect on life lived under the covenant. Its wealth teaching is placed between Torah's laws and the prophets' indictments on one side and the Gospel's reversal on the other. In every traditional order it stands close to Job, Ecclesiastes, or both. In the Tiberian order it is followed by Ruth, which puts its counsel into narrative (section 3). **Inference:** this placement makes Proverbs the canon's *everyday* voice on money. It does not speak in the courtroom of Torah, the lawsuit of the prophets or the cry of the lament. It speaks in the daily choices of the market, the household and the gate. The rest of the canon guards that voice from being heard as either a guarantee or a cynic's shrug. The Application section draws out how a reader today should live inside that voice.

---

## Application

Proverbs talks about money more practically than almost any other book of Scripture. It speaks to the household that brings in a harvest, the merchant with a bag of weight-stones, the neighbor asked to co-sign a loan, the man tempted to hold back grain in a bad year, the poor man pleading at the gate, and the person praying that God will give "neither poverty nor riches." This section carries that teaching into the present. It follows the method used in the rest of this study. *Observation* marks what the text says. *Inference* marks what follows from it. *Tradition* marks how the church has received it. *Application* marks the bridge to modern life. Application is the most flexible of the four, so it is held most loosely.

Two limits apply throughout. Laziness as a cause of poverty belongs to "The Sluggard", and it is mentioned here only as one cause among several. The king's duty to judge the poor (Proverbs 29:14; 31:8-9) belongs to "The King". The theology of friendship behind the sayings about the rich man's many friends belongs to "The Friend and Neighbor". Scripture quotations are from the Berean Standard Bible (BSB). Other passages are cited by reference.

### 1. Clarifying the Original Meaning for a Modern Audience

**The world these sayings came from.** The first hearers of Proverbs lived in an agrarian economy built around the household. A family's life depended on its inherited plot of land, which was marked out by boundary stones (Proverbs 22:28; 23:10). Its security was a full barn and a wine vat (Proverbs 3:10). One failed rainy season could put it into debt. Silver was weighed against stones carried in a bag, so every purchase depended on trusting the seller's weights (Proverbs 11:1; 16:11; 20:10, 23). A man guaranteed another's debt by "striking hands" in public (Proverbs 6:1; 17:18; 22:26). If the debtor defaulted, the guarantor's goods could be seized, "your bed... from under you" (Proverbs 22:27). The town gate served as the local court, and there a poor man's claim could be heard or "crushed" (Proverbs 22:22). The Torah forbade charging interest on loans to a poor fellow Israelite (Exodus 22:25; Leviticus 25:35-37). It required the firstfruits of the harvest to be brought to the LORD (Exodus 23:19; Deuteronomy 26:1-11). Proverbs takes up these laws in the form of short, memorable sayings.

*Observation:* the "son" addressed in Proverbs is usually a person of some means. He has firstfruits to give (Proverbs 3:9). He has the power to help a neighbor "when you already have the means" (Proverbs 3:28). He might be asked to put up security for someone (Proverbs 6:1-5). *Inference:* the book's teaching on wealth is aimed first at people who have something to lose, something to give and some power over others. That describes most readers of this study. By the standards of the ancient world, and of much of today's world, they are the ones with barns. The poor are spoken *about* more than *to*. When the poor are defended, the LORD Himself is their defender (Proverbs 22:23; 23:11).

**How a proverb about money works.** Proverbs is not a contract, and it is not a book of investment tips. Many of its money sayings state how God has ordered the world in general. "One gives freely, yet gains even more; another withholds what is right, only to become poor" (Proverbs 11:24). "Whoever gives to the poor will not be in need, but he who hides his eyes will receive many curses" (Proverbs 28:27). The same collections also set limits on that pattern, and they do so openly. "Better a little with righteousness than great gain with injustice" (Proverbs 16:8) assumes that the righteous sometimes have little and the unjust sometimes have much. "Abundant food is in the fallow ground of the poor, but without justice it is swept away" (Proverbs 13:23). On the reading the BSB adopts, this is poverty that someone else has caused. *Inference:* a modern reader has to keep both kinds of saying in view. The retribution sayings are true generalizations about the way conduct tends under God's rule. They are not guarantees that can be cashed on demand. The "better than" sayings are not a cynical denial of the pattern either. Most of them sit in Proverbs 15-21 (Proverbs 15:16-17; 16:8, 19; 17:1; 19:1, 22), with others at Proverbs 12:9, 22:1 and 28:6. They set out an order of goods: integrity, the fear of the LORD, love and peace rank above money. The Theological Analysis develops this. For application, the point is simple. Anyone who uses Proverbs 3:9-10 to promise wealth, or Proverbs 16:8 to sneer at prudence, has read only half the book.

| Ancient Reality | Modern Equivalent |
|:--|:--|
| Firstfruits brought before the harvest was secure (Proverbs 3:9-10) | Giving to God from the first of one's income, planned at the top of the budget rather than taken from whatever is left over |
| "Come back tomorrow and I will provide" when the means are at hand (Proverbs 3:28) | Late payment of wages, invoices and refunds; putting off help that could be given today |
| Two sets of weight-stones in the merchant's bag (Proverbs 11:1; 20:10, 23) | Hidden fees, padded billing hours, inflated expense claims, misleading specifications, false reporting to tax authorities or investors |
| The buyer crying "Worthless, worthless!" and then gloating (Proverbs 20:14) | Dishonest bargaining, lowballing through invented flaws, boasting about having taken advantage of a seller |
| The grain-hoarder in a lean year (Proverbs 11:26) | Price-gouging and stockpiling in emergencies, profiting from a shortage |
| Striking hands as surety for a neighbor or stranger (Proverbs 6:1-5; 17:18; 22:26-27) | Co-signing a loan or lease, giving a personal guarantee on another's business debt |
| Interest and usury taken from the poor (Proverbs 28:8) | Predatory lending: very-high-interest short-term loans, exploitative rent-to-own and debt-trap products marketed to the desperate |
| The bed taken from under the defaulter; the borrower as servant (Proverbs 22:7, 27) | Repossession, eviction and collections; the loss of freedom that debt brings |
| Moving the ancient boundary; encroaching on the fields of the fatherless (Proverbs 22:28; 23:10-11) | Title fraud, financial abuse of the elderly, cheating widows and heirs out of an estate |
| Crushing the afflicted "at the gate" (Proverbs 22:22) | Using the legal system, paperwork or superior legal help against people who cannot afford to defend themselves; wage theft |
| The poor man's harvest "swept away" without justice (Proverbs 13:23) | Poverty caused or deepened by corruption, unpaid wages, or lack of access to fair courts |
| The rich man's "fortified city... in his imagination" (Proverbs 18:11) | A portfolio, an emergency fund or insurance treated as one's final security |
| Haste to be rich; wealth that "makes wings for itself" (Proverbs 23:4-5; 28:20, 22) | Get-rich-quick schemes, speculative manias, gambling and lotteries, burning out for the next promotion |
| The lover of "wine and oil" who will never be rich (Proverbs 21:17) | Lifestyle inflation; spending driven by pleasure and image |
| "Many are those who love the rich" (Proverbs 14:20; 19:4) | Status-driven networking; attention and honor given to big donors and influencers |
| "Giving gifts to the rich" (Proverbs 22:16) | Buying access, flattering the powerful, generosity aimed upward rather than downward |
| A dish of vegetables with love, a dry morsel in quietness (Proverbs 15:17; 17:1) | A modest home at peace rather than an expensive one full of conflict and strain |
| "The bread that is my portion" (Proverbs 30:8) | A settled idea of "enough" and a ceiling on lifestyle |

**Universal principles.** When these sayings are read together, ten principles stand out. Each rests on more than one text.

1. **God owns and gives; we hold in trust.** "The blessing of the LORD enriches, and He adds no sorrow to it" (Proverbs 10:22). The first portion belongs to Him (Proverbs 3:9-10). Honest scales are His concern (Proverbs 16:11).
2. **Wealth is a real good, but a lesser one.** Wisdom gives riches (Proverbs 3:16; 8:18). Her fruit is still worth more than gold (Proverbs 8:19), and a good name ranks above great riches (Proverbs 22:1). Money must never be bought at the price of righteousness (Proverbs 16:8).
3. **Conduct tends toward consequences under God's rule, but not on a schedule we control.** The pattern holds (Proverbs 11:24-25; 28:27). Its working may wait for "the day of wrath" (Proverbs 11:4) or for "a future" beyond what can now be seen (Proverbs 23:17-18; 24:19-20).
4. **Poverty has many causes.** Proverbs names laziness ("The Sluggard"). It also names love of pleasure (Proverbs 21:17), drunkenness and gluttony (Proverbs 23:20-21), haste and stinginess (Proverbs 21:5; 28:22), refusing correction (Proverbs 13:18), and other people's injustice (Proverbs 13:23; 22:22-23; 30:14). Behind them all stands the LORD, who made rich and poor alike (Proverbs 22:2).
5. **How we treat the poor is how we treat God.** "Whoever oppresses the poor taunts their Maker, but whoever is kind to the needy honors Him" (Proverbs 14:31; compare Proverbs 17:5). "Kindness to the poor is a loan to the LORD, and He will repay the lender" (Proverbs 19:17).
6. **Justice and generosity belong together.** Proverbs forbids taking from the poor (Proverbs 22:16, 22-23; 23:10-11; 28:8). It also commands giving to them (Proverbs 3:27-28; 22:9; 28:27). Neither can stand in for the other.
7. **Honesty in business is a matter of worship.** "Dishonest scales are an abomination to the LORD, but an accurate weight is His delight" (Proverbs 11:1). The word "abomination" comes from the vocabulary of worship. The market stall is under the same eye as the altar.
8. **Debt and pledges are relationships of power.** "The rich rule over the poor, and the borrower is slave to the lender" (Proverbs 22:7). That is a sober description, not an endorsement. It is the reason behind the repeated warnings against rash surety (Proverbs 6:1-5; 11:15; 17:18; 22:26-27).
9. **Our trust belongs to the LORD, not to riches.** "He who trusts in his riches will fall" (Proverbs 11:28). The rich man's wall stands "in his imagination" (Proverbs 18:11), while "the name of the LORD is a strong tower" (Proverbs 18:10).
10. **Wealth and poverty both test faithfulness.** Agur fears that too much would lead him to say "Who is the LORD?" and that too little would tempt him to "steal, profaning the name of my God" (Proverbs 30:9).

**A note on the character chart.** The working notes in Distinctions.md chart a progression from the Simple through the Sluggard or the Prudent to the Mocker or the Wise. The chart has no place for the rich or the poor. The wealth sayings explain why. In Proverbs a poor man can walk in integrity (Proverbs 19:1; 28:6) and see through a rich man (Proverbs 28:11), and a rich man can be "wise in his own eyes" (Proverbs 28:11). *Inference:* in Proverbs, economic standing does not mark a stage of character. It is better seen as ground that every kind of person has to cross, and the crossing shows what that person is. *Application:* a bank balance is not a spiritual report card, whether it is one's own or someone else's. Reading prosperity as proof of God's approval, or poverty as proof of sin, gets wrong the book that is supposedly being applied.

### 2. What Must Change

Proverbs calls for changes of attitude and action, and the two go together. In the book, money shows what the heart is like ("the eye", Proverbs 22:9; 28:22) and what it trusts (Proverbs 11:28; 18:10-11). The table summarizes eleven shifts. The paragraphs after it take them up one at a time, each with questions for self-examination.

| From | To | Key texts |
|:--|:--|:--|
| "I earned this" | "The LORD gave this" | Proverbs 3:9-10; 10:22 |
| Generosity someday | Generosity owed today | Proverbs 3:27-28; 21:26 |
| The averted eye | The good eye and the open ear | Proverbs 21:13; 22:9; 28:27; 29:7 |
| One explanation of poverty | Discernment about causes | Proverbs 13:23; 21:17; 22:2 |
| Deference to the rich, harshness to the poor | Impartial honor | Proverbs 14:20-21; 18:23; 19:4, 7; 22:16 |
| Hidden weights | Honest dealing | Proverbs 11:1; 20:10, 14, 23; 11:26 |
| Profit from the weak | Protection of the weak | Proverbs 22:22-23; 23:10-11; 28:8 |
| Haste and craving | Restraint and patient gain | Proverbs 13:11; 21:17; 23:4-5; 28:20, 22 |
| Casual pledges and debt | Sober obligations | Proverbs 6:1-5; 17:18; 22:7, 26-27 |
| Security in riches | Refuge in the LORD | Proverbs 10:15; 11:28; 18:10-11 |
| Always more | Enough | Proverbs 15:16-17; 17:1; 30:7-9 |

**1. From "I earned this" to "the LORD gave this."** *The modern pattern:* the self-made story. Success is credited to talent, effort and smart decisions, and giving comes out of whatever is left once one's own plans are funded. *The corrective:* "Honor the LORD with your wealth and with the firstfruits of all your crops" (Proverbs 3:9). The firstfruits were offered before the harvest was safely in. The act was a confession that the whole crop was a gift. Proverbs 10:22 credits enrichment to "the blessing of the LORD". *Inference:* this does not deny that diligence matters ("The Sluggard" makes that plain). It places diligence inside a larger gift. Moses warned about exactly this forgetfulness in prosperity (Deuteronomy 8:17-18). The promise of full barns in Proverbs 3:10 is a father's word to a son who is learning to trust. It is not a formula that obliges God.

- When I give, does the gift come first in my planning, or is it whatever is left at the end of the month?
- When I tell the story of what I have, does God appear in it as anything more than a figure of speech?
- Have I ever given in a way that cost me some security, or only when the barns were already full?

**2. From generosity someday to generosity owed today.** *The modern pattern:* sincere intentions to be generous "once things settle down", after the debt is paid, the house is bought or retirement is funded. *The corrective:* "Do not withhold good from the deserving when it is within your power to act. Do not tell your neighbor, 'Come back tomorrow and I will provide'—when you already have the means" (Proverbs 3:27-28). The phrase "the deserving" is literally "its owners". The good is described as already belonging to the person who needs it, and delay counts as withholding. The righteous "give without restraint" (Proverbs 21:26). *Application:* this applies most directly to what is already owed: a worker's wages, a supplier's invoice, a refund, a promised gift, a repair a landlord has been putting off. It also applies to the neighbor whose need is in front of us today, whom we can help.

- Is there someone I owe, a payment, a wage, a reply or a promised gift, whom I am putting off when I could act now?
- Do I tell myself "I'll give more when I have more" while my spending rises to match each raise?
- Whose need have I seen this week that I could have met, and did not?

**3. From the averted eye to the good eye and the open ear.** *The modern pattern:* life arranged so that the poor are never seen or heard. That can mean neighborhoods, schools, churches and social-media feeds sorted by income, or a quick glance away from the person at the intersection. *The corrective:* "A generous man will be blessed, for he shares his bread with the poor" (Proverbs 22:9). In Hebrew the generous man is the one who is **good of eye** (Hebrew: טוֹב־עַיִן, *ṭôb-ʿayin*; H2896 + H5869). The stingy man has an "evil" eye (Proverbs 23:6; 28:22). Proverbs judges the averted eye: "he who hides his eyes will receive many curses" (Proverbs 28:27). It judges the stopped ear: "Whoever shuts his ears to the cry of the poor, he too shall cry out and receive no answer" (Proverbs 21:13). It also asks for understanding: "the righteous consider the cause of the poor" (Proverbs 29:7). *Inference:* in Proverbs generosity begins with attention. One has to see and hear before one's hand moves, and one has to learn a person's actual situation as a judge would learn a case.

- Do I know by name anyone who is poor, and does that person know my name?
- When I see need, is my first reflex to look, to look away, or to look for a reason not to help?
- Have I ever taken the trouble to understand *why* a particular person is in need, rather than assuming I already know?

**4. From one explanation of poverty to discernment about causes.** *The modern pattern:* a single story about why people are poor. One version says the poor are always to blame. Another says they never are. Each version tends to excuse the person who holds it from acting. *The corrective:* Proverbs names many causes. One is laziness ("The Sluggard"). Others are love of pleasure ("He who loves pleasure will become poor," Proverbs 21:17), drink and gluttony (Proverbs 23:20-21), haste and stinginess (Proverbs 28:22), refusing correction (Proverbs 13:18), and plain injustice (Proverbs 13:23; 22:22-23; 30:14). Above them all stands the reminder that "The rich and the poor have this in common: The LORD is Maker of them all" (Proverbs 22:2). *Inference:* telling causes apart is not a way to decide whether someone deserves help. The poor share a Maker with the rich no matter what put them where they are (Proverbs 14:31). Telling causes apart is a way to decide what kind of help will truly help. A person defrauded of wages needs an advocate. A person trapped in addiction needs more than cash. A family hit by a sudden disaster needs bread now (Proverbs 22:9).

- What is my default explanation of poverty, and which Proverbs texts does it leave out?
- Does my explanation tend to excuse me from doing anything?
- When I have been poor or in need myself, how would I have wanted others to explain it?

**5. From deference to the rich and harshness to the poor to impartial honor.** *The modern pattern:* the social reflex that Proverbs reports without approving. "The poor man is hated even by his neighbor, but many are those who love the rich" (Proverbs 14:20). "Wealth attracts many friends, but a poor man is deserted by his friend" (Proverbs 19:4). "The poor man pleads for mercy, but the rich man answers harshly" (Proverbs 18:23). It appears in churches that honor large donors, in workplaces where the powerful get patience and the junior get curtness, and in giving aimed upward to win favor. *The corrective:* Proverbs follows the first observation at once with a verdict. "He who despises his neighbor sins, but blessed is he who shows kindness to the poor" (Proverbs 14:21). It also warns that "giving gifts to the rich will surely lead to poverty" (Proverbs 22:16). The syntax of that verse is debated, but its warning against currying favor upward is clear. "A rich man is wise in his own eyes, but a poor man with discernment sees through him" (Proverbs 28:11). *Application:* James 2:1-7 applies the same point to the seating plan of a church gathering.

- Whose calls do I return first, and whose do I let wait?
- Am I warmer, more patient and more generous toward people who can do something for me?
- In my church or organization, does a person's giving or status change how they are listened to?

**6. From hidden weights to honest dealing.** *The modern pattern:* small dishonesties that are accepted as "how business works", such as padded hours, hidden fees, creative expense reports, a "bargain" won by running down the goods, or prices raised in a crisis because desperate people will pay. *The corrective:* "Dishonest scales are an abomination to the LORD, but an accurate weight is His delight" (Proverbs 11:1). "Differing weights and unequal measures—both are detestable to the LORD" (Proverbs 20:10). Proverbs even notices the buyer's trick: "'Worthless, worthless!' says the buyer, but on the way out, he gloats" (Proverbs 20:14). It condemns profiteering from scarcity: "The people will curse the hoarder of grain, but blessing will crown the one who sells it" (Proverbs 11:26). *Inference:* the "two weights" in the bag were one set for buying and one for selling. The modern equivalent is any double standard: one rule for what I am owed and another for what I owe.

- Are there numbers I report, to an employer, a customer, a tax authority or an insurer, that I would be ashamed for the LORD to check?
- Do I use one standard when I buy and another when I sell?
- Have I ever profited from someone's emergency more than was fair?

**7. From profit from the weak to protection of the weak.** *The modern pattern:* income that depends, sometimes at a distance, on the desperation of others. Examples are predatory loans, unpaid or underpaid labor, exploitative rental practices, schemes aimed at the elderly or the grieving, and investments whose returns come from such practices. *The corrective:* "Do not rob a poor man because he is poor, and do not crush the afflicted at the gate, for the LORD will take up their case and will plunder those who rob them" (Proverbs 22:22-23). "Do not move an ancient boundary stone or encroach on the fields of the fatherless, for their Redeemer is strong; He will take up their case against you" (Proverbs 23:10-11). "He who increases his wealth by interest and usury lays it up for one who is kind to the poor" (Proverbs 28:8). *Inference:* the LORD does not stand neutral between the strong and the weak in court. The phrase "because he is poor" in Proverbs 22:22 names the particular wickedness of picking a victim *because* he cannot fight back. *Application:* the question is not only "Did I break a law?" It is also "Does my gain depend on someone's inability to defend himself?"

- Is any part of my income, rent, interest, fees or investment return drawn from people who have no real alternative?
- Have I ever used my better access to lawyers, information or time against someone who had none?
- Is there an elderly relative, widow or heir whose affairs I could protect, or whose affairs I am tempted to exploit?

**8. From haste and craving to restraint and patient gain.** *The modern pattern:* the get-rich-quick dream, which takes many forms: lotteries and sports betting, speculative manias, schemes promising quick returns, or simply wearing oneself out for the next level of income. It often goes together with lifestyle inflation, where wants rise to absorb every increase. *The corrective:* "Do not wear yourself out to get rich; be wise enough to restrain yourself. When you glance at wealth, it disappears, for it makes wings for itself and flies like an eagle to the sky" (Proverbs 23:4-5). "Dishonest wealth will dwindle, but what is earned through hard work will be multiplied" (Proverbs 13:11). "A faithful man will abound with blessings, but one eager to be rich will not go unpunished" (Proverbs 28:20). "A stingy man hastens after wealth and does not know that poverty awaits him" (Proverbs 28:22). "An inheritance gained quickly will not be blessed in the end" (Proverbs 20:21). "He who loves pleasure will become poor; the one who loves wine and oil will never be rich" (Proverbs 21:17). *Observation:* Proverbs 23:4 does not say "do not work". It says do not *exhaust yourself* for riches, and know when to stop. *Inference:* the target is the craving, not the earning. The craving pushes a person into folly, and it often harms others along the way.

- If my income doubled, what would I do with it, and is that answer different from what I am doing now?
- Am I spending my health, my family's evenings or my Sabbath rest to get richer?
- Have I put money into something mainly because it promised to make me rich quickly?

**9. From casual pledges and debt to sober obligations.** *The modern pattern:* co-signing a loan to avoid an awkward conversation, offering a personal guarantee without counting the cost, or taking on consumer debt as a normal part of life. *The corrective:* "My son, if you have put up security for your neighbor, if you have struck hands in pledge with a stranger, if you have been trapped by the words of your lips... Go, humble yourself, and press your plea with your neighbor... Free yourself, like a gazelle from the hand of the hunter" (Proverbs 6:1-3, 5). "Do not be one who gives pledges, who puts up security for debts. If you have nothing with which to pay, why should your bed be taken from under you?" (Proverbs 22:26-27). The verb for standing surety is **ʿārab** (Hebrew: עָרַב, *ʿārab*, H6148). *Inference:* this is not a ban on generosity. The same book calls kindness to the poor a loan to the LORD (Proverbs 19:17), and Judah's pledge for Benjamin uses the same verb in a self-giving way (Genesis 43:9). What Proverbs forbids is the rash pledge: one made in public under social pressure (Proverbs 17:18), one made for someone whose reliability is unknown, or one larger than the guarantor can pay. Proverbs 22:7 explains why: debt makes the borrower the lender's servant. *Application:* when asked to co-sign, the wise question is "Can I afford to pay this entire debt myself, and would I give this amount as a gift?" If the answer is no, a smaller outright gift is often both kinder and wiser than a guarantee.

- Have I promised to cover anything I could not actually pay?
- Is any debt I carry making me the servant of a lender in ways that limit my obedience or my generosity?
- When I say no to a request for a guarantee, can I still offer help that I can actually give?

**10. From security in riches to refuge in the LORD.** *The modern pattern:* treating savings, insurance, home equity or a pension as one's final safety. Prudence is good. Proverbs commends storing up (Proverbs 21:20) and knowing the state of one's flocks (Proverbs 27:23-24). The problem comes when the fund becomes the fortress. *The corrective:* Proverbs repeats one line and changes its ending. "The wealth of the rich man is his fortified city, but poverty is the ruin of the poor" (Proverbs 10:15). "A rich man's wealth is his fortified city; it is like a high wall in his imagination" (Proverbs 18:11). The verse before that second saying names the real fortress: "The name of the LORD is a strong tower; the righteous run to it and are safe" (Proverbs 18:10). "He who trusts in his riches will fall, but the righteous will thrive like foliage" (Proverbs 11:28). The verb is **bāṭaḥ** (Hebrew: בָּטַח, *bāṭaḥ*, H982), the same verb as in "Trust in the LORD with all your heart" (Proverbs 3:5). *Inference:* Proverbs 10:15 is true as far as it goes. Money does protect against many things. Proverbs 18:11 marks where that protection runs out.

- Where does my mind go first when I feel threatened: to my balance or to the LORD?
- How would I describe my peace if my savings disappeared tomorrow?
- Do I pray about my finances, or only plan them?

**11. From always more to enough.** *The modern pattern:* no settled idea of "enough". Every raise resets the target. *The corrective:* Agur's prayer: "Give me neither poverty nor riches; feed me with the bread that is my portion. Otherwise, I may have too much and deny You, saying, 'Who is the LORD?' Or I may become poor and steal, profaning the name of my God" (Proverbs 30:8-9). "My portion" is **ḥōq** (Hebrew: חֹק, *ḥōq*, H2706), an allotted share. The "better than" sayings train the same contentment by placing peace and love above plenty. "Better a little with the fear of the LORD than great treasure with turmoil. Better a dish of vegetables where there is love than a fattened ox with hatred" (Proverbs 15:16-17). "Better a dry morsel in quietness than a house full of feasting with strife" (Proverbs 17:1). *Observation:* Agur fears both too much and too little, and for spiritual reasons. *Application:* contentment in Proverbs is not passivity, and it does not glorify poverty. It is a decision about what is truly good, made in prayer, and it puts a ceiling on wanting.

- Have I ever named, before God, what "enough" would be for my household?
- Is my home closer to "a fattened ox with hatred" or to "a dish of vegetables where there is love"?
- In which direction am I more tempted: the pride of having too much, or the desperation of having too little?

**For readers who are poor or in debt.** Proverbs speaks mainly to those with means, but it has words for the poor as well. Their dignity does not depend on their income, because "the LORD is Maker of them all" (Proverbs 22:2). Integrity is worth more than wealth: "Better a poor man who walks with integrity than a rich man whose ways are perverse" (Proverbs 28:6; compare Proverbs 19:1). The LORD takes up their case (Proverbs 22:23). Proverbs also warns the poor honestly. Agur names the temptation to steal when in want (Proverbs 30:9), and Proverbs 6:30-31 insists that even a hungry thief must make restitution. Schemes that promise quick riches prey on the poor most of all (Proverbs 13:11; 28:22). A pledge one cannot pay can cost even one's bed (Proverbs 22:27). *Application:* where poverty comes from one's own choices, Proverbs calls for repentance and patient rebuilding. Where it comes from others' injustice, Proverbs offers the LORD as advocate and calls the community to act for the poor.

### 3. When, Where, and How to Implement

Proverbs expects its sayings to be applied with judgment to particular situations (compare the paired sayings of Proverbs 26:4-5). The table below sets the main contexts side by side. The suggestions in the "How" column are *application*: they are concrete ways of obeying the texts, not commands the texts themselves contain.

| Context | When | Where | How |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| Household budget | At each payday and in an annual review | Kitchen table, budgeting app | Set the giving line first (Proverbs 3:9); name an "enough" level for lifestyle (Proverbs 30:8); limit growth in pleasure spending (Proverbs 21:17) |
| Paying what is owed | The day a bill, wage or refund is due, or sooner | Accounts payable, payroll, personal bills | Pay on time or early when able; never use "come back tomorrow" as a cash-flow tactic (Proverbs 3:27-28) |
| Running a business | Pricing, invoicing, contract drafting, crisis periods | Shop floor, sales desk, online store | One standard for buying and selling; disclose fees; refuse to raise prices on emergency necessities beyond fair cost (Proverbs 11:1, 26; 20:10) |
| Working for someone else | Timesheets, expense claims, sales pitches | Office, job site, remote work | Report hours and expenses exactly; describe products truthfully (Proverbs 11:1; 20:23) |
| Buying and bargaining | Negotiations, large purchases, secondhand sales | Car lots, marketplaces, online listings | Bargain hard but truthfully; do not invent flaws or boast about cheating a seller (Proverbs 20:14) |
| Employing others | Hiring, wage-setting, scheduling | Small businesses, households with domestic help, farms | Pay fair wages promptly; never use a worker's lack of options as leverage (Proverbs 22:22; 3:27) |
| Investing and lending | When choosing funds, loans or rental terms | Brokerage accounts, rental property, family loans | Ask where returns come from; avoid profiting from predatory lending; lend to the needy on generous terms or give outright (Proverbs 28:8; 19:17) |
| Requests to co-sign or guarantee | When a friend, relative or colleague asks | Bank offices, family conversations | Guarantee only what you could pay in full as a gift; consider giving a smaller sum outright instead; get out quickly if already trapped (Proverbs 6:1-5; 22:26-27) |
| Personal debt | Before any major purchase; in a debt-reduction plan | Credit cards, car loans, consumer finance | Treat debt as a form of servitude; reduce it so as to be free to give and obey (Proverbs 22:7) |
| Get-rich-quick offers | When a scheme, tip or app promises fast money | Gambling apps, social-media pitches, speculative markets | Decline; prefer steady gain, "what is earned through hard work" (Proverbs 13:11; 23:4-5; 28:20, 22) |
| Encounters with the poor | Daily, as need appears | Streets, workplaces, schools, the church foyer | Look and listen before deciding; give bread when bread is the need (Proverbs 22:9; 21:13; 28:27) |
| Church life | Seating, leadership selection, benevolence decisions | Congregational gatherings, deacon boards | Refuse favoritism to donors; keep a ready benevolence fund; teach Proverbs without prosperity promises (Proverbs 14:20-21; 22:2; James 2:1-7) |
| Family and inheritance | When elders age, estates are settled, wills are written | Family meetings, probate, elder care | Protect widows, orphans and the elderly from financial abuse; do not hurry an inheritance (Proverbs 15:25; 20:21; 23:10-11; 28:24) |
| Community and civic life | When the afflicted are being crushed "at the gate" | Courts, housing boards, workplaces, local government | Support legal aid and fair access to justice; speak for those without a voice, through means each conscience judges best (Proverbs 22:22-23; 29:7) |
| Seasons of plenty | After a raise, bonus, windfall or good year | Financial planning, prayer | Raise giving before raising lifestyle; ask whether fullness is making you forget the Giver (Proverbs 3:9-10; 30:9) |
| Seasons of want | After job loss, illness, a failed business | Home, church, the lender's office | Keep integrity (Proverbs 19:1; 28:6); refuse the shortcut of theft or fraud (Proverbs 30:9); avoid desperate pledges; ask for help without shame (Proverbs 22:2) |
| Prayer | Daily, and at every money decision | Personal devotion, household prayer | Pray Agur's prayer; ask for "the bread that is my portion" (Proverbs 30:7-9; compare Matthew 6:11) |

**Practices that carry the sayings into ordinary life.** The following are *application*. They are ways Christians have found to obey these sayings, offered as examples, not as rules.

- **The first transfer.** Arrange giving so that it happens first, as the firstfruits did (Proverbs 3:9), for example by an automatic transfer set up for payday. Proverbs does not set a percentage. Christians differ on whether the tithe still binds, but they agree that the first portion belongs to God.
- **The "today" test.** Once a week, ask: "Is there good I am withholding that I could do now?" (Proverbs 3:27-28). Pay that bill, send that wage, keep that promised gift.
- **An honest-scales audit.** Once a year, review the places where your numbers meet other people's trust: tax returns, invoices, expense claims, product descriptions, timesheets (Proverbs 11:1; 16:11). Where something was wrong, make restitution. Proverbs 6:31 assumes repayment, and Zacchaeus in Luke 19:8 shows a repentant man promising fourfold repayment.
- **A co-signing rule.** Settle the rule before anyone asks: "I will guarantee only what I could afford to give away" (Proverbs 22:26-27). Deciding in advance makes it possible to say no kindly and to offer a gift instead.
- **Naming "enough".** Write down, before God, what a sufficient household income and lifestyle would be, and decide in advance what to do with anything above it (Proverbs 30:8; 15:16).
- **Relationships across income lines.** Look for ongoing contact with people poorer than yourself, through church, mentoring, shared meals or local service, so that "the cry of the poor" can actually reach you (Proverbs 21:13; 22:2).
- **Asking where returns come from.** Before investing or lending, ask whose loss produces your gain (Proverbs 28:8; 22:16, 22).
- **Praying about money.** Bring decisions about money to God before making them. Agur's prayer (Proverbs 30:7-9) is the only prayer in Proverbs, and it is about money.

**Implementation in the church.** A congregation can apply these texts together as well as individually. *Application:* it can preach Proverbs 3:9-10 alongside Proverbs 16:8 and 28:6, so that the promise is not heard as a guarantee. It can keep a benevolence fund that acts quickly, because "Come back tomorrow" (Proverbs 3:28) is as wrong for a church as for an individual. It can guard against honoring donors above others (Proverbs 14:20-21; James 2:1-4). It can offer practical teaching on debt, surety and budgeting. It can welcome the poor as fellow creatures of the one Maker (Proverbs 22:2), not only as the objects of programs.

### 4. Meaning Versus Method

Some of what Proverbs says about money is fixed: it rests on who God is and what human beings are. Some of it comes in forms belonging to an ancient agrarian economy. Separating the two keeps us from two mistakes. One is dismissing the teaching as outdated. The other is copying its ancient forms in ways that miss its purpose.

| Timeless Principle | Ancient Form in Proverbs | Flexible Modern Methods |
|:--|:--|:--|
| God owns everything; He comes first | Firstfruits of the harvest (Proverbs 3:9-10) | First-portion giving from wages, profits or pensions; the proportion is decided by conscience before God |
| Good owed should not be delayed | "Come back tomorrow" to a neighbor at the door (Proverbs 3:28) | Prompt payment, fair payment terms, quick benevolence |
| God hates deceit in trade | Two weight-stones and two measures (Proverbs 11:1; 20:10) | Honest pricing, accurate data, transparent contracts, truthful advertising |
| The weak must not be exploited | Interest from the poor; moved boundary stones; crushing at the gate (Proverbs 22:22; 23:10; 28:8) | Refusing predatory lending, title fraud, wage theft, elder financial abuse; supporting access to justice |
| Do not promise what you cannot pay | Striking hands as surety (Proverbs 6:1-5; 22:26-27) | Caution about co-signing and personal guarantees; giving outright instead |
| Debt creates servitude | The borrower as servant; the bed taken (Proverbs 22:7, 27) | Restraint in borrowing; plans for getting out of debt |
| Wealth is a lesser good | Vegetables with love, a dry morsel in quiet (Proverbs 15:17; 17:1) | Choosing peace, time and relationships over income when they conflict |
| Trust belongs to God alone | The rich man's imagined wall (Proverbs 18:10-11) | Prudent saving held loosely; prayer as part of financial planning |
| Each household should seek "enough" | "The bread that is my portion" (Proverbs 30:8) | A personal, prayerful ceiling on lifestyle; giving whatever comes in above it |
| The poor share our Maker | "The LORD is Maker of them all" (Proverbs 22:2) | Dignity-preserving help; friendship across income lines |

**The retribution sayings: meaning versus method.** This is the most important distinction in the topic. *Meaning:* God governs the world morally. Generosity, honesty and the fear of the LORD tend toward life and plenty, and greed, fraud and oppression tend toward ruin (Proverbs 10:22; 11:24-25; 28:27). *Method:* the proverb states a general pattern. It does not set out a contract with terms and a date. Proverbs itself supplies the qualifications: righteous poverty and wicked wealth exist (Proverbs 16:8; 28:6), injustice can sweep away a poor man's harvest (Proverbs 13:23), and some outcomes wait for "the day of wrath" (Proverbs 11:4) or "a future" (Proverbs 23:18). *Inference:* the New Testament keeps the meaning and moves the final settlement to the day of judgment. Jesus tells of a rich fool who stored up treasure but was not "rich toward God" (Luke 12:13-21), and of a rich man and a poor man whose positions are reversed after death (Luke 16:19-31). Paul tells the rich to be generous and so to store up "a firm foundation for the future" (1 Timothy 6:17-19), and he applies the sowing-and-reaping logic of Proverbs 11:24-25 to giving (2 Corinthians 9:6-11). *Application:* two errors need to be avoided.

- **The prosperity error** turns Proverbs 3:9-10 or 19:17 into a technique: give in order to get. It makes the poor man of integrity in Proverbs 28:6 impossible. It turns the fear of the LORD into a tool for gain, which comes close to the danger Agur's prayer names (Proverbs 30:9). And it leaves the poor believer feeling condemned.
- **The cynical error** treats the "better than" sayings as cancelling the rest, as though Proverbs had no confidence that God rewards anything. This drains the motive from generosity and the warning from greed, and it ignores the book's repeated statements that the LORD repays and defends (Proverbs 19:17; 22:23).

What lasts is trust in a God who sees, repays and judges. How and when He does so is His to decide.

**Surety: a principle, not an absolute ban.** *Meaning:* do not put your household at risk by promising what you cannot pay, especially under social pressure or for someone whose reliability you do not know (Proverbs 6:1-5; 11:15; 17:18; 22:26-27). *Method:* the ancient handshake and the seizure of the bed. *Inference:* the principle does not forbid every guarantee. The same verb **ʿārab** (Hebrew: עָרַב, *ʿārab*, H6148) describes Judah's self-sacrificing pledge for Benjamin (Genesis 43:9), and Paul offers to cover Onesimus' debt (Philemon 18-19). A guarantee made with open eyes, within one's means and as an act of love is a different act from the rash pledge Proverbs condemns. *Tradition, held with care:* Christian readers have long noticed that Hebrews 7:22 calls Jesus the **guarantee** (Greek: ἔγγυος, *engyos*, G1450) of a better covenant. Proverbs warns against pledging what one cannot pay. The gospel speaks of a Guarantor who could pay and did. That is a canonical reflection. It is not the meaning of Proverbs 6:1-5, and it does not overturn the practical warning.

**Interest: exploitation, not every loan.** *Meaning:* do not profit from a neighbor's distress (Proverbs 28:8). *Method:* the Torah's ban on **nešek** (Hebrew: נֶשֶׁךְ, *nešek*, H5392) and **tarbît** (Hebrew: תַּרְבִּית, *tarbît*, H8636) in loans to a poor fellow Israelite (Leviticus 25:35-37; Deuteronomy 23:19-20). *Tradition:* the medieval church long treated all interest as usury. Many Protestant interpreters since the Reformation have distinguished fair commercial lending from exploitative lending to the needy. *Inference:* the context of Proverbs 28:8, which ends with "one who is kind to the poor", supports that distinction. *Application:* a believer may lend and invest. A believer may not build wealth on the desperation of those with no other option, and should be ready to lend to the needy on generous terms or simply to give (Proverbs 19:17).

**Firstfruits: priority, not a percentage.** *Meaning:* the LORD is honored first and from the whole (Proverbs 3:9). *Method:* agricultural firstfruits brought to the sanctuary. *Tradition:* Christians differ on whether the tithe (Malachi 3:10) binds the church. Some treat it as a floor. Others take the New Testament principle of proportional, cheerful giving (2 Corinthians 8-9) as replacing a fixed rate. *Inference:* Proverbs itself names no percentage. It fixes the priority, the posture of trust and the connection between worship and wealth. The amount is a matter of conscience before God.

**Justice for the poor: an obligation, not a policy platform.** *Meaning:* the LORD defends the poor, and His people must not exploit them and must "consider the cause of the poor" (Proverbs 22:22-23; 29:7). *Method:* the village gate and the elders' court. *Inference:* Proverbs speaks to every householder, not only to rulers (the royal duty is developed in "The King"). It does not supply a modern economic program. Christians of good faith disagree about which public policies best serve the poor. *Application:* what is fixed is the obligation and its motive. Every believer must refuse to profit from injustice, must pay attention to the poor, and must use whatever influence they have, in business, church, neighborhood or public life, on the side of those who cannot defend themselves. How that influence is best used is left to wisdom and conscience.

**Contentment: a prayer, not a vow of poverty.** *Meaning:* both riches and poverty test faithfulness, so pray for "enough" (Proverbs 30:8-9). *Method:* Agur's request for his allotted bread. *Inference:* Proverbs does not command poverty, which it counts as a danger (Proverbs 10:15; 30:9). It does not condemn wealth either, which it counts as a gift (Proverbs 10:22). What it requires is a heart that can hold either without forgetting God. *Application:* "enough" will be different for different households, callings and seasons. What does not change is the practice of naming it before God and holding to it.

**The motive beneath the method.** Proverbs grounds its teaching on wealth in the LORD's character: He is Maker of rich and poor (Proverbs 22:2), He delights in honest weights (Proverbs 11:1), He repays kindness to the poor (Proverbs 19:17), and He is the strong tower (Proverbs 18:10). The New Testament adds a motive Proverbs could only anticipate. Paul grounds his appeal for generosity in the grace of the Lord Jesus Christ, who "though He was rich" became poor for others (2 Corinthians 8:9). Jesus counts what is done for the least of His brothers as done for Him (Matthew 25:40), which carries forward the logic of Proverbs 19:17. *Application:* the believer who gives first, pays promptly, trades honestly, refuses to exploit, pledges soberly, trusts the LORD rather than riches and prays for daily bread is not following a technique for success. He is living out, in money matters, the fear of the LORD with which the book begins, and responding to the generosity of the Christ in whom "all the treasures of wisdom and knowledge" are found (Colossians 2:3).
